===== PAGE 1 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD
SPHERE DYNAMICS
YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
Abstract. We provide a rigorous derivation of Boltzmann’s kinetic equation from the hard sphere system
for rarefied gas, which is valid for arbitrarily long times, as long as the solution to the Boltzmann equation
exists. This extends Lanford’s landmark theorem [40], which justifies this derivation for a sufficiently short
time. In a companion paper [24], we connect this derivation to existing literature on hydrodynamic limits.
This completes the resolution of Hilbert’s Sixth Problem pertaining to the derivation of fluid equations from
Newton’s laws, in the case of a rarefied, hard sphere gas.
The general strategy follows the paradigm introduced by the first two authors for the long-time derivation
of the wave kinetic equation in wave turbulence theory. This is based on propagating a long-time cumulant
ansatz, which keeps memory of the full collision history of the relevant particles, by an important partial
time expansion. The heart of the matter is proving the smallness of these cumulants in L1, which can be
reduced to combinatorial properties for the associated diagrams which we call molecules. These properties
are then proved by devising an elaborate cutting algorithm, which is a major novelty of this work.

===== PAGE 3 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 3
1. Introduction
Dating back to the year 1872, Boltzmann’s kinetic theory is one of the most profound and revolutionary
scientific ideas ever introduced. At the heart of this theory lies Boltzmann’s kinetic equation which gives
an effective macroscopic description of the statistics of a large system of microscopic interacting particles.
A significant feature of this theory, which caused some controversy at the time of its inception, is the fact
that the macroscopic description is given by a time-irreversible equation (i.e. with an entropy functional
that increases forward in time), which is derived from a microscopic system satisfying the time-reversible
Newton’s laws of motion. This is known as the emergence of the arrow of time .
Unsurprisingly, this profound physical theory prompted the deep mathematical question, namely to rig-
orously justify the derivation of Boltzmann’s equation starting from Newton’s laws. This question was asked
by Hilbert, in his address at the International Congress of Mathematicians in 1900, as the sixth problem 1
in his celebrated list of outstanding mathematical problems for the twentieth century. More precisely, he
proposed the question of giving a mathematically rigorous derivation for the macroscopic equations of fluids,
such as the Euler and Navier-Stokes equations, from the Newtonian laws governing microscopic particle
interactions, with the derivation of Boltzmann’s kinetic equation as a crucial intermediate step. This prob-
lem requires justifying two limits: (i) the kinetic limit where we pass from the (microscopic) Newtonian
dynamics for an N-particle system to Boltzmann’s kinetic equation for the one-particle density function in
the limit N→∞ , and (ii) the hydrodynamic limit where we pass from Boltzmann’s kinetic equation to the
(macroscopic) equations of fluid motion in the limit where the collision rate goes to infinity.
Despite being the subject of extensive study in the last century, this problem remained open for more
than 120 years after it was first announced. While the second hydrodynamic limit is much better understood
(see the discussions in Section 1.4.1 below), the main difficulty in the resolution of Hilbert’s sixth problem
lies in justifying the first kinetic limit, which is the subject of this manuscript. In this direction, the state of
art can be briefly summarized as follows, see Section 1.2 for a more extensive review. A landmark result of
Lanford [40] in 1975 provides the rigorous derivation of the Boltzmann equation for sufficiently short time .
This result has since been completed, improved, and further developed by different groups of authors (see for
example Illner-Pulvirenti [34], Gallagher-Saint Raymond-Texier [27] and more recent works [49, 8, 9, 11, 7]
concerning fluctuations). However, all these works are still restricted to perturbative regimes, either under
short-time or near-vacuum conditions or at the exact Maxwellian equilibrium.
The main result of this paper fills this gap, and thus completes the justification of the first limit needed
for the resolution of Hilbert’s sixth problem. More precisely, we provide a rigorous derivation of Boltzmann’s
equation from hard sphere dynamics, for arbitrarily long time that covers the full lifespan of the Boltzmann
solution. This solution may come from any given initial data, which can also be arbitrarily large. In
particular, if a solution to the Boltzmann equation exists globally, then our derivation is valid for all finite
times, and times going (slowly) to infinity. In the companion work [24], we will connect this to the second
hydrodynamic limit, thus giving a full resolution to Hilbert’s sixth problem (see Section 1.4.1).
1.1. Setup and the main result. In this section we state our main result, Theorem 1. We start by
describing the microscopic dynamics of the interacting particles, the kinetic formalism involving s-particle
density functions, and the Boltzmann equation governing the effective dynamics of those functions. The
microscopic system here is given by the standard hard sphere particle model, under the Boltzmann-Grad
scaling law. We shall discuss some other scenarios in Section 1.4.2.
1.1.1. The hard sphere dynamics. Consider N hard sphere particles with diameter ε.
Definition 1.1 (The hard sphere dynamics [1]) . We define the dynamics for the N-particle hard sphere
system with diameter ε in dimension d≥ 2. This will be referred to as the Original or O-dynamics when
necessary, to distinguish it with the various modified dynamics in Definitions 3.15, 3.17, 4.1 and 4.3 below.
1This was one of the two questions proposed in Hilbert’s follow-up article [33] expanding on his ICM address. The other
question was to establish the mathematical foundation of probability theory, which was done in the 1930–40s.

We list some basic properties of the hard sphere dynamics, which hold up to Lebesgue zero sets:
Proposition 1.2. There exists a closed subset Z⊆D N with Lebesgue measure|Z| = 0, such that the time
evolution defined by the hard sphere system (Definition 1.1 (3)) exists and is unique on DN\Z, and satisfies
(1) No two collisions happen at the same time t;
(2) The total number of collisions has an absolute upper bound that depends only on d and N;
(3) The flow maps HN(t) defined in Definition 1.1 (4) are measure preserving diffeomorphisms from
DN\Z to itself, and satisfy the flow or semi-group property HN(t +s) =HN(t)HN(s) for t,s≥ 0.
Proof. This is proved in Alexander [1]. □

From now on we will always ignore the Lebesgue zero set Z in Proposition 1.2 (as they do not contribute
to Lebesgue integrals), and will not distinguish the set DN fromDN\Z. We will do the same for all the
modified dynamics below (Definitions 3.15, 3.17, 4.1 and 4.3).
1.1.2. The grand canonical ensemble. In this paper, we study the hard sphere system with a random number
of particles which have random initial states, whose law is given by the grand canonical ensemble.
The grand canonical ensemble is defined by a density function W0 :∪NDN → R≥0 (or equivalently a
sequence of density functions W0,N :DN→ R≥0) that determines the law of the random initial data for the
hard sphere system. We state the definition as follows:
Definition 1.3 (The grand canonical ensemble). Fix 0<ε ≪ 1 and a nonnegative function f0 =f0(z) with´
f0(z) dz = 1. We define the grand canonical ensemble as follows.

Remark 1.5. We make a few comments on the scope of Theorem 1.
(1) Assumptions on the Boltzmann solution : the Maxwellian decay off(t,z ) inv is standard for solutions
to the Boltzmann equation, and in principle can be replaced by bounds in any space in which the
Boltzmann equation is locally well-posed by fixed point arguments. On the other hand, the required
integrability inx in (1.15) for the initial data f0 is basically at the level of L1
x, which is the minimal
requirement to define and normalize the ensemble in (1.7)–(1.8).
(2) The time interval [0,t fin]: if the solution f(t,z ) to the Boltzmann equation (1.13) develops singularity
at some finite time tsing, then the tfin in Theorem 1 can be chosen as anything strictly less than tsing.
If the solution exists and is uniformly bounded for all time t> 0, then the tfin in Theorem 1 can be
chosen as a function of ε that diverges as ε→ 0; the current proof would lead to tfin∼ log| logε| (or
a power of it). We do not expect this to be optimal, and it is an interesting question whether this
can be improved (say to tfin∼| logε|).

===== PAGE 7 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 7
(3) Parameters and convergence rate : the restriction s≤| logε| and the convergence rate εθ in (1.18)
are not expected to be optimal, and are chosen primarily for convenience. The method of our proof
would cover the case s≤ε−κ for sufficiently small κ >0 depending on tfin; on the other hand, if s
is as large as ε−1 then (1.18) might not hold even for initial data, see [49].
Next, fors = 1, the optimal value ofθ should be 1− and can be obtained by refining our proof; as
for the non-factorized parts or cumulants fors≥ 2, their optimal decay rate is linked to the derivation
of the fluctuation equations about the Boltzmann limit, which we will discuss in Section 1.4.3 below.
Finally, the value E(N)·εd−1 (inverse of the Knudsen number) occurring in the Boltzmann-Grad
scaling law (1.12) can be replaced by any constant α independent of ε (by replacing the ε−(d−1)N in
(1.7)–(1.8) with (αε−(d−1))N), or (a suitable power of) log | logε| as in (2).
(4) The grand canonical ensemble : we choose to work with the grand canonical ensemble (Definition
1.3), as opposed to the canonical ensemble where the particle number N is fixed, only to simplify
some calculations with cumulants. This simplification was observed in several recent works on this
subject [49, 8, 9, 7]. Theorem 1 remains true for the canonical ensemble, but the proof would be
much longer due to the extra manipulations needed to reduce the expressions of cumulants.
1.2. Background and history. We briefly discuss the background and history of the rigorous derivation
of Boltzmann’s equation, and the closely related problem of deriving its wave counterpart, i.e. the wave
kinetic equation in wave turbulence theory.
1.2.1. Particle systems and the Boltzmann equation. The study of large particle systems has a long history,
starting from the beginning of the theory of statistical physics. The kinetic theory, which lies at the heart
of nonequilibrium statistical physics, started with the following genius observation of Boltzmann [12, 13]:
assuming independence of states between different particles, the full statistics of the system can be described
by a single closed effective equation, namely the kinetic equation, in the limit of infinite degrees of freedom.
The first instance of what is now known as the Boltzmann equation was written down in the 1870s;
since then, the mathematical justification of this limiting process has been a major challenge. The main
difficulty comes from justifying the assumption of (asymptotic) independence of different particles, which
is known as the Stosszahlansatz or molecular chaos. The formulation of this problem as known today was
first put forward by Grad, and the works of Cercignani and Grad in the 1950s introduced the important
idea of the BBGKY hierarchy to this subject. In the mean time, a simplified model was also introduced and
studied by Kac ([38], see also subsequent and related developments [47, 51, 43, 44, 45]). Motivated by these
contributions, Lanford [40] (see also King [39]) was able to set a first milestone in this subject, by proving
the molecular chaos assumption and deriving the Boltzmann equation for sufficiently small time.
Since [40], there have been numerous results devoted to the derivation of the Boltzmann equation and
related problems. We do not attempt to be exhaustive in recounting the vast literature, but only list some of
the most important ones below; see the surveys [53, 6, 10] and references therein for the other contributions.
• Illner-Pulvirenti [34] (see also [35]), which obtained a long-time result but for the near-vacuum case
(i.e. the collision rate E(N)·εd−1≪ 1 in (1.12));
• Gallagher-Saint Raymond-Texier [27], which completed and refined the result of [40], and obtained
explicit convergence rates;
• Pulvirenti-Simonella [49], which obtained explicit decay rates for cumulants associated with the
hard sphere system, with the exponent proportional to the number of involved particles, again in
the short-time regime;
• Bodineau-Gallagher-Saint Raymond [3, 4, 5], which studied the related problems of deriving Brow-
nian motion, Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes, and the Stokes-Fourier system from the hard sphere
dynamics, by passing through a linearized Boltzmann equation;
• Bodineau-Gallagher-Saint Raymond-Simonella [8, 9, 11, 7], which investigated the further question
of deriving the equation for fluctuations, either for short time, or for longer times but at the exact
Maxwellian equilibrium.

===== PAGE 8 =====
8 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
Finally, apart from the question of deriving the kinetic equations, there has also been a huge literature
concerning the solution theory of these equations (Boltzmann, Landau etc.). In general strong solutions
exist in local and perturbative settings, but only weak solutions are known globally (see however [30, 36] on
recent global existence results in the homogeneous setting). We will not elaborate on this, but refer to the
reviews in [56, 57] and references therein.
1.2.2. Wave turbulence and the wave kinetic equation. One of the most important developments in the
physics of the twentieth century was the emergence of quantum physics as a theory to describe subatomic
particle dynamics. A central idea here is the particle-wave duality, which contributed to an exponential rise
in the physical and mathematical interest in nonlinear wave systems. This led to the recasting of many
classical particle theories in the context of interacting wave systems, including the theory of nonequilibrium
statistical physics for waves, known as the wave turbulence or wave kinetic theory.
Same as the particle case, the wave kinetic theory aims at describing the statistics of a wave system using
a closed equation for the lowest order correlation; usually this is the second moment of the wave field, as
opposed to the one-particle density function in the kinetic theory for particles. The role of the Boltzmann
equation is then played by the wave kinetic equation (WKE). For instance, in dimension d = 3, when the
wave system is modeled by the cubic nonlinear Schr¨ odinger equation, the corresponding wave kinetic equation
takes the form
(∂t +v·∇x)f =
ˆ
R3
ˆ
S2
 
(v−v1)·ω

+·
 
f′f′
1(f +f1)−ff1(f′ +f′
1)

dωdv1 (1.19)
(including also the homogeneous variant where the v·∇x term in (1.19) is absent), where we use the same
notation as in (1.14). The wave kinetic equations, similar to (1.19) but for different wave systems, were first
introduced in the work of Peierls [48] in 1929 for anharmonic crystals. This was shortly followed by works
of Nordheim [37] and Uehling and Uhlenbeck [55] in the context of quantum gases. Since then, the theory
has attracted a great deal of attention from mathematicians and physicists, and has developed into a rich
and broad subject. In particular, these contributions included the influential works of Hasselmann [31, 32]
in 1962–1963, which applied the wave kinetic theory to ocean waves and led to the modern methods of ocean
surface forecasting, as well as the work of Zakharov [58] in 1965, which provided a systematic approach
to the theory and how it can be used to understand turbulent phenomena for waves. In [58], Zakharov
also introduced the notion of Kolmogorov-Zakharov spectra, along the same lines as Kolmogorov’s theory of
hydrodynamic turbulence. It is for this reason that the theory is also commonly known as wave turbulence
theory. We refer the reader to [46] for a textbook treatment.
Mathematically, the study of the derivation of wave kinetic equations like (1.19) started with the work
of Spohn [54]. For linear models, this program was completed in Erd¨ os-Yau [26] and Erd¨ os-Salmhofer-Yau
[25]. For the full nonlinear problem, an important early milestone was due to Lukkarinen-Spohn [41] and
subsequent partial results were obtained in [14, 19, 16, 17]. We refer the reader to the introduction of [22]
for a more exhaustive historical recount. The full derivation of (1.19) in the short-time regime was obtained
by the first two authors [20, 21, 22], serving as the wave-counterpart of Lanford’s theorem.
Recently, in [23], the first two authors have extended the derivation of (1.19) to arbitrarily long times
that cover the full lifespan of solutions to (1.19). We remark that the result of [23] is parallel to Theorem
1, not only conceptually but also on a very concrete level. In fact, while the proof in the current paper is
self-contained, many of the key ideas in this proof are obtained by adapting the ideas from the proof in [23]
to the particle setting. In Section 2.5 we will make a detailed comparison between the proofs in [23] and in
the current paper, including both similarities and differences.
1.3. Main ideas of the proof. In this subsection, we present a mostly philosophical description of the
proof, its strategy, and its most important ideas. A more elaborate and concrete discussion of these ideas
can be found in Section 2.
To a large extent, the framework of the proof in this paper is the same as that in [23]. Below we will
explain this framework in the current context of particle systems.

===== PAGE 9 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 9
1.3.1. Time layering. The well-known fundamental difficulty in extending Lanford’s theorem to longer times,
which is the same difficulty in the long-time derivation of the wave kinetic equation, is the divergence of time
expansions of solutions. In fact, in both settings, the proof of the short-time result is based on matching two
different time expansions: expansion I, given by the Duhamel expansion of the dynamics of the microscopic
system (or the BBGKY hierarchy satisfied by the correlation functions fs), and expansion II, given by the
Duhamel expansion of the kinetic equation. The bulk of the proof 2 goes into identifying the leading terms in
expansion I, matching them order-by-order with the terms in expansion II, and proving that all the remaining
non-leading terms vanish in the limit. At a basic level, both expansions are bounded like P
nCntn, which is
convergent only for sufficiently small times |t|<C−1 given by the radius of convergence. Beyond this time
both expansions will diverge, and this methods fails in a fundamental way.
To go beyond this threshold and reach an arbitrarily large time t =tfin, a natural idea is to subdivide the
long time interval [0,t ] into L subintervals (called time layers) [(ℓ− 1)τ,ℓτ ] with 1≤ℓ≤ L andτ sufficiently
small, such that on each subinterval, the solution to the kinetic equation is given by a convergent series
expansion with base time ( ℓ− 1)τ. This time layering is the first key idea in the proof, which allows
to resolve the divergence problem for expansion II mentioned above, regarding solutions to the Boltzmann
equation. This leaves us with the task of controlling the microscopic system (expansion I) on [( ℓ− 1)τ,ℓτ ]
using the data at time ( ℓ− 1)τ. An obvious difficulty arises with this approach, as the data at ( ℓ− 1)τ
will no longer have exact independence between different particles. Such independence (or equivalently the
complete factorization of the correlation functions fs) is granted3 at time 0, and plays a fundamental role in
controlling the expansion I of the microscopic system in the short-time proof.
1.3.2. Cumulants. In order to go from time ( ℓ− 1)τ to ℓτ, we need to propagate some information for the
ensemble at time ℓτ that describes departure from independence. This will be given by the cumulants.
Following [49], the cumulants can be defined as the EH occurring in the following expansion of the s-point
density function:
fs(t,zs) =
Y
j∈[s]
fA(t,zj) +
X
∅̸=H⊆[s]
 Y
j∈[s]\H
fA(t,zj)

·EH(t,zH)
| {z }
OL1(εθ)
, (1.20)
where [s] = {1,...,s } and zH = (zj)j∈H. Here the first term on the right-hand side represents the fully
factorized part of the correlation, withfA(t,z ) being a one-particle density function, and the remaining terms
involving cumulants EH describe the deviation from independence. Similar cumulant expansions have also
played a central role in the wave setting [23] and in a series of recent works on particle systems [49, 7, 8, 10].
The physical interpretation of (1.20) is as follows: the left-hand side denotes the probability density of
finding s-particles, say 1,··· ,s , with states z1,··· ,zs, at time t. This depends on the collision history of
these particles among themselves and with remaining particles. The first term on the right hand side comes
from collision histories where the s particles are disconnected, i.e. they do not interact with each other and
each of them interacts with a disjoint set of the remaining particles. The second term comes from collision
histories where the particles in H are connected to each other via collisions.
By (1.20), we see that Theorem 1 would follow if we can show that (a) fA converges to the Boltzmann
solution, and (b)∥EH∥L1 converges to zero in the limit as ε→ 0.
1.3.3. The cumulant ansatz. As discussed in Section 1.3.2, a natural route of proving Theorem 1 is to
approximate fA by the Boltzmann solution (Proposition 6.1; this is relatively easy, see Section 2.4 for
further discussions, and Section 14 for the proof), and prove that
2In the wave case, there is an extra level of complication coming from the fact that the series does not converge absolutely,
but only conditionally.
3Strictly speaking, we do not have exact independence due to the 1 DN (zN) factor in (1.7), but the cumulants caused by
this factor are easy to deal with (see Proposition 5.18), and will be ignored here.

===== PAGE 10 =====
10 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
• For each 1≤ ℓ≤ L, we have the cumulant expansion (1.20) at t = ℓτ, with some constant α >0
such that
∥EH(ℓτ)∥L1≤εα|H|, ∀H̸= ∅. (1.21)
This EH estimate is stated in Proposition 6.2. Note that this is a reasonable conclusion to aim for. In
fact, for ℓ = 0 (1.21) is trivial, while for ℓ = 1 this follows from Lanford’s theorem and the proof in [49]. The
next natural question is: how to proceed on the intervals [( ℓ− 1)τ,ℓτ ], to prove (1.21) at time ℓτ.
In [49], the authors show that if the cumulants at ( ℓ− 1)τ satisfy the L∞ estimate
∥EH((ℓ− 1)τ)∥L∞≤εβ|H|,
then one can recover the L1 estimate (1.21) at time ℓτ. The crucial point here is that one has to start
with the stronger L∞ bound, and can only recover the weaker L1 bound, therefore this estimate cannot be
iterated.
There is something deep behind this phenomenon: for any Banach space X, it is not possible to obtain
smallness of ∥EH(ℓτ)∥X (in the form of (1.21)) using only smallness of the same norm ∥EH((ℓ− 1)τ)∥X,
precisely because of the time irreversibility of Boltzmann’s equation.
In fact, suppose the contrary, that such an inductive proof of the ∥·∥ X bound was possible. Take ℓ = 2.
Then, by the time reversibility of the Newtonian dynamics (Definition 1.1), we could either go forward from
time τ to time 2τ, or backward to time 0, which just differ by flipping the signs of the velocities vj at time
τ. Since ∥·∥ X is invariant under such sign flipping (which is true for any natural Banach norm such as Lp
norms), we would conclude from (1.20) (assuming the approximation fA(t,z )≈f(t,z )) that
fs(2τ,z 1,··· ,zs) =f(2τ,z 1)··· f(2τ,zs) +OX(ευ)
as well as
fs(0,z 1,··· ,zs) =f(2τ,z 1)··· f(2τ,zs) +OX(ευ).
This is impossible, as the leading term in fs(0,··· ) is given by tensor product of f(0,·) and not f(2τ,·).
By the above discussion, we know that (1.21) cannot be proved by direct induction in ℓ. In fact, in order
to prove (1.21), we need to propagate structural information (which goes beyond any Banach norms) for
the cumulants EH(ℓτ) by induction in ℓ. Such structural information will be provided by a partial time
expansion argument, a second key ingredient of the proof, which we discuss below.
Start from a cumulantEH(ℓτ), and assumefA has already been approximated by the Boltzmann solution.
By performing a cluster expansion (see Section 5 or [7]) on [( ℓ− 1)τ,ℓτ ], we can express this cumulant as
a sum of Duhamel time integrals involving fA((ℓ− 1)τ) and EH((ℓ− 1)τ). Now, the crucial point in the
partial time expansion is that, we refrain from expandingfA((ℓ− 1)τ) any further in time (and use instead
its proximity to the Boltzmann solution), and only expand the cumulant terms EH((ℓ− 1)τ) in terms of
fA((ℓ− 2)τ) and EH((ℓ− 2)τ), and so on. This is very important in our proof as it allows us to avoid the
tn divergence (discussed in Section 1.3.1); indeed, such divergence only comes from the aggregation if we
were to expand the leading terms fA all the way to time t = 0, which we are avoiding in this partial time
expansion. On the other hand, the cumulants come with power gains (of size εα|H|, see (1.21)), which offsets
the tn divergence even if they are expanded all the way to time t = 0.
This structural information constructed above will be further discussed in Section 1.3.4 below. As far
as we know, such partial expansion construction has not appeared in previous literature, and is crucial in
obtaining a time irreversible equation from time reversible dynamics.
1.3.4. Representing cumulants by molecules. Recall the physical interpretation of the cumulants in Section
1.3.2 and the partial time expansion in Section 1.3.3. With these, we know that the cumulant EH(ℓτ) can
be written as a sum of contributions corresponding to probability densities of possible collision histories on
[0,ℓτ ] involving particles in H. These collision histories are constructed by the partial time expansions, and
they satisfy that the particles in H are connected to each other via collisions (cf. Section 1.3.2).
If we disregard the precise positions, velocities and collision times in the particle trajectories, and focus
only on their combinatorial structure, then each collision history will topologically reduce to an abstract

===== PAGE 11 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 11
diagram, called a molecule. Such diagrams encode the possible patterns of collisions among the particles
in H, and disregard the precise geometric information. This process of abstraction is illustrated in Figure 5
(Section 3.1), where particle trajectories are depicted as sets of edges sharing the same color, and collisions
are represented by nodes, called atoms. In addition, the molecule also retains the ordering between different
collisions of the same particle, and the layer of each collision (represented by horizontal lines in Figure 5).
Now, with the molecular representation of collision histories, we can formally state the structural infor-
mation for the cumulant EH(ℓτ) in Section 1.3.3, as follows:
|EH(ℓτ)|≤
X
M
|IN M|, (1.22)
where the sum is taken over molecules M that are constructed from the partial expansion process in Sec-
tion 1.3.3 (see Definition 3.20 and Proposition 3.25), and |IN M| is an explicit expression representing the
normalized probability density that the collision history described by M happens (see Definition 3.23).
The formulas (1.20) and (1.22) will be stated in Proposition 3.25 and proved in Section 5. For reasons
that will be discussed in Section 1.3.5 below, in this proof we also need a truncation on the dynamics, which
is defined in Section 4. For a more detailed discussion of all the above ingredients, see Section 2.1.
1.3.5. Reduction to molecule estimates and truncation of the dynamics. To obtain the bound on ∥EH∥L1
in (1.21) using (1.22), one has to obtain effective estimates for the L1 norm of the normalized probability
densities|IN M|. Here, the central quantity in understanding such estimates is the number of recollisions
ρ in the molecule M. This is defined as the circuit rank of M, viewed as an undirected graph, which is the
number of independent cycles in M. Heuristically, one expects that as the recollision number ρ increases,
the number of molecules M in the sum (1.22) also increases. This divergence has to be balanced by gains in
the estimates on|IN M| for molecules with larger ρ. This heuristic is quantified by establishing the following
two estimates. Given ρ≥ 1, we have:
• Bounding the number of terms in (1.22). The number of molecules M with ρ recollisions satisfies
the bound
#M≤C|M|·| logε|Cρ; (1.23)
• Bounding the contribution of each term. The contribution of each molecule M is bounded by


|IN M|


L1≤τ|M|·ευρ (υ >0). (1.24)
Note how the above two bounds exhibit the effect of recollisions: each more recollision leads to | logε|C
loss in the number of molecules in (1.23), which is offset by the ευ gain in the renormalized probabilities in
(1.24). The cumulant estimate (1.21) then follows directly from (1.22)–(1.24), provided that ε≪τ≪ 1.
From our construction, the molecule M consists of ℓ layers, and can be seen as a concatenation of ℓ
sub-molecules Mℓ′ (ℓ′≤ ℓ), where Mℓ′ contains the atoms in layer ℓ′. To prove (1.23) and (1.24), we need
to impose an upper bound on the number of atoms and recollisions in each of the sub-molecule Mℓ′. To
enforce the above restrictions, we introduce the notion of truncated dynamics (see Definition 4.3) within
each time layer. More precisely, in the truncated dynamics, at each time where a collision would occur, we
check whether or not this collision would cause a violation to the following two conditions:
(1) The number of particles in each cluster is at most Λ := | logε|O(1), and
(2) The number of recollisions (i.e. independent cycles) in each cluster is at most Γ = O(1),
where clusters are the subsets of particles that are connected by collisions. If this collision would cause a
violation, then we “turn off” this collision and allow the particles to cross each other; otherwise we allow
this collision just as in the original hard sphere dynamics.
Of course, this truncation also leads to an error term ferr
s which occurs when the original dynamics (or in
fact the extended version of it, see Definition 4.1) does not coincide with the truncated dynamics. We will
control this error term in Proposition 6.3. In fact, it can be expressed in terms of |IN M|, and bounded using
similar arguments, in a much similar way as EH, see Section 2.4 and Section 15.

===== PAGE 12 =====
12 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
Given (1) and (2), it follows that the number of atoms in any molecule M can be bounded by| logε|O(1).
The estimate (1.23) then follows from the well-known combinatorial fact that the number of graphs whose
vertices have maximum degree 4, with at most m vertices, and ρ independent cycles is bounded by Cm·mρ
for some constant C >0. Applying this to molecules yields:
#M≤C|M|·| M|ρ≤C|M|·| logε|Cρ,
as claimed in (1.23) (Proposition 7.2; see Section 2.2 for more details). As a result, we are left with proving
the estimate (1.24), which is the most delicate part of the proof.
1.3.6. The cutting algorithm. In the proof, we will reduce the estimate of


|IN M|


L1 to a purely combinato-
rial problem for the molecule M. To this end, we first rewrite it as


|IN M|


L1 =ε(d−1)|H|·I M(Q), whereIM
is an operator involving integration over all intermediate positions and velocities (associated with the edges
of M) and all collision times (associated with the atoms of M), and Q is a specific input function. These
integrals involve Diracδ functions, which encode the relations between incoming and outgoing velocities at
each collision. The reduction to this form is contained in Section 7, see Proposition 7.5.
Then, to get optimal bounds forIM(Q), we need to find the correct order of variables in which to integrate.
For this, we introduce the key notion of cutting, see Definition 8.3, which provides a systematic way of
deciding the order of integration in IM (i.e. applying the Fubini theorem). More precisely, each cutting
divides the molecule M into two separate molecules M1 and M2; by first integrating in those variables
associated with the molecule M2 (with those variables associated with the molecule M1 regarded as fixed)
and then integrating in those variables associated with the molecule M1, we get that
IM =IM1◦I M2. (1.25)
Now, using (1.25), our strategy is to devise a cutting algorithm which cuts M into a number of “small”
molecules Mj with one or two atoms, called elementary molecules (see Definition 8.11). The operators
IMj can be directly calculated and estimated (say in the L∞→ L∞ norm), so that applying (1.25) then
yields an upper bound forIM. The two most important types of elementary molecules are the {3} molecules
which represent collisions (i.e. a particle p colliding with a given particle p′), and the{33} molecules which
represent collisions followed by recollisions (i.e. a particle p colliding with a given particle p′, and then
colliding again with another given particlep′′). It is known that{3} molecules are normal in the sense that
integrating it does not gain any power, while {33} molecules are good in the sense that integrating it gains
a power ευ for some constant υ >0 (there are also {4}-molecules which are usually viewed as bad due to
“wasting” of dimensions of integration). The discussion of elementary molecules, and associated weight and
volume bounds, are proved in Section 9.1, see Definition 9.4.
With the above discussions, it is now clear that the main ingredient in the proof of (1.24) is to construct
a cutting algorithm (see Proposition 9.7) that produces sufficiently many good molecules. This is a major
novelty and main technical ingredient, and is discussed in more details in Section 2.3. Roughly speaking,
the cutting algorithm consists of two big steps: (A) reducing the multi-layer case to the two-layer case by
performing a layer refining and a layer selection process; (B) treating the two-layer case by exhibiting a
dichotomy between two main scenarios and applying several basic algorithms.
In Section 11, we start with a simplified case, illustrating the main ideas of the algorithm; then we extend
these ideas to full generality, and present the proofs in steps (A) and (B) in Sections 12 and 13 respectively.
1.4. Future horizons. Here we list some future directions, now within reach, with the proof of Theorem 1.
1.4.1. The hydrodynamic limit. After deriving the Boltzmann equation from particle dynamics in the kinetic
limit, we would need to match it with the hydrodynamic limit and derive the fluid equations (Euler and
Navier-Stokes). Formally, this requires considering densities that are local Maxwellians, i.e. those functions
that are Maxwellian in v but with mass, center and variance depending on ( t,x ):
f(t,x,v ) = ρ(t,x )

===== PAGE 13 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 13
By considering perturbation expansions (i.e. Hilbert and Chapman-Enskog expansions) around (1.26), we
can derive the compressible Euler equation for ( ρ,u,T ) and the incompressible Euler and Navier-Stokes
equations for subsequent terms, in a suitable scaling limit.
Compared to the derivation of the Boltzmann equation, the derivation of fluid equations is much better
understood, see for example [18, 29, 52, 28]. In [24], by combining these results with an analog of Theorem
1 on the torus Td, we can finally execute both steps of Hilbert’s program in tandem, and derive the fluid
equations from Newtonian particle particle systems in the Boltzmann-Grad limit.
1.4.2. The short-range potential case. With the methods in the current paper, a natural problem that should
be within reach, is the short range potential case studied in [27], namely

2. A detailed overview of the proof
In this section, we give a more detailed sketch of the proof of Theorem 1. As explained in Section 1.3,
we will divide [0 ,t fin] into smaller intervals [( ℓ− 1)τ,ℓτ ] where τ ≪ 1, see (1.30). Recall also the notion
zA := (zj)j∈A and f⊗A in Definition 1.6 (4).
2.1. The cumulant expansion formula. As discussed in Section 1.3, the first step of the proof is to
develop the cumulant expansion formulas (1.20) and (1.22). This is inspired by the previous works [49, 7],
but with the key new idea of multi-layer, partial time expansion (see Section 1.3.3) and several extra twists.
2.1.1. Statement of the formula. We start by stating the precise version of (1.20) and (1.22) as follows:
f(ℓτ,zs) =
X
H⊆[s]

fA(ℓτ,·)
⊗([s]\H)
·EH(ℓτ,zH) + Err(ℓτ,zs),
|EH(ℓτ)|≤
X
M∈F,r (M)=H
|IN M|.
(2.1)
Here Err(ℓτ,zs), omitted in (1.20), denotes an extremely small error term, which we will neglect in the
remainder of the analysis. The sets F, r(M) and the quantity|IN M| will be defined and discussed below.
As explained in Section 1.3.4, a molecule M (Definition 3.1) is a diagram, illustrated in Figure 3 (Section
3.1), that encodes the combinatorial structure of the collision history. It is obtained by disregarding the
precise spatial and temporal details of particle trajectories, retaining only the topological pattern of inter-
actions, in a process called topological reduction (Definition 3.8). It consists of several key components
listed below, which are illustrated in Figure 3:
• C/O atoms, which represent individual collisions/overlaps, are depicted as nodes like ◦/•.
• Edges, which represent segments of free transport between collisions along a particle trajectory, are
depicted as edges between nodes.
• Particle lines , which represent the full trajectory of each particle, are visualized in Figure 3 as
chains of edges sharing the same color. They will be identified with particles in Sections 3–5. Each
particle line has two ends, corresponding to the initial and final trajectories of the particle.
• Layers are the subsets Mℓ′ ⊆ M in Figure 3 between the horizontal lines representing times ℓ′τ
and (ℓ′ + 1)τ. Dividing M into layers Mℓ′ corresponds to assigning a layer ℓ′ to each atom n which
corresponds to a collision that happens in the time interval [ ℓ′τ, (ℓ′ + 1)τ].

===== PAGE 16 =====
16 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
• Each particle line p has bottom end e, and a corresponding start layerℓ1[p] =ℓ1[e]; this indicates
the time (ℓ1[p]− 1)τ at which the particle starts interacting with the system. Similarly, it also has
a top end e′ and a finish layerℓ2[p] =ℓ2[e′], indicating the time ℓ2[p]·τ at which the particle stops
interacting with the system.
• Root particle lines are the particle lines in H (occurring in the cumulant EH), i.e. those pene-
trating the top horizontal line ℓτ in Figure 3.
We now explain the definitions of |IN M| andF in (2.1). The expression |IN M| is given by

where the notations are explained as follows:
• r(M),p(M), and (p\r)(M) denote the sets of root particle lines, all particle lines, and non-root particle
lines respectively, and |M|p\r :=|(p\r)(M)|. Each particle in p(M) is connected (via collisions and
overlaps) to some root particle in r(M). Moreover, ℓ1[p] is the start layer of p as above, i.e. the first
time that p is involved in the system.
• SM denotes the dynamics involving only the particles in p(M). The indicator function 1 M restricts
the integration to trajectories that topologically reduce to the molecule M. The term 1 ε
L enforces
additional constraints that certain particles are initially close to each other, which comes from initial
cumulants.
• The expression in (2.2) expresses |IN M| as the renormalized probability density contributed by
the collision history encoded by M (with additional information contained in 1 L). Here z(p\r)(M)
represent the final states of non-root particles, and integration in them leaves the result being a
density function of only the root variables zr(M).
The input factors fA((ℓ1[p]− 1)τ) come from the start time ( ℓ1[p]− 1)τ where particle p enters
the system; their variables represent the initial states of particles, which are related to the variables
zp(M) by the transformationSM (strictly speaking, there should be a linear transport in the variables
of fA which transforms between time 0 and (ℓ1[p]− 1)τ, see (3.13)).
The sum in (2.1) is taken over molecules M∈F , which is defined as follows. Recall Mℓ′⊆ M is formed
by atoms with layer ℓ′. Define r(Mℓ′) to be the set of particle lines p that cross into layer ℓ′ from higher
layers (i.e. when the start layer ℓ1[p]≤ℓ′, and the finish layer ℓ2[p]>ℓ′), which is also the the root particle
set of Mℓ′. Then, roughly speaking, the set F consists of molecules M that satisfy the following conditions:
(1) Every connected component of Mℓ′ must contain at least one particle line from r(Mℓ′).
(2) The number of atoms in Mℓ′ is bounded above by| logε|O(1).
(3) Each layer Mℓ′ is “almost” a forest; i.e. we have proper control on its number of cycles.
(4) For all 1≤ℓ′≤ℓ, every particle line in r(Mℓ′) is connected either to another particle line in r(Mℓ′),
or to a particle line in r(Mℓ′−1), via edges within Mℓ′.
In the above conditions, (1) states that in layer Mℓ′, we only track the dynamics of those particles that are
connected to the root particles inr(Mℓ′), through a chain of collisions and overlaps within Mℓ′. Next, (2)–(3)
come from the truncation of dynamics (described in Section 1.3.5), which limits the number of particles and
recollisions in each layer.
Finally, (4) states that every particle inr(Mℓ′) is either connected (via collisions and overlaps within Mℓ′)
to another particle line inr(Mℓ′), or connected to a particle that occurs in the cumulantEH at time (ℓ′−1)τ
(i.e. crosses into layers < ℓ′ from layer ℓ′ and belongs to r(Mℓ′−1)). This is because we are consideing the
collision histories that contribute to the cumulant EH, and because of our partial expansion (Section 1.3.3),
where only the cumulants EH((ℓ′− 1)τ) are further expanded into previous time layers.
The precise definitions of |IN M| andF will be given in Definitions 3.23 and 3.20, respectively. The
cumulant expansion formula (2.1) will be formally stated in Proposition 3.25. The conditions (1)–(4) listed
above correspond, with minor modifications, to those in Definition 3.20 (1a)–(1d).

2.1.2. Proof of the formula. In this section, we sketch the proof of the cumulant formula (2.1). By (1.10)
and (1.11), we have
f(ℓτ,zs) =ε(d−1)s

Prerequisite: The truncated dynamics. Before starting, we note that, in order that the moleculeM satisfies
(1)–(3) in Section 2.1.1 (in particular, Mℓ′ being almost a forest means we need to control the number of
recollisions), we need to perform a truncation on the dynamics (see Definition 4.3) within each layer.
In practice, the truncation is done by monitoring each potential collision event. When a collision is about
to occur (i.e. a pre-collisional configuration forms), we check whether it would violate the constraints in (2)
or (3). If such a violation would happen, we “turn-off” the collision by allowing the particles to pass through
one another without a collision; otherwise, we allow the collision to happen as in the original dynamics.
Step 1: The induction setup. The general strategy is to verify (2.1) by induction. For the base case ℓ = 0,
we rely on the exact factorization at t = 0 in (1.7), except the factor 1 DN . This latter factor leads to the
initial cumulants, which is the reason of the factors 1 ε
L in (2.2). These initial cumulants can be treated in
the standard way (see Proposition 5.18), and will be ignored in this section.
Then, we need the induction step, where we derive (2.1) for ℓ from the same formula for ℓ− 1. In order
to identify the factorized and cumulant parts at time ℓτ based on those at (ℓ− 1)τ, we need to perform the
cluster expansion, which is detailed in Step 2 below. Here, the clusters associated with the trajectories
on [(ℓ− 1)τ,ℓτ ] (and the corresponding transport operators SN(τ)), are defined as the maximal subsets of
particles that are connected by collisions within [(ℓ− 1)τ,ℓτ ]. Denote by M the molecule (without O-atoms)
which is the result of topological reduction of the trajectory on [( ℓ− 1)τ,ℓτ ], and let Mj be the connected
components of M, corresponding to each cluster.
Step 2: The cluster expansion. Let p(M) be the set of all particles in M etc. Let 1 M be the indicator
function that the trajectory of the particles in p(M) topologically reduces to M, and let 1 Mj̸∼Mj′ be the
indicator function that the particles in p(Mj) never collide with those in p(Mj′). Then we have the following
cluster expansion identity:

Step 3: Penrose argument and overlaps. The indicator function 1 Mj̸∼Mj′ in (2.5) indicates there is
no overlap between the trajectories of particles in p(Mj) and p(Mj′), which implies the further factoriza-
tion 1 Mj̸∼Mj′ = Q

into a summation of terms, using the standard Penrose argument similar to those in [8]. Here, compared
to [8], we need to perform an extra truncation in the Penrose argument (in order to bound the number of
clusters by Λ =| logε|C), which leads to extra complications.

===== PAGE 18 =====
18 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
In the end, we can recast (2.5) as a summation over single layer molecules M which satisfy (1)–(3) in
Section 2.1.1. This M is formed by joining the clusters Mj (which contain only C-atoms) by O-atoms,
which correspond to overlaps between edgese∈ Mj ande′∈ Mj′, which come from the indicator functions
1 (Mj,e)∼(Mj′,e′). Then, once M is fixed, we can insert the induction hypothesis (2.1) for (ℓ−1)τ, and factorize
the resulting term, to get an expression of form (2.1) for ℓτ, thus completing the inductive step.
Step 4: Formation of multi-layer molecules. With Steps 1–3 above, we can then induct on ℓ to prove the
first line in (2.1), as well as an expression (upper bound) of |EH(ℓτ)| in terms of summation over sequences
(Mℓ,··· , M1) of molecules. Then, by merging the corresponding top and bottom ends for the same particle
in each different Mℓ′, we can construct a multi-layer molecule M from the sequence (Mℓ′), so the summation
over this sequence is rewritten as a summation over multi-layered molecule M, which leads to the second
line in (2.1) and completes the proof.
The prerequisite, i.e. truncation of dynamics, will be discussed in Section 4. Then, Steps 1–4 will be
carried out in Section 5. The cluster expansion in Step 2 is contained in Section 5.1 (Lemma 5.5), and the
Penrose argument in Step 2 is contained in Section 5.2 (Proposition 5.10). Then we complete the inductive
step in Section 5.3–5.4 (Proposition 5.16), and the base case (initial cumulant) in Section 5.5 (Proposition
5.18). Finally, in Section 5.6, we carry out Step 4 and put everything together to complete the proof.
2.2. Reduction to local integral expressions. Now we have proved (1.20) and (1.22). As discussed in

2.3. The cutting algorithm. Recall that we need to prove (1.24). Using (2.9), we only need to establish
an upper bound for IM(QM). This is a multiple integral; involving many integrated variables; in order to
estimate it, we need to reduce it to an iterated integral by choosing an appropriate order of variables in
which we integrate. This process is implemented through the cutting operation and cutting algorithm
(as sketched in Section 1.3.6), which we now introduce.
2.3.1. Cutting operations, and elementary molecules. Given any molecule M, cutting is the operation that
divides M into two disjoint sub-molecules M1 and M2, where each bond connecting an atom n1∈ M1 to an
atom n2∈ M2 is turned into a free end at n1 and a fixed end at n2. We say M1 is cut as free and M2 is
cut as fixed. See Figure 2 for an illustration, and Definition 8.3 for the precise definition.
Figure 2. Cutting a molecule M into smaller molecules M1 and M2 (where red atoms in
M1 are cut as free, and green atoms in M2 cut as fixed). The fixed ends are marked with
red× symbols.
In terms of the integral in IM, cutting M1 as free (and M2 = M\M1 as fixed) simply means that we first
integrate in the ( ze) and (tn) variables associated with M2 (treating the variables associated with M1 as
fixed) and then integrate in those variables associated with M1. Here when a bond e between M1 and M2 is
cut into a free end e1 at M1 and a fixed end e2 at M2, we always impose the condition ze =ze1 =ze2; see
Figure 2. This is merely a diagrammatic representation of Fubini’s theorem.
With the above conventions, it is straightforward to verify that
IM =IM1◦I M2 (2.10)

In our cutting algorithm, presented in Section 2.3.2 below, we will repeatedly cut M into smaller molecules
until all of them becomes elementary molecules Mj, such that each of them contains only one or two
atoms, so the integralIMj can be calculated explicitly. We can then controlIM(QM) byIM(QM)≤∥QM∥L∞·Q

There are four major types of elementary molecules, denoted by {2},{3},{4} and{33}, indicated by the
degree (i.e number of bonds plus free ends, not counting fixed ends) of each atom. See Figure 15 (Section
8.2) for a picture of elementary molecules and Definition 8.11 for its rigorous definition. Note that {33}
molecules are divided into types{33A} and{33B}, and we also allow{44} molecules which play minor roles
and will not be discussed here.
The key observation is that, for each {33} molecule Mj that occurs in the cutting process (see M2 in
Figure 2), we have the operator norm bound ∥IMj∥L∞→L∞≤ ευ for some positive constant υ, at least in
typical cases. For this reason, we define each {33} molecule to be good, and each {3} and{2} molecule
to be normal. In fact, a {3} molecule corresponds to a normal collision, a {2} molecule corresponds to a
recollision, and a{33} molecule (which can be further decomposed to a{3} and a{2} molecule) corresponds
to a recollision that follows a normal collision, which constitutes the basic unit of gain, as already observed
in [20] in the context of wave turbulence. Finally, we define a {4} molecule to be bad as it “wastes” one
integral and leads to losses∥IMj∥L∞→L∞≤ε−(d−1). See Definition 9.4 for the precise version of classification
of elementary molecules.
Returning to the estimate ofIM(QM), suppose there exists a cutting sequence that decomposes a molecule
M into a collection of molecules ( Mj). Then, by the discussion above and the fact that QM is bounded (say
∥QM∥≤ 1), we obtain
∥|IN M|(zH)∥L1 =ε(d−1)|H|IM(QM)≤

∥IMj∥L∞→L∞≤ευ·#good+(d−1)(|H|−#bad), (2.12)
where we gain a factor of ευ for each good molecule and lose a factor of ε−(d−1) for each bad molecule. By
(2.12), it is clear that (1.24) would follow if we can construct a cutting sequence satisfying
υ· #good + (d− 1)· (|H|− #bad)≥c·ρ. (2.13)
This inequality is stated in Proposition 9.7, and is the main goal of the combinatorial part of this paper. We
discuss the algorithm of constructing the cutting sequence, in Section 2.3.2 below.
The discussion and estimates of elementary molecules are contained in Section 9.1, see Propositions 9.1–
9.3 and Definition 9.4. The proof of (1.24) (as part of Proposition 6.2, see (9.53)) is contained in Section
9.2, using also the auxiliary results (Propositions 9.8–9.9) in Section 9.3.
2.3.2. The cutting algorithm. Finally we discuss the proof of (2.13) stated as Proposition 9.7. This is the
main technical ingredient of the proof, and will occupy Sections 11–13. The idea is to maximize the number
of good (i.e. {33}) molecules and minimize the number of bad (i.e. {4}) molecules, and balance between
them if needed. Below we discuss the main ideas and a few important points in this construction.

===== PAGE 21 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 21
Toy models. The full algorithm, see Sections 12–13, is quite complicated. In Section 11, we will neglect
a few technicalities by making the simplifying assumptions that (i) M has no O-atoms, and (ii) each layer
Mℓ′ is precisely a forest. Moreover, we define the toy models (I, I+, II and III, see Definitions 11.2, 11.9,
11.15, 11.20 and Figure 20) to be specific cases of two layer molecules M: it has an upper layer MU and a
lower layer MD, each of which is a tree. The ρ in (2.13) is comparable to the number of bonds between MU
and MD.
The proof in Section 11 (under the simplifications (i)–(ii)) will consist of two parts: reducing general
multi-layer molecules M to one of the toy models (via layer selection), and designing a cutting algorithm for
each individual toy model.
The UP algorithm. We start with a simple algorithm called UP, see Definitions 11.10. Basically, in
this algorithm we select a lowest degree 3 atom n, and cut the atoms in Sn (the set of descendants of n) from
high to low, see Figure 28 (Section 11.3).
We can show that all the molecules resulting from UP are elementary by preserving the key monotonicity
property: each atom either has no top fixed end or no bottom fixed end, see Proposition 11.12 (1). Moreover,
this algorithm minimizes #{4} (the number of{4} molecules) by allowing only one{4} molecule per connected
component of M.
The drawback is that, the UP algorithm does not necessarily maximize #{33} (the number of {33}
molecules), so it is not directly useful in our proof. However, it is still useful in “finishing off” unimportant
parts of the molecule where we do not expect any gain. Moreover, in some important cases, one can actually
make special adaptations to UP to guarantee the abundance of{33} molecules; this leads to the important
algorithms 2CONNUP and 3COMPUP below.
Toy model I. The feature of toy model I, see Definition 11.2, is that each atom in MU has exactly
one bond with MD. In this case, the cutting algorithm is defined in Definition 11.4. It combines a trivial
algorithm in MU (always cutting lowest) with a greedy algorithm in MD (cutting a{33} molecule, and the
related{343} molecule, whenever possible). See Figure 22 (Section 11.2).
In this algorithm, one can show that the number of {4} molecules is minimized, i.e. # {4}≤ 1; moreover
the greedy algorithm in MD guarantees the abundance of {33} molecules, i.e. # {33}≳ ρ, see Proposition
11.7. This relies on the fact that we never get any degree 2 atom in MD (Lemma 11.8), which crucially uses
that MD is a forest.
Toy model I+. Toy model I+ (Definition 11.9) is similar to toy model I, but only a subset X⊆ MU of
atoms has one bond with MD, and the others have none. Moreover, in toy model I+ we assume the number
of connected components of X satisfies #comp(X)≪ρ.
In this case the algorithm (which is essentially the MAINUD algorithm) is defined in Definition 11.13,
and is similar to Definition 11.4, see Figures 30–31 (Section 11.3). The only difference is that we only apply
the naive “cutting lowest” algorithm inX, and instead apply UP in the rest of MU, to minimize the number
of{4} molecules. In fact this guarantees that # {4}≲ #comp(X), which is negligible compared to the gain
#{33}≳ρ, see Proposition 11.14.
Toy model II. In toy model II (Definition 11.15), we have the same assumptions as toy model I, but
with #comp(X)≳ρ. In this case the algorithm is defined in Definition 11.17. Basically we first remove MD
and cut it using DOWN the dual of UP, and then cut MU using a special variant of UP which is called
the 3COMPUP algorithm, see Figure 34 (Section 11.4).
The idea here is that, after removing MD, the degree 3 atoms in MU are precisely those in X. Then, MU
will satisfy the key property that the degree 3 atoms are separated by degree 4 atoms , i.e. the number of
connected components of degree 3 atoms satisfies # 3comp(MU) = #comp(X)≳ρ. Under this assumption, one
can design a special variant of UP (basically choosing two lowest deg 3 atoms n instead of one), namely the
3COMPUP, to guarantee the abundance of {33} molecules, see Proposition 11.19.
Toy model III. In toy model III (Definition 11.20), we assume there exists≳ρ atoms in MU, each having
two bonds with MD; they are called 2-connections. In this case, we simply apply the DOWN algorithm (dual
of UP in MD), see Definition 11.21 and Figure 38 (Section 11.5). In the process, each 2-connection atom

===== PAGE 22 =====
22 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
n will naturally occur as a {33} molecule, which we then cut it to obtain a good component. Consequently
we get #{33}≳ρ and #{4} = 1, see Proposition 11.22.
Reduction to toy models. With the algorithm constructed for each toy model, it now remains to
reduce general multi-layer molecules M (under the assumptions (i)–(ii)) to one of the toy models. The first
step is to reduce M to a two-layer molecule by a process called layer selection.
Since we have ignored the recollisions within each layer Mℓ′ by assuming each layer is a forest, we know
that the recollisions in M must happen across different layers . These recollisions come from the condition
(4) in Section 2.1.1: this condition implies that the particle lines in r(Mℓ′) are connected both within layer
Mℓ′+1 and within layers M≤ℓ′ :=∪ℓ′′≤ℓ′Mℓ′′, resulting in cycles.
The idea of layer selection is as follows. First choose a layer ℓ1 such that sℓ1−1 =|r(Mℓ1−1)|≳ ρ, and
thatsℓ1−1≫sℓ1; this means that the particle lines in r(Mℓ1−1) are connected to each other (with negligible
exceptions) within layer Mℓ1. Now, choose the highest layerℓ2 <ℓ 1 such that sufficiently many particle lines
in r(Mℓ1−1) are connected to each other within M[ℓ2:ℓ1−1] :=∪ℓ2≤ℓ′≤ℓ1−1Mℓ′. Then, by putting together
layers Mℓ′ (ℓ2 <ℓ′≤ℓ1) into MU (and removing a negligible number of exceptions), and definingMℓ2 := MD,
we obtain a two layer molecule where each of MU and MD is a tree (or forest), and there exist ≳ ρ bonds
between them. For details see Definition 11.23 and Proposition 11.24, and Figure 40 (Section 11.6).
Once we are in the two layer setting, we then consider the dichotomy determined by the quantities #2conn
(the number of 2-connections as defined above) and # comp(X) (the number of connected components of X),
where X is the set of atoms in MU that have exactly one bone with MD. By definition, if # 2conn≳ρ then
M is toy model III. If # 2conn≪ρ, then the 2-connections will be negligible and we may assume# 2conn = 0.
Then, M will be toy model I+ or II, depending on whether # comp(X)≪ρ or #comp(X)≳ρ. This completes
the proof in Section 11.
The full algorithm. Finally we discuss the extra ingredients in the full algorithm in Sections 12–13, in
addition to those in Section 11. These are summarized in Section 11.7 and include the followings:
• The O-atoms: in the general case we need to replace the relevant notions (adjacency, connectedness
etc.) by the ones associated with ov-segments, and modify the cutting algorithms accordingly.
For example, the algorithms UP (Definition 11.10), 2CONNUP (Definition 11.21), 3COMPUP
(Definition 11.17) and MAINUD (Definition 11.13), have their full versions defined in Definitions
12.1, 13.1, 13.6 and 13.12 respectively.
• Layer refinement: this is discussed in Definition 12.8 (Section 12.3), and is needed to treat the possible
recollisions within each layer Mℓ′. We further sub-divide each Mℓ′ into finitely many subsets, such
that each of them is precisely a forest, which recovers assumption (ii). This also leads to an extra
step in the layer selection process, see Definition 12.12.
• The strong an weak resonances: these are exceptional cases where {33} molecules do not gain
powers, see Definitions 12.4 and 12.16. In reality, if this effect becomes significant, we shall perform
alternative cutting algorithms that exploit such degeneracies to gain powers of ε in a different way,
see Propositions 12.5 and 12.17.
• The pre-processing step: this is discussed in Definition 13.10 (Section 13.3). This additional step
is needed to deal with possible fixed ends in MD for the two-layer molecule M, which may cause
trouble to the greedy algorithm in the toy model I+.
For a summary of the ingredients of the proofs in Sections 12–13 and their logical relations, see Figure 41
(Section 11.7).
2.4. The Boltzmann approximation and truncation error. In Sections 2.1–2.3 above, we have focused
on the proof of the EH estimates in (1.21) (see Proposition 6.2). Now it remains to prove the approximation
of fA by the Boltzmann solution, see Proposition 6.1.
By discussions in Section 2.1, we know thatfA(ℓτ) is given by an explicit series expansion of iterated time
integrals that involve only fA((ℓ− 1)τ), therefore this part follows from essentially the same arguments in
the short-time theory. We identify those leading terms that do not contain recollision, and those sub-leading

===== PAGE 23 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 23
terms that contain recollision. We then match each leading term with the corresponding term that occurs
in the Duhamel expansion of f(ℓτ) in terms of f((ℓ− 1)τ); note that the cluster expansion (see (3.16))
actually leads to extra terms in addition to those occurring in Boltzmann Duhamel expansions, but these
extra terms turn out to cancel each other, as shown in Proposition 14.2. As for the sub-leading terms, they
can be proved to be vanishing by applying a very simple special case of the cutting algorithm in Sections
11–13 (see Section 14.3, Point 5), since we only have one single layer and the number of recollisions in each
cluster is bounded by Γ. All these are contained in Section 14.
Finally we discuss the estimate for the error termferr
s caused by the truncated dynamics. This estimate is
stated in Proposition 6.3 and is proved in Section 15. Here again we reduce the estimate to the construction
of a cutting algorithm. However, as we now have many recollisions, one new difficulty arises due to the
possibility that a small group of particles within distance O(ε) of each other producing many recollisions
within timeO(ε). To treat this, we rely on a new ingredient, namely the absolute upper bound on the number
of collisions given the number of particles, which is proved in Burago-Ferleger-Kononenko [15]. Basically, if Γ
is sufficiently large, then these Γ recollisions must involve sufficiently many particles, with each new particle
providing a{33} molecule in a suitable algorithm.
The algorithm in Section 15 is similar to the one in Sections 11–13, and is based on a two-layer molecule
formed by A+ and A where A is (almost) a tree and A+ is arbitrary; see Proposition 15.4 (which actually
involves three layersA+,A andA−, but in reality we only exploit two of them). The main algorithm, called
MAINTRUP, is similar to the MAINUD algorithm in Section 13.4, where each time we cut a lowest
atom in A+, and keep cutting any {33} or{343} structure in A that occur. This will then guarantee a
lower bound for the number of {33} molecules, provided we have a lower bound for the number # +
conn of
bonds connecting an atom in A to an atom in a specific subset X+(A)⊆A+. This last lower bound is then
provided by (the inverse function of) the Burago-Ferleger-Kononenko upper bound in [15], see Proposition
15.2. For more details of the algorithm, see Definition 15.9 and Proposition 15.10.
2.5. Comparison between wave and particle systems. As mentioned in Section 1.2, the proof in this
paper is deeply inspired by that in the wave setting by the first two authors [23]. Here we make a brief
comparison between the two proofs, in terms of their similarities and differences. In the case of wave kinetic
theory, the microscopic system in [23] is given by the nonlinear Schr¨ odinger (NLS) equation on a torus Td
L
of size L, with a nonlinearity of strength α. The lowest-order nontrivial statistical quantity is the second
moment of the Fourier modes E|bu(t,k )|2, where bu(t,k ) is the Fourier transform of the solution u(t,x ) to
the NLS equation, which is to be approximated by the solution of the (homogeneous) wave kinetic equation
(1.19), in the limit L→∞ andα→ 0. This kinetic limit is taken under an appropriate choice of scaling law
betweenL and α, namely α =L−γ, where γ∈ (0, 1) in the homogeneous setting, see [23].
At a conceptual level, the two proofs are very similar. The fundamental difficulty represented by the
divergence of the series expansion on the long time interval [0 ,t fin] is the same, and the same goes for the
need to create an arrow of time where, in both cases, the microscopic system is time-reversible but the
limiting kinetic equation is irreversible. The idea of performing a partial expansion, where one refrains from
expanding the lowest order statistic (the fA here, and the second moments in the wave case) but expands
the cumulants all the way to time t = 0, is also the same. This keeps track of all the relevant collisions or
interactions on the long time interval [0,t fin] that affect the cumulants at time tfin.
Even at a more technical level, there are uncanny similarities between the two proofs. First, both proofs
start with a truncation of the dynamics in order to control the size of the diagrams appearing in the cumulant
expansions. Also, in both proofs, the needed cumulant estimate is reduced to a combinatorial problem
that is resolved through an elaborate algorithm. This combinatorial problem, in both cases, involves the
combinatorial structure of molecules, which are formed by atoms that represent collisions or interactions.
While the algorithms are vastly different in the wave case and particle case, there is an important similarity
in the gain mechanism. As shown in Section 2.3, the basic unit of gain here is the special structure of {33}
molecules; similarly, in the wave case, the basic unit of gain also comes from the {33} molecules and the
associated five-vector counting problem.

===== PAGE 24 =====
24 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
All this being said, the analogy between the particle and wave kinetic theories has its limitations, and
despite the above similarities, each proof carries its own distinct set of challenges. In the wave case, a large
box limit needs to be performed to go from the discrete Fourier space of Td
L to the continuum setting of the
wave kinetic equation (1.19). This links the wave kinetic theory to some deep number theory problems, which
seems to be absent in the particle setting. Also, the Feynman diagram expansion for the NLS equation turns
out to converge only conditionally and not absolutely , and requires highly nontrivial cancellations between
arbitrarily large diagrams, which is another distinctive feature and novelty of [23]. On the other hand, the
cumulant expansion in the particle setting is technically more involved, due to the application of inclusion-
exclusion principle and the resulting notion of overlaps, which does not seem to be have an analog in the
wave case. Also the main algorithm in the particle case seems to be conceptually more complicated than
the corresponding algorithm in the wave case, which may be due to the singular nature of the δ integrals in
IM(QM) compared to the counting problems in [23].
2.6. The rest of this paper. The rest of this paper is organized as follows. Note that, although the notion
of cutting and cutting algorithm is the main novelty and central to the proof, they have to wait until Section
8 (cutting) and Section 11 (algorithm) to appear, due to the necessary preparations in Sections 3–7.
• Part I: Sections 3–5 are devoted the statement and proof of Proposition 3.25.
• In Section 3 we define all the relevant notions (molecules, topological reduction etc.) in Sections
3.1–3.4, and then state Proposition 3.25 in Section 3.5.
• In Section 4 we define the truncated dynamics (Definition 4.3) and its properties, which are
needed in the proof of Proposition 3.25.
• In Section 5 we prove Proposition 3.25.
• Interlude I: Section 6 contains the statement of the main propositions, Propositions 6.1–6.3, of this
paper. We also prove that these propositions imply Theorem 1.
• Part II: Sections 7–9 are devoted to the first half of the proof of Proposition 6.2, where we reduce it
to Proposition 9.7.
• In Section 7 we make the necessary preparations, and reduce the the expression|IN M| occurring
in the upper bound of EH to the local integration IM(QM).
• In Section 8 we define the notion of cutting (Definition 8.3), study its properties, and how the
integralIM(QM) behaves under cutting (Proposition 8.14).
• In Section 9 we state the main result concerning cutting algorithms, i.e. Proposition 9.7, and
prove that it implies Proposition 6.2.
• Interlude II: Section 10 contains a few simple reductions and reiterations of properties of the molecule
M, in preparation of the proof of Proposition 9.7.
• Part III: Sections 11–13 are devoted to the second half of the proof of Proposition 6.2, i.e. the proof
of Proposition 9.7.
• In Section 11 we study the two-layer toy models, namely toy models I, I+, II and III (in Sections
11.2–11.5 respectively). In Section 11.6 we define layer selection in the toy model case, and in
Section 11.7 we sketch the additional ingredients needed in the full algorithm.
• In Section 12 we present the first half of the proof of Proposition 9.7, where we reduce it
to Proposition 12.18. This section contains the UP algorithm (Definition 12.1), strong and
weak degeneracies (Definitions 12.4, 12.16), layer refinement (Section 12.3) and layer selection
(Section 12.4).
• In Section 13 we finish the proof of Proposition 12.18. In Sections 13.1–13.4 we introduce the
algorithms 2CONNUP, 3COMPUP, pre-processing and MAINUD respectively. Finally,
in Section 13.5 we finish the proof of Proposition 12.18 by invoking the dichotomy and reducing
to one of the toy model cases in Section 11.
• The Finale: Sections 14–15 are devoted to the proof of Propositions 6.1 and 6.3.
• Appendices: Appendix A–C contain auxiliary results and tables summarizing important notions.
