3. The cumulant expansion formula
In this section, we will state the main cumulant formula in Proposition 3.25, which has form
fs(ℓτ,zs) =
X
H⊆[s]
(fA(ℓτ))⊗([s]\H)·EH(ℓτ,zH) + (error terms), (3.1)
|EH(ℓτ,zH)|≤
X
[M]∈FΛℓ
|IN M|(zH). (3.2)
Here fA is an approximate solution to the Boltzmann equation, and M is a molecule (Definition 3.1). Also
FΛℓ is a collection of molecules (Definition 3.20) and |IN M| is an integral expression associated with the
molecule M (Definition 3.23), which represents the densities given by trajectories that topologically reduce
to M (Definition 3.8).
The statement of Proposition 3.25 involves many concepts that need to be defined. We define these
notions in Sections 3.1–3.4, which are related to molecules M and topological reductions. Then, in Section
3.5 we define the set FΛℓ and the associated integral |IN M|(zH) in (3.2), and then state Proposition 3.25.
This will be the main goal in the first part of the paper, whose proof will occupy Sections 4–5.
3.1. Molecules. We start with the definition of molecules. As seen in Figure 5 (Section 3.2), molecules are
topological reductions of actual particle trajectories (see Definition 3.8 for precise formulation).
Definition 3.1 (Molecules). We define a (layered) molecule to be a diagram demonstrated by Figure 3.
More rigorously, we define a (layered) molecule M = (M,E,P,L) to be a diagram as follows. For the
correspondence between molecule pictures and physical particle pictures, see Table 3 in Appendix B.
Figure 3. An example of molecules.
(1) Atoms, edges, bonds and ends. M is a set of nodes referred to as atoms, andE is a set of edges
consisting of bonds and ends to be specified below. For convenience, below we will abuse notation
and use M instead ofM to denote the set of atoms in M.
(a) An atom n∈ M is either a C-atom (collision) or an O-atom (overlap), denoted respectively
by◦ and• as in Figure 3. Each atom is assigned with a unique layerℓ[n] which is an integer
in [ℓ : ℓ], where ℓ is the highest layer and ℓ is the lowest layer of M. Define Mℓ′ to be the set
of atoms n∈ M of layer ℓ[n] = ℓ′. The boundaries between layers are indicated by horizontal
lines, as in Figure 3.
(b) Each edge in E is either a bond between two atoms n and n′, or an end emanating from one
atom n, or an empty end that does not involve any atom. Each edge is either top or bottom
when viewed at each involved atom n; a top (resp. bottom) edge is drawn above (resp. below)
the atom, as in Figure 3. Moreover, each end is also either free or fixed, with fixed ends
indicated by a ×, as shown in Figure 3. We understand by convention that empty ends are
always free, and count as both top and bottom.

===== PAGE 26 =====
26 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
(c) For two atoms n and n′, if a bond exists between n and n′ that is bottom at n and top at n′, we
say n is parent of n′ and n′ is child of n.
(2) Particle lines. P is a collection of paths: each path starts from a bottom end at an atom, then keeps
connecting to a parent by a bond, until reaching a top end at another atom. Each chain p∈P is
called a particle line. We require that each edge belongs to a unique particle line; in particular,
each empty end is also viewed as a particle line by convention. For a top edge and a bottom edge at
an atom n, we say they are serial if they belong to the same particle line. Note that each particle line
p contains a unique bottom end and a unique top end. We will identify particle lines with physical
particles, see Table 3.
Recall ℓ defined in (1a). We may define a start layer ℓ1[p]∈ [ℓ :ℓ] and finish layer ℓ2[p]∈ [ℓ :
ℓ + 1] for each particle line p. As p contains a unique bottom (and unique top) end, we may also
equivalently writeℓ1[e] (resp. ℓ2[e]) for each bottom (resp. top) end e instead of ℓ1[p] and ℓ2[p]. We
refer to the interval [ℓ1[p] : ℓ2[p]] as the lifespan of the particle line p. In Figure 3, the lifespan of
p1 andp2 are [1 : 3] and [1 : 2], respectively. For p1, its finish layer is ℓ2[p1] = ℓ + 1 = 3, which is
above the highest layer ℓ. To indicate this, we draw it penetrating the top horizontal line.
(3) Initial links. Finally,L is a collection of pairs of bottom ends (e,e′) withℓ1[e] =ℓ1[e′] = 1 (provided
ℓ = 1). Since each particle line has a unique bottom end by (2), we can equivalently view L as a
collection of pairs of particle lines with start layer 1. Each pair in L is called an initial link. In
Figure 3,L contains a single pair (e4,e 9), which is indicated by a dashed line connecting them.
(4) Ov-segments. For later use, we also define an ov-segment to be a path between two atoms (or ends)
such that all intermediate atoms in this path are O-atoms (except the possible endpoints), and this
path is contained in a single particle line. We say an ov-segment is maximal if it is not contained
in other ov-segments. In Figure 3, the path consisting of edges e1 and e2, and atoms n1 and n3,
is an example of a maximal ov-segment. Note that each edge in M belongs to a unique maximal
ov-segment. Also each bond in M is a special case of ov-segment (not necessarily maximal).
(5) The objects (M,E,P,L) satisfies the following requirements.
(a) Each atom has exactly 2 top edges and exactly 2 bottom edges.
(b) Starting from any atom n, it is impossible to get back to n by iteratively taking parent or by
iteratively taking child.
(c) If n is a parent of n′ then we must have ℓ[n]≥ ℓ[n′]. If n belongs to a particle line p then we
must haveℓ1[p]≤ℓ[n]≤ℓ2[p].
Definition 3.2 (Full molecules). If a molecule M has no fixed end, we say M is a full molecule.
In the statement of the cumulant formula, we will need to label different particles to distinguish them.
Also the particles corresponding to zs or zH in fs(ℓτ,zs) or cumulant EH(ℓτ,zH), i.e. the root particles,
will play a special role.
Definition 3.3 (Label and root particle lines) . Given a molecule M, we may assign labels (which takes
values in N) to each particle line p of M. This makes M a labeled molecule . Note that two labeled
molecules with the same molecule structure (Definition 3.1) but different labelings are regarded as different.
For any molecule M, define the set of all particle lines in M to be p(M). Define a particle line p to be
a root particle line , if its finish layer ℓ2[p] = ℓ + 1, where ℓ is the highest layer in M. Define the set of
root particle lines to be r(M), and the set of non-root particles to be ( p\r)(M) :=p(M)\r(M).
For two labeled molecules M and M′ with ℓ layers, we define them to be equivalent, denoted M∼ M′,
if they have the same molecule structure (Definition 3.1) and the same labels for root particle lines. Define
[M] to be the equivalence class of M, which is obtained by relabeling non-root particle lines in M.
In Figure 3, we can label p1,p2 andp3 by 1, 2 and 3 respectively, where p1 is the root particle line.
Remark 3.4. Clearly an equivalence class [ M] is uniquely determined by an unlabeled molecule M, and a
fixed labeling of all root particle lines. In the proof of the cumulant expansion formula (Proposition 3.25) in
Sections 4–5, it is important that we use M to represent labeled molecules and sum over equivalence classes

===== PAGE 27 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 27
[M] (see relevant notations in Definition 1.6 (6)). Once this proof is finished, in subsequent analysis of the
|IN M| terms, the labeling will not play any role, so we will ignore it and use M to denote unlabeled molecules
in Sections 6–15 (we still need to label the root particles, which will not play a major role, see Remark 3.26).
We introduce some additional notions associated with molecules, which will be used in the proof below.
Definition 3.5 (Ordering of atoms). Let M be a molecule and n, n′∈ M be atoms.
(1) Descendants and ancestors. If n is obtained from n′ by iteratively taking parents, then we say n is an
ancestor of n′, and n′ is a descendant of n. We use Sn (resp. Zn) to denote the set of descendants
(resp. ancestors) of n. By convention we understand that n∈Sn (and n∈Zn).
(2) The partial ordering of atoms and edges. We also define a partial ordering between all atoms, by
defining n′≺ n if and only if n′̸= n and is a descendant of n (equivalently n is ancestor of n′). In
particular n′≺ n if n′ is a child of n (equivalently n is parent of n′).
(3) Given any subset A⊆ M, we can define a lowest atom in A to be any atom n∈ A that satisfies
n′̸∈A for each n′≺ n (i.e. n′̸= n is descendant of n). Define highest atoms in the same way. These
are just minimal and maximal elements of A under the partial ordering≺, and note that they need
not be unique for general A.
Definition 3.6 (Various sets associated with molecules) . Let M = (M,E,P,L) be a molecule.
(1) SetsE∗ andEend. DefineE∗ to be the set of all edges that are either bonds or free ends (i.e. not
counting fixed ends). Define also the sets Eend (resp. E−
end,E+
end) to be the set of all ends (resp.
bottom ends, top ends). Note that empty ends belong to E±
end by convention.
Note that the setsE−
end andE+
end are in bijection with the setp(M) of all particle lines, see Definition
3.1 (2).
(2) Number of atoms and particle lines. We will use |M|,|M|O,|M|p,|M|r and|M|p\r to denote the
number of atoms, O-atoms, particle lines (i.e. |p(M)|), root particle lines (i.e. |r(M)|), and non-root
particle lines (i.e. |(p\r)(M)|) of M respectively.
3.2. Topological reduction. In this section, we rigorously formulate the notion of topological reduction.
We start with the notion of admissible trajectories, which generally occur from modifications of hard sphere
dynamics where certain collisions are neglected according to certain rules (see Definition 3.15).
Definition 3.7 (Admissible trajectories) . Letz(t) = zN(t) = (zj(t))j be any particle trajectory (a priori
with no equation or restriction). We say it is admissible (see Figure 4), if
Figure 4. Admissible trajectories (Definition 3.7), where it is allowed to have|xi−xj|<ε ,
but two particles reaching distance ε from “inside” will not collide and not change velocity,
as they do not satisfy the pre-collisional condition.
(1) Each zj(t) = (xj(t),vj(t)) is piecewise linear, xj(t) is continuous and vj(t) is left continuous, with a
discrete set of times of velocity discontinuity referred to as collisions;
(2) At each collision time t, there exist two and only two particles whose velocities are discontinuous,
their positions (xi,xj) and velocities ( vi,vj) satisfy (1.2) with xi(t)−xj(t) = εω (ω∈ Sd−1), and
(vi(t)−vj(t))·ω≤ 0. We refer to this set of conditions as the pre-collisional condition.
(3) Additionally, we define an overlap to be a time when xi(t)−xj(t) = εω (ω∈ Sd−1) and (vi(t)−
vj(t))·ω≤ 0, but the velocities vi and vj are continuous at time t.

===== PAGE 28 =====
28 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
Definition 3.8 (Topological reduction). Let z(t) be an admissible trajectory for t∈ [0,ℓτ ], and M be a
molecule with ℓ layers (Definition 3.1). We say z(t) topologically reduces to M, if there is a bijection
between maximal ov-segments of M (Definition 3.1 (4)) and maximal linear pieces of the trajectory z(t), see
Figure 5, which satisfies the following requirements:
(1) If two maximal ov-segments intersect at a C-atom, then the corresponding linear pieces of z(t) have
a collision. If they intersect at an O-atom, then the corresponding linear pieces have an overlap.
(2) The induced correspondence between atoms and collisions/overlaps preserves order (i.e. if n is parent
of n′ then the corresponding collision/overlap happens later in time), and is bijective on collisions
(it is injective on overlaps by time ordering, but might not be surjective).
(3) If two maximal ov-segments belong to the same particle line in M, then the corresponding linear
pieces belong to the trajectory of the same particle.
(4) The collision/overlap corresponding to an atom in layerℓ must occur in the time interval [(ℓ−1)τ,ℓτ ].
For a summary of correspondence of concepts in molecules and physical trajectories, see Table 3. In Figure
5, the overlap in [τ, 2τ] of the red and green particles corresponds to the O-atom on layer 2, and the collision
in [0,τ ] of the red and green particles corresponds to the C-atom on layer 1.
Figure 5. An illustration of topological reduction.
Remark 3.9. The topological reduction M of a given trajectory z(t) may not be unique, since we do not
require a bijective correspondence between O-atoms and the actual set of overlaps. The O-atoms may
represent only an arbitrary subset of all overlaps. However there is always a unique topological reduction
that is C-molecule (i.e. contains only C-atoms).
3.3. Clusters. As seen in Definition 3.8, O-atoms correspond to overlaps that do not affect the dynamics.
Ignoring all such overlaps leads to the definition of clusters.
Definition 3.10 (Create and delete an O-atom) . Given a molecule M, we define the following operations,
see Figure 6. The particle lines and ov-segments will not be broken after these operations.
(1) Delete an O-atom. Let o be an O-atom with 4 edges ei (1≤ i≤ 4) connected to other atoms ni,
wheree1 ande3 are serial and same for e2 ande4. By deleting the O-atom o, we remove o and the
4 edges ej from M, and add two bonds e′
1 between n1 and n3, and e′
2 between n2 and n4, with the
same top/bottom assignment as (e1,e 3) and (e2,e 4). Here if some ni is absent, say e3 is a free end
at o, then the bond (e1,e 3) should be replaced by a free end at n1, etc.
(2) Create an O-atom. Given two edgese′
1 between n1 and n3 ande′
2 between n2 and n4, we can create
an O-atom o by reverting the above process: add a new O-atom o, removee′
1 ande′
2, add the bonds
ej between o and nj with the same top/bottom assignment as e′
1 ande′
2, and make e1 serial with e3
and e2 serial with e4. We also refer to this as joining e′
1 and e′
2 by o.

===== PAGE 29 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 29
Figure 6. Creating and deleting O-atoms as in Definition 3.10. Note that each atom nj
can be either C- or O-atoms, or absent.
Definition 3.11 (Clusters). Given a molecule M, we obtain a new molecule M′ by deleting all O-atoms
from M as in Definition 3.10, leaving only C-atoms. The connected components ( M1,..., Mk) of M′ are
called the clusters of M. We say that an O-atom o joins Mj and Mj′ if the two particle lines at o connects
the C-atoms in Mj and in Mj′ respectively. We also define the auxiliary cluster graph to be the graph
with nodes being clusters Mj, and an edge is drawn between Mj and Mj′ for each O-atom joining them. See
Figure 7 for an illustration.
Figure 7. An example of clusters and O-atoms joining them as in Definition 3.11, and the
corresponding cluster graph. Here we have 3 clusters Mj, with (M1, M2) and (M1, M3) each
joined by an O-atom.
Definition 3.12 (C-molecule). A molecule consists of only C-atoms are referred to as C-molecules. Clus-
ters are always C-molecules.
Definition 3.13 (Recollision number). Given a subset A⊆ M, we define its recollision number by ρ(A) =
#bond(A)−|A| + #comp(A), where # bond(A) and # comp(A) are respectively the number of bonds between
atoms inA, and the number of connected components of A. We say a set A is recollisional (or equivalently
cyclic) if ρ(A)> 0.
Remark 3.14. Recollision number is an important notion and will be used frequently later. It equals the
circuit rank which is the number of (independent) cycles in A. It is easy to see that ρ(A)≥ 0 for any A.
3.4. Molecule prescribed dynamics. In Proposition 3.25, the quantity IN M basically represents an in-
tegral over the non-root particle variables, under the restriction that the particle trajectories under certain
dynamics topologically reduces to M. In order to exclude the possible effects of other collisions, we need to
introduce the molecule prescribed dynamics (Definition 3.17), which ignores any possible collision that does
not come from the molecule. This is an example of general modified dynamics, which we introduce below.
Definition 3.15 (Modified dynamics). We define the modified dynamics, which is governed by a given
property Pro, as follows.

===== PAGE 30 =====
30 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
(1) Governing property. Pro is a property, which can be expressed in terms of an admissible trajectory
γ and two particlesp,p′. This means that, given any admissible trajectory γ and particlesp,p′, we
can uniquely determine whether Pro(γ,p,p′) equals True or False.
(2) Modified dynamics. Given initial configurationz0 =z0
N, we define the dynamicszPro(t) =zPro(t,z0)
governed by Pro, as follows:
(a) We have zPro(0) =z0.
(b) Suppose zPro(t′) is known for t′∈ [0,t ]. Let γ be the admissible trajectory zPro(t′) (t′∈ [0,t ]).
If two particles satisfy the pre-collisional condition (Definition 3.7 (2)) at time t, and also
Pro(γ,p,p′) = True for the molecule M′ and the particle lines p,p′ corresponding to these two
particles, then they collide as in (1.2).
(c) If a particle does not satisfy (2b) with any other particle, then it does not have any collision
and moves linearly by (1.3).
(3) Flow map and flow operator. We define the flow mapHPro(t) =HPro
N (t) by
HPro(t) : R2dN→ R2dN, HPro(t)(z0) =zPro(t) (3.3)
wherezPro(t) =zPro

still define the flow operatorS Pro(t) by

(4) Collision and overlap. The notions of collisions and overlaps for any trajectory of modified dy-
namics, are defined as in Definition 3.7.
(5) Label of particles. In this definition, we assume that particles are labeled by [ N] ={1,··· ,N} (see
the convention in Definition 1.6 (4)). For a general labeling set λ, the formulation remains the same,
except that the subscript N in expressions like zN(t),HN(t),SN(t) etc. is replaced by λ.
Remark 3.16. We make a few remarks regarding Definition 3.15. First, up to Lebesgue zero sets, the modified
dynamics is well-defined just as Definition 1.1, and produces admissible trajectories in Definition 3.7. Also,
we allow|xi−xj|<ε and do not restrict to DN, and only allow collisions under pre-collisional condition.
Second, the modified dynamics can be described as “collision ⇔ pre-collisional and property Pro holds”,
in particular the dynamics is completely determined by the condition Pro. Examples of this Pro include the
molecule prescribed dynamics (Definition 3.17), extended dynamics (Definition 4.1) and truncated dynamics
(Definition 4.3).
Definition 3.17 (Molecule prescribed dynamics). Given a C-molecule M, the dynamics prescribed by M
is a modified dynamics as in Definition 3.15, with particle set being p(M), and governing property given by
Pro(γ,p,p′) = True⇔ M′⊆ M, and p andp′ intersects at a C-atom n∈ M\M′, and Sn\{n}⊆ M′.
Here M′ is the unique C-molecule which is topological reduction 6 ofγ as in Definition 3.8 (the uniqueness
is guaranteed by Remark 3.9), Sn is the set of descendants of n (Definition 3.5), and p andp′ are viewed as
particle lines in M. See Figure 8.
If M contains O-atoms, the the dynamics prescribed by M is defined to be the dynamics prescribed by
eM, which is the C-molecule formed by deleting all the O-atoms in M.

of particles and crossings in each layer Mℓ′.

• p1 is connected to a cycle within A, if p1 intersects a component of A that contains a cycle;
• p1 forms an initial link within A, if there exists a particle line p′
1 which either equals p1 or is
connected to p1 via A, such that the bottom end in p′

Remark 3.21. We briefly explain the ideas behind the items in Definition 3.20. Here (1a) comes from the
truncation of dynamics (Definition 4.3) and the truncation in the Penrose argument, and (1c) also comes
from the construction in the Penrose argument. Next, (1b) follows from the fact that we only expand the
particles that are connected to root particles, and (1d) follows from the condition for the cumulant EH. The
other conditions (2)–(4) are similar but with modifications associated with single layer or error terms.

Remark 3.24. We make a few remarks on Definitions 3.22–3.23.
(1) The time (ℓ−ℓ + 1)τ is because M hasℓ−ℓ + 1 layers, and each layer corresponding to evolution of
time τ. Note that, in the support of 1 M, the M-prescribed dynamics contains exactly the collisions
in M. Thus (S◦ 1 )M is given by a fixed composition of explicitly defined changes of variables of form
(1.2).
(2) In (3.11)–(3.12) we have two sets of variables, namely zp andz′

dynamics. In particular they are related by the explicit change of variables in ( S◦ 1 )M as in (1)
(under the support of 1 M contained in (S◦ 1 )M).
(3) The integralsIN M and|IN M| in (3.11)–(3.12) represent the probability density under the assumption
that all collisions and overlaps in M (and initial links) occur as expected. This is clear from the
transport operator and indicator function in S◦ 1 as described above. The integral in all non-root
particlesz(p\r)(M) leaves the density as a function of the root particles zr(M).
Finally, the input function Q in (3.13) contains the initial link/initial cumulant factor 1 ε

contains the fA factors (approximate Boltzmann solution) for each particle p, at time (ℓ1[p]− 1)τ
corresponding to the start layer ℓ1[p]. This is the first time p becomes relevant in the dynamics
prescribed by M. Note the linear transport x′

Remark 3.26. The quantity efs is defined by the truncated dynamics, see Section 4. Note that the equation
(3.16) defines a recurrence relation forfA, because bothIN M and|IN M| are expressed as integrals involving
products of fA. These expressions are also constant on equivalence classes [ M].
The summation (3.17) (and similarly (3.18) and (3.20)) involves the condition r(M) = H. Fixing the
labels of these root particles may lead to an additional |H|! factor, which is usually harmless (but requires
some extra discussion in Section 15). Apart from this, the equivalence classes [ M] of labeled molecules M
are then the same as unlabeled molecules, see Remark 3.4.
4. Truncation of dynamics
We now start the proof of Proposition 3.25. In this section we will define the truncated dynamics and
establish the decomposition (3.14). This is needed in order to control the number of particles and recollisions
in each cluster in each time layer, that occur in the molecule expansion (for example in Definition 3.20 (1a),
the number of particles in layer ℓ′ is bounded by Λ 2
ℓ′Aℓ′).

4.1. The extended and truncated dynamics. In this subsection, we define the extended and truncated
dynamics as two instances of modified dynamics in Definition 3.15.
Definition 4.1 (The extended dynamics). We define the extended dynamics (or E-dynamics), denoted
byzE(t), to be the modified dynamics in Definition 3.15, with the governing property Pro(γ,p,p′)≡ True.
Remark 4.2. In the E-dynamics, we have a collision whenever the pre-collisional condition is satisfied. In
particular, if the distance of any two particles becomes ≥ε, then this separation is preserved thereafter.
The E-dynamics is a natural extension of the original dynamics (Definition 1.1) from DN to the whole
space R2dN. If we restrict the initial data of the E-dynamics to DN, then the trajectory stays inDN forever,
and the E-dynamics coincides with the O-dynamics (see Proposition 4.5 below).
Definition 4.3 (The truncated dynamics) . Let (Λ, Γ) be fixed. We define the truncated dynamics (or
(Λ, Γ)-, or T-dynamics), denoted by zΛ,Γ(t), to be the modified dynamics in Definition 3.15, with the
governing property Pro(γ,p,p′) given by
Pro(γ,p,p′) = True⇔ either (Mi = Mj and ρ(Mi)≤ Γ− 1),
or (Mi̸= Mj and|Mi| +|Mj|≤ Λ and ρ(Mi) +ρ(Mj)≤ Γ), (4.2)
where Mi (resp. Mj) is the connected components of M in which the particle line p (resp. p′) occurs, and
M is the unique C-molecule which is topological reduction of γ.
Remark 4.4. In the (Λ, Γ)-truncated dynamics, we force the property that each cluster has size ≤ Λ and
recollision number≤ Γ (Proposition 4.5 (5)), by neglecting any collisions that may violate this property.
Proposition 4.5. The E- and (Λ, Γ)-dynamics (Definitions 4.1 and 4.3) satisfy the following properties.
(1) Up to some Lebesgue zero set ofz0, the E- and T- dynamics in Definitions 4.1 and 4.3 are well-defined
and produces admissible trajectories in Definition 3.7.
(2) Define (HE,SE) and (HΛ,Γ,SΛ,Γ) as special cases of (3.3) and (3.4). Then, when the initial data
z0∈D N, the E-dynamics coincides with the O-dynamics in Definition 1.1, i.e. zE(t) = z(t) and
HE
N(t)(z0) =HN(t)(z0). Moreover, we haveSE

(3) The HE

5. Proof of the cumulant formula
We already have (3.14), withefs andferr
s defined in (4.17) and (4.18) respectively. To complete Proposition
3.25, in this section, we prove the upper bound forferr
s (tfin,zs) in (3.20), and the cumulant expansion formula
(3.15) for efs(ℓτ), with bounds (3.16)–(3.19). This is done in several steps.
Recall that the definition offWN and efs involves the transport operatorSΛℓ′,Γ
N (τ) defined by the truncated
dynamics. In Sections 5.1–5.2 we will prove a crucial decomposition formula for this operator, namely
Proposition 5.10. Then in Sections 5.3–5.4, we will use this decomposition to prove Proposition 5.16. This
is the key inductive step, which establishes the cumulant decomposition (3.15) at time ℓτ provided the same
expansion holds at time (ℓ−1)τ, and expresses the cumulantEH(ℓτ) in terms ofEH((ℓ−1)τ) and a molecule
Mℓ with single layer ℓ. Then in Sections 5.5–5.6, by induction in ℓ using Proposition 5.16, and using the
initial cumulant formula (time t = 0, see Proposition 5.18), we complete the proof of Proposition 3.25.
5.1. Cluster expansion. In this section, we perform the cluster expansion for the (Λ, Γ)-truncated dynam-
ics and prove Lemma 5.5 by introducing the C-molecules, which allow us to write the transport operator

(3) Composition of partial dynamics. Given disjoint subsets (λ1,··· ,λk), the composition of the partial

Remark 5.2. Note that in general zλ(t) ̸= (zN(t))λ, where the left hand side is defined by the partial
dynamics, and the right hand side is defined by selecting the particles in λ from the full dynamics. This is
due to the possible collisions between particles in λ and λc.
Note also that, in the composition of partial dynamics of ( λ1,...,λ k), collisions occur only between two
particles in the same subset λj; any pre-collisional configuration between two particles in different subsets
λj are always ignored.
Definition 5.3 (Interaction of partial dynamics) . Let M and M′ be C-molecules with only one layer ℓ,
and particle sets p(M) and p(M′) respectively. Let zΛ,Γ(t) be the truncated dynamics on [( ℓ− 1)τ,ℓτ ] and

(2) The indicator functions 1 M̸∼M′. We write M̸∼ M′ if and only if in the trajectory zΛ,Γ
p(M)∪p(M′)(t),
there is no collisions between any atom in p(M) and any atom in p(M′). Define the indicator
function 1 M̸∼M′(z0) to be 1 if M̸∼ M′, and 0 otherwise.
(3) The indicator functions 1 (M,e)̸∼(M′,e′). Given two molecules ( M, M′) and two edges e ∈ M and
e′∈ M′, we write ( M,e )̸∼ (M′,e′) if any of the following two situations occur.
(a) No overlap: under the composition dynamicsHΛ,Γ
p(M)◦H Λ,Γ

tory corresponding to e and e′ never enter a pre-collisional configuration at any time t∗.

p(M)◦
HΛ,Γ
p(M′) at timet∗, but Pro(γ,p,p′) = False. Here p,p′ are the particles containing edgese and

p(M)◦H Λ,Γ
p(M′) dynamics up to time t∗, and Pro is the
condition in the (Λ, Γ)-truncated dynamics (Definition 4.3) with particle set p(M)∪p(M′).
We define the indicator function 1 (M,e)̸∼(M′,e′)(z0) to be 1 if ( M,e )̸∼ (M′,e′), and 0 otherwise.
Note that these objects are well-defined only when the trajectory of particles in p(M) and p(M′)
topologically reduce to M and M′.
Remark 5.4. We explain the idea behind Definition 5.3 (3). The statement ( M,e )̸∼ (M′,e′) here means
that, either the edges e and e′ do not overlap, or their overlap does not cause a collision in theHΛ,Γ
p(M)∪p(M′)
dynamics due to the (Λ, Γ)-truncation. We have to define it in this way (instead of just saying e and e′ do
not overlap), in order to ensure that the equality (5.5) is true. Note that ( M,e )̸∼ (M′,e′) is less restrictive
than saying e and e′ do not overlap, therefore the opposite statement ( M,e )∼ (M′,e′) is more restrictive
than saying e and e′ do overlap. This implies an upper bound for the corresponding indicator function
1 (M,e)∼(M′,e′), which is all we need in all subsequent proofs. We emphasize that below it is always safe to
treat (M,e )̸∼ (M′,e′) in the same way as the natural non-overlapping condition.

factorizes the transport operator SΛ,Γ

be treated in Section 5.2 using the Penrose argument.

the C-molecule topological reduction of zΛ,Γ(t) andzΛ,Γ

λ (t) at time t∗. By Definition
4.3 of the (Λ, Γ)-dynamics, we know that, whether this collision is allowed is determined only by properties
of the molecule Mλ, in both cases. As such, the two dynamics must follow the same scattering rule at t∗.
Moreover, up to a zero measure, no other collisions occur for t > t∗ sufficiently close to t∗. Therefore, the
two dynamics remain identical for such t, contradicting the maximality of t∗. This contradiction shows that
we must have t∗ =τ, as desired.

Proof of Lemma 5.5. All the molecules in this proof are C-molecules. First, the decomposition (1) is obvious,
because every trajectory topologically reduces to a unique C-molecule (Remark 3.9). The property that
|Mj|p≤ Λ and ρ(Mj)≤ Γ follows from the (Λ, Γ)-truncation. Second, the equalities (5.3)–(5.4) in (2) follow
directly from Lemma 5.6 in view of the various indicator functions defined in Definition 5.3. As (4) follows
from simply putting together (1)–(3), now we only need to prove (3).
To prove (3), it suffices to show that
M̸∼ M′⇔ (M,e )̸∼ (M′,e′) for all e∈ M and e′∈ M′. (5.9)
In fact, suppose the̸∼ is replaced by∼ on either side of (5.9), then there exists a pre-collisional configuration
between a particle p∈ M and a particle p′∈ M′, either in the composition dynamics HΛ,Γ
p(M)◦H Λ,Γ
p(M′) or in
the full dynamics HΛ,Γ
p(M)∪p(M′). Consider the first time t∗ when such a pre-collisional configuration occurs,
then the two trajectories must coincide before t∗, call it γ. Now, if M∼ M′ due to a collision in the full
dynamics, then we must have Pro(γ,p,p′) = True by in Definition 4.3, hence ( M,e )∼ (M′,e′) for some
edges e and e′ in the particle lines p andp′ by Definition 5.3 (3). Conversely, if ( M,e )∼ (M′,e′), then we
also have Pro(γ,p,p′) = True by Definition 5.3 (3), hence M∼ M′ by Definition 4.3. □

Proof part 4: construction of π. In this part, we define the map π and prove properties (2)–(3)
in Part 2. The idea is similar to Proposition 2.3.3 in [8], but the arguments are more complicated due to
truncation.
Before defining π, we first make some preparations. Recall E is the set of edge pairs ( e,e′); given a
labeling of particle lines, we can define a linear ordering on the set E as follows: suppose o = (e,e′)∈ E,
wheree ande′ belong to particle lines p andp′ respectively, andp<p′ (in terms of labels). Then the linear
ordering is defined by first comparing p (in terms of labels), then comparing p′, and then comparing the
order in the same particle line (descendant < ancestor). Clearly this linear ordering is invariant if we relabel
the non-root particles, but keeping the order of particle labels within each component of M.
Now let G∈ 2E be fixed. In the definition below, we will consider the graph MG defined in Part 1 (by
creating an O-atom at each o = (e,e′)∈ G) and its cluster graph Cl G. For each root particle line r∈ [s],
let Qr be the (root) cluster containing r, and MG(r) be the component of MG containing r (and Qr), and
ClG(r) be the corresponding component of Cl G. For each cluster Q∈ ClG(r), consider the graph theoretic
distance between Q and Qr (i.e. the length of shortest path connecting them), and define Cl q
G(r) to be the
set of Q∈ ClG(r) with the above distance being q≥ 0.
Algorithm defining π. For each root particle r∈ [s], let MG(r) and Cl G(r) be defined above. We
distinguish two cases:
Case 1: if |ClG(r)|< Λ for each r. We perform the following steps:
(1) Start from the first root particle r = 1, and work in the graph Cl G(1) (note that this graph may
contain multiple edges, but this does not affect the arguments below).
(2) Consider all the elements o = (e,e′)∈ G (i.e. O-atoms in MG) that join a cluster in Cl q
G(1) and a
cluster in Clq+1
G (1) for some q≥ 0. We order them first in the increasing order in q, and then in the
increasing order in o (following the linear order defined above).
(3) Start with the set S :={Q1}. Each time, select the smallest o = (e,e′) in (2) that still remains in
MG(1). Let K be the cluster in Clq+1
G (1) that is joined by o to a cluster in Clq
G(1), we add{K} to the
set S, and delete all other elements of G (i.e. O-atoms) that join K to another cluster in S. Repeat
untilS exhausts all clusters in ClG(1), then stop working in this component.
(4) Then, select the next smallest root particle r that does not belong to any previously operated
component, and repeat (1)–(3) until all root clusters have been accounted for. Finally, delete all
O-atoms that do not belong to any MG(r). Define the result to be F =π(G).
Case 2: if |ClG(r)|≥ Λ for some r. We perform the same steps as in Case 1, except at the first time when
|ClG(r)|≥ Λ. At this time we perform (1)–(3) as in Case 1 , but terminate early when |S| = Λ (without
adding the next atom to S). Then we delete all the o that still remains in MG(r) and do not join two
clusters in S. After this, stop exploring (even if there are still root clusters) and delete all O-atoms that do
not belong to any previously operated component. Define the result to be F =π(G).
It is easy to verify the following properties of π(G) =F ; in particular, they imply that F∈ F∪ F err by
definitions in Part 1.
• The cluster graph ClF is a forest. This is because in our construction (3), each new cluster in S has
only one overlap (after deletion) joining it to earlier clusters in S.
• If|ClG(r)|< Λ, then ClF (r) = ClG(r) and Clq
F (r) = Clq
G(r). This is because in our construction (3),
the set S remains connected in the cluster graph, and each cluster in S∩ Clq
G(r) is always connected
to Qr by a path of length q even after the deletions.
• In Case 2, ClF has a unique component Cl F (r0) that contains Λ clusters; each component Cl F (r′)
that is operated before Cl F (r0) contains < Λ clusters, and each component other than Cl F (r0) and
ClF (r′) contains only one cluster. This is clear from the construction in Case 2.
Definition of FB (F ). Now we define the set FB (F ) as follows.
Case 1: when |ClF (r)|< Λ for each r. We define o∈FB (F ) if and only if
(1) o joins two clusters in two different components Cl F (r);

===== PAGE 47 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 47
(2) o joins a cluster in Clq
F (r) and a cluster in Cl q′
F (r) with|q−q′|≥ 2; or
(3) o̸∈F joins a cluster Q∈ Clq
F (r) and a cluster K∈ Clq+1
F (r), and the unique o′∈F joining K to a
cluster in Clq
F (r) satisfies o< o′ in the linear ordering; or
(4) o joins a cluster in ClF (r) and a cluster in Cl F not in any ClF (r′).
Case 2: when |ClF (r0)| = Λ. We define the components Cl F (r0) and previously operated components
ClF (r′) as above, and let their union be Cl F (≤ r0). Consider also the last cluster K reaching|S| = Λ in
Case 2 of the algorithm; assume it belongs to Cl q0
F (r0) and the associated smallest o is o0. Then, we define
o∈FB (F ) if and only if
(5) o satisfies one of (1)–(3) holds within Cl F (≤r0); or
(6) o joins one cluster K outside Cl≤r0 and another cluster Q, which either belongs to Cl F (r′) for some
r′ as above, or belongs to Cl q
F (r0) for some q <q0− 1, or belongs to Clq0−1
F (r0) and o< o0.
Proof of (2)–(3) in Part 2. Now, with the mapping π and the set FB (F ) defined as above, we can
prove the properties (2)–(3) in Part 2.
The main point is to show (5.20). Recall the properties (1)–(6) in the definition of FB (F ) above. We
first show that if π(G) = F then FB (F )∩G = ∅ (as F ⊆ G is obvious). Assume o∈ FB (F )∩G for
contradiction, and consider each of the cases (1)–(6).
(1) If o joins a clusters in Cl F (r) and a cluster in Cl F (r′)̸= ClF (r), then we have Cl G(r) = Cl G(r′)
because of o, but this is contradiction because Cl F (r) = ClG(r) and ClF (r′) = ClG(r′).
(2) If o joins a clusters in Clq
F (r) and a cluster in Clq′
F (r) withq′≥q +2, then this latter cluster belongs to
Clq+1
G (r) or lower because of o, but this is contradiction because Clq+1
G (r) = Clq+1
F (r) is disjoint with Clq′
F (r).
(3) If o joins Q∈ Clq
F (r) and K∈ Clq+1
F (r), and satisfies o < o′ where o′∈ F , then consider the time
when we add {K} into S. By the construction in (3) of the algorithm, we would select o and delete o′, so
the result will not be F , contradiction.
(4) If o joins one cluster in Cl F (r) to a cluster K not in any ClF (r′), then K∈ ClG(r) because of o, but
this implies K∈ ClF (r), contradiction.
(5) This case is basically the same as (1)–(3) but restricted to Cl F (r0) and ClF (r′).
(6) Assume o joins K̸∈ ClF (≤ r0) and Q as specified. If Q∈ ClF (r′) for some r′ in the definition of
FB (F ) above, then we have K∈ ClG(r′) = ClF (r′) due to o, which is a contradiction. If Q∈ Clq
F (r0) for
q <q0− 1, then similarly K∈ Clq+1
G (r0)⊆ ClF (r0) (the subset relation is because q + 1<q 0, so all clusters
in Clq+1
G (r0) must have been exhausted in the construction in (3) of the algorithm before reaching |S| = Λ),
which is also contradiction. Finally if Q∈ Clq0−1
F (r0) and o< o0, then again the in construction we would
select o at some point before selecting o0, so the result will not be F , contradiction.
We next show that if F⊆G and FB (F )∩G = ∅, then we must have π(G) =F . This is basically done
by reverting the proof in cases (1)–(6) above, which we carry out below.
Assume first that |ClF (r)| < Λ for each r. Then, using the absence of elements in (1) and (4), we
deduce that ClF (r) = ClG(r) for each r; using the absence of elements in (2), we can inductively show that
Clq
F (r) = Clq
G(r) for each r and q. Finally, using the assumptions for (3), we see that the choice of edges in
the construction of π(G) exactly leads to those edges already in F .
The case when some |Fr0| = Λ is similar. The same arguments above implies that Cl F (r′) = ClG(r′) for
thoser′ in the definition of FB (F ) above, and Clq
F (r0) = Clq
G(r0) for q <q0, and the result up to this point
coincides with F . Then, when we execute (3) of the algorithm and each time choosing a smallest remaining
o, the absence of elements in (6) guarantees that we cannot add anything outside Cl F (≤r0) into S. So the
result coincides with F , as desired.
Finally, we prove the properties of AL(F ) in (3) in Part 2. In fact, the first two properties (5.21)–(5.22)
follow immediately from the definition of FB (F ) (and thus AL(F )) above. The last property concerning
relabeling is also obvious, because the set AL(F ) is constructed using the linear ordering for o, which is
invariant under the said relabelings. □

Now, using (5.34) and note thats =|M|r, we see that the main term in (5.39) exactly matches that in (5.33).
Since the error term (Err) can be treated in the same way, this completes the proof of Proposition 5.12. □
5.4. Recurrence formula for cumulants. In this subsection, starting from (5.33) in Proposition 5.12, we
shall insert the ansatz (3.15) for efs((ℓ− 1)τ) and subsequently derive the same expansion for efs(ℓτ). This
also includes a recurrence formula for both fA andEH in (3.15), see Proposition 5.16. This is the main step
in the proof of Proposition 3.25; with it, we simply proceed by induction in ℓ, and use the initial cumulant
expansion for ℓ = 0 (Proposition 5.18 in Section 5.5), which is done in Section 5.6.
Definition 5.14. Given a molecule M and H⊆p(M), we define r(M)↘H, if each particle line r∈r(M)
belongs to a component of M, which contains either another particle line in r(M), or a particle line in H.
Remark 5.15. Using Definition 3.19, the condition r(M)↘H can also be expressed as “each particle line in
r(M) is connected to either another particle line in r(M) via M, or connected to a particle line in H via M,
or belongs to H”, in the same manner as Definition 3.20 (1d).
In reality, r(M) = r(Mℓ′) will be the root particle set of a certain layer Mℓ′, and H = r(Mℓ′−1) will be
the root particle set of the previous layer. The symbol r(M)↘ H means that r(M) is either connected
“horizontally” to itself, or “vertically” to H in the previous layer.
Proposition 5.16. Fixℓ and (Aℓ, Λℓ) defined in (1.32). Assume for each setQℓ−1 withs+ =|Qℓ−1|≤ Λ2
ℓAℓ,
we have cumulant expansion at t = (ℓ− 1)τ,

whereEHℓ−1((ℓ− 1)τ) is a function of zHℓ−1 (withE∅ = 1 by convention) and is symmetric in its variables,

Remark 5.17. We make a few remarks regarding Proposition 5.16. First, the function EH(ℓτ) depends only
on|H| and is symmetric in its variables, and the functions Err, Err 1 and Err 2 are symmetric in all their
variables. These will be guaranteed for the rest of this paper.

EHℓ should be completely independent from the rest (and thus be factorized out). This is equivalent to the
condition in Definition 3.20 (1d), and is crucial in the subsequent algorithms.

5.5. The initial cumulant expansion. In this subsection we prove the initial cumulant expansion (5.41)
for ℓ = 0, without the Err term, see Proposition 5.18. The initial cumulants EH0(0) correspond to the

1 M(z0) = 1. To prove this, consider the trajectoryzE(t) defined by the E-dynamics with initial configuration

By Definition 4.6, we know that fort =τ, the molecule M(τ) has a component Mi with either|Mi|p > Λ or

∗ ) has a large component
Mi; there must be a collision n happening at t∗, such that M = M(t+

∗ ) by adding
one more collision n. Note that this collision either happens within a component Mj of M(t−
∗ ) (so does not

∗ ) together (so the
new component Mi of M(t+
∗ ) satisfies|Mi|p =|Mj|p +|Mk|p and ρ(Mi) =ρ(Mj) +ρ(Mk)). By definition of
t∗, we know that no component of M(t−
∗ ) is large, so |Mj|p≤ Λ and ρ(Mj)≤ Γ, and the same for Mk. In
particular, we know that Mi is large, and|Mi|p≤ 2Λℓ and ρ(Mi)≤ 2Γ.

Now, let M be the molecule formed by adding to Mi the empty ends corresponding to each root particle in

Remark 5.19. With Proposition 3.25 proved, in the rest of this paper, we need to estimate the quantitiesIN M
and|IN M| on the right hand side of (3.16)–(3.20). Note that these quantities are constant in equivalence
classes [ M]. Thus, as said in Remark 3.4, for the rest of this paper, we will use M to denote unlabeled
molecules only (plus labelings of root particles only).

7. Preparations for EH estimates
From this section until Section 13, we will prove Proposition 6.2 (and the associated Proposition 9.7).
We start from the upper bounds of EH and Err1 in (3.17)–(3.18) in Proposition 3.25, which have the formP
M|IN M| (here and below we will replace [ M] by M as M now represents unlabeled molecules, see Remark
5.19). To bound these terms, it suffices to (i) control the number of terms M in the summation, and (ii)
control the size of|IN M| for each individual M.
In this section, first in Section 7.1 we prove an upper bound (Proposition 7.2) on the number of choices
of M, and then in Section 7.2 we reduce the L1 norm of |IN M| to an integral IM(QM) involving Dirac δ
functions that localizes each individual collision and overlap (Proposition 7.5). The estimates of this quantity
IM(QM) will then occupy Sections 8–9.
7.1. Upper bounds of combinatorial factors. In this subsection we prove an upper bound on the number
of molecules M in Proposition 7.2. For this we need to introduce the following parameter ρ for molecules M:
Definition 7.1 (The parameter ρ). Given a molecule M with highest layer ℓ and lowest layer 1, with layer
ℓ′ denoted by Mℓ′. Define the parameters sℓ′ and Rℓ′ by sℓ′ :=|r(Mℓ′)| and Rℓ′ := ρ(Mℓ′) for ℓ′∈ [1 : ℓ];
also define s0 =|H0| where H0 is the initial cumulant set (see (5.87)), i.e. those particles involved in initial
links. Now define the parameter ρ by

We interpret the quantity ρ as the sum of Rℓ′ (the total number of recollisions within each single layer),
together with the sum of sℓ′ (the total number of particles lines crossing different layers).

Proof. We only consider the case M∈F Λℓ, as the other cases are similar. Note that M is constructed from
its layers Mℓ′ by the following process, see Part 3 of the proof of Proposition 3.25 and Figure 11:
• Label the root particles, and draw a molecule Mℓ′ with a single layer ℓ′, for each ℓ′∈ [1 :ℓ];
• For each ℓ′∈ [1 : ℓ− 1], identify a subset of p(Mℓ′) with a subset of p(Mℓ′+1), call this identified
subset Hℓ′ and|Hℓ′| =sℓ′;
• For each particle line in Hℓ′, merge the corresponding top end in Mℓ′ and bottom end in Mℓ′+1 to
form a bond;
• Finally choose a set H0 ⊆ p(M1), and fix the initial links L between particle lines in H0 as in
Proposition 5.18.
We now estimate the number of choices for M, by considering each of the above steps.
Proof part 1 . In this part we prove that |M|≤| logε|C∗
and|M|p≤| logε|C∗
. In fact, by Definition

Proof part 2. In this part we consider the first step of labeling and constructing individual Mℓ′. Note
that labelings lead to |H|!≤| logε|C∗ρ choices as|H| =|r(M)| =|r(Mℓ)| = sℓ≤ ρ. Next we may fix the
values of (|Mℓ′|)ℓ′∈[1:ℓ]. As these numbers add up to the fixed |M|, we have at most 2 |M| choices which is
acceptable in (7.2). Similarly, we can also fix the values of ( sℓ′) and (ρ(Mℓ′)) at an acceptable price. Now,
note that each Mℓ′ is a graph with each atom having at most 4 bonds; moreover it is formed by adding at
most ρ(Mℓ′) edges to a forest.
It is known that the number of trees (and forests) with |Mℓ′| atoms and each atom having at most 4
bonds can be bounded by 10 |Mℓ′| (the result for forests follows from that for trees; for trees, we may fix a
root and turn the tree into a rooted, at most ternary tree, and use the well-known counting upper bound
for the number of binary and ternary trees).
Once the tree parts are fixed, we add in the ≤ρ extra edges one by one; each edge has at most |Mℓ′|2≤
| logε|C∗
choices, which leads to| logε|C∗ρ(Mℓ′) choices in total. All these factors are acceptable in (7.2) even
after accounting for different ℓ′ (for exampleQ

Proof part 3. In this part we consider the steps of matching and merging particle lines/ends in Mℓ′ and

Proof part 4. In this part we consider the last step of constructing H0 andL. Note that |H0| =s0, and
|L| (i.e. the number of initial links in L) is bounded by 2|H0| asL is a forest. The number of choices of H0
andL is then trivially bounded by | logε|C∗s0, which is also acceptable.
By putting Parts 1–4 above together that accounts for all steps in the construction of M, we have proved
(7.2) and Proposition 7.2. □

Remark 7.4. We make a few remarks concerning Definition 7.3.
(1) The time variables tn represent the collision or overlap time of n. The vector variables ze = (xe,ve)
are associated with the linear piece of trajectory along edges e, where ve is the velocity (which is
constant along this piece), and xe is the position transported backwards to time 0 (so the actual
position of the particle at time t in this linear piece is xe +tve).
(2) The Dirac functions in ∆n prescribes the collision or overlap by forcing the elastic collision formula
in (7.3) and forcing the same velocity in (7.4). In (7.6), we treat all the variables ze for fixed ends e
as fixed, so the result of this integral is a function (or distribution) or these fixed variables.
(3) In (7.5) we have the natural (partial) time ordering, consistent with the atom ordering in Definition
3.5: the time tn for n must be before the time tn+ for n+ (its parent, or equivalently the next
collision). Moreover collisions in layer Mℓ′ must happen in time interval [( ℓ′− 1)τ,ℓ′τ],which leads
to natural orderings between different layers. These time orderings will occasionally be useful in the

===== PAGE 65 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 65
proof below. Apart from these, we do not assume any total ordering of collision times (except in an
auxiliary step in the proof of Proposition 7.5 below).

Remark 7.6. By (7.7) and (7.9), we know that changing the value of QM on Lebesgue zero sets does not
affect the value of IM(QM). Below we will exploit this to make sure that the functions QM integrated in
(7.6) are everywhere defined Borel functions, and their support satisfy that no two collisions happen at the
same time, and no collision happens at any ℓτ.
Remark 7.7. Note the following difference between C-atoms and O-atoms. In (7.3), the distribution ∆ is
symmetric under permutation (e1,e 2,e′

and also distinguish serial edges, in order for (7.3) to be well-defined; however there is also symmetry between
“top” and “bottom” in the case when the [( ve1−ve2)·ω]− factor does not play a role.
On the other hand, in (7.4), the distribution ∆ is symmetric under permutation exchanging e1 with e′

distinguish top and bottom edges, as long as the serial edges (or particle lines) are properly distinguished.
8. Operations on molecules
From this section until Section 9, we will prove Proposition 6.2 (pending Proposition 9.7 which will be
proved in Sections 10–13). Thanks to the preparations in Section 7, we only need to estimate the quantity
IM(QM) in (7.7)–(7.8) for a fixed molecule M.
In Section 8.1, we define several operations on the molecule M, which reduce it to smaller and simpler
elementary molecules (see Definition 8.11). In Section 8.2 we study the behavior of the quantityIM(Q) under
these operations, see Propositions 8.13–8.14. With these, it then suffices to estimate the IM(Q) integrals for
elementary molecules, which are done in Section 9.
8.1. Cutting, deleting and splitting. The operations we will define include cutting (see Definition 8.3;
this is the main operation that breaks M into two smaller molecules, which corresponds to rewriting the
integralIM in (7.6) as an iterated integral), deleting (see Definition 8.1; this forgets the overlap condition
at an O-atom), and splitting (see Definition 8.9; this restricts to different sub-domains in the integral, or
equivalently the support of Q in (7.6)).
The notion of deleting an O-atom is already defined as part of Definition 3.10. We repeat here for the
reader’s convenience, together with a few more notions for molecules.
Definition 8.1 (Deleting and various other notions) . We define the following notions for a molecule M.
(1) Delete an O-atom. Let o be an O-atom with 4 edges ei (1≤ i≤ 4) connected to other atoms ni,
wheree1 ande3 are serial and same for e2 ande4. By deleting the O-atom o, we remove o and the
4 edges ej from M, and add two bonds e′
1 between n1 and n3, and e′
2 between n2 and n4, with the
same top/bottom assignment as (e1,e 3) and (e2,e 4). Here if some ni is absent, say e3 is a free end
at o, then the bond (e1,e 3) should be replaced by a free end at n1, etc.

===== PAGE 68 =====
68 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
(2) Degree. Define the degree of n (abbreviated deg) to be the number of edges at n that are not fixed
ends (counting only bonds and free ends). Note that any atom has deg at most 4, and every atom
has deg 4 if and only M is full.
(3) Sub-molecules. Let M′⊆ M be a subset of atoms. We will regard M′ as a molecule (referred to as a
sub-molecule of M), by viewing each bond between an atom in M′ and an atom in M\M′ as a free
end at the atom in M′.
Next we define the important notion of regular molecules, which is an important property that governs
our treatment of O-atoms and definition of cutting (Definition 8.3) below.
Definition 8.2. We say M is regular, if for any O-atom n∈ M, any fixed end at n must be serial with a
free end at n (we say they form a simple pair), see Figure 12. This property will be assumed for all the
molecules studied below, and will be preserved throughout the reduction process.
Figure 12. Structures allowed and not allowed at O-atoms in regular molecules. The
simple pair is formed by a fixed end and serial free end at an O-atom as shown on the left.
Now we introduce the operation of cutting. For some intuitions see Remark 8.6.
Definition 8.3 (Cutting). Let M be a regular molecule and A⊆ M be a set of atoms. The operation
of cutting A as free (resp. M\A as fixed) turns M into a new molecule M′ which is the disjoint union
of two molecules, i.e. M′ = M1⊔ M2. For convenience, we may also say that M is “cut into two smaller
molecules M1 and M2”. We understand that all empty ends belong to M1; as for the atoms and other edges,
we describe the procedure as follows, see Figure 13. For some intuitions see also Remark 8.6.
(1) The atom sets of M1 and M2 are A and M\A respectively. If M contains only C-atoms (so each
maximal ov-segment is just a bond), then we simply break each bond e between an atom in A and
an atom in M\A, by removing it and drawing a free end e1 (resp. fixed end e2) at the corresponding
atom in A (resp. M\A). We form M1 (resp. M2) by atoms in A (resp. M\A) and these edges.
(2) Now, suppose M contains O-atoms. For each maximal ov-segment σ that contains an atom in A and
an atom in M\A (other maximal ov-segments are not affected), let the highest and lowest atoms of
σ be n+ and n−, and let ( p1,··· , ps) be the atoms in A∩σ (ordered high to low). Then, we take
the following steps, which extend the steps in (1) from bonds to ov-segments, see Figure 13:
(a) First remove every bond in σ. Then, for 1 ≤ j≤ s− 1, draw a bond between pj and pj+1.
Then, if p1̸= n+, draw a top free end at p1; if ps̸= n−, draw a bottom free end at ps. Let M1
be formed by atoms in A and these edges (constructed from different σ).
(b) Next, if n+∈ σ\A, draw a bottom fixed end at n+; if n−∈ σ\A, draw a top fixed end at n−.
Then, for any n±̸= n∈ σ\A, draw a simple pair at n consisting of a free and and a (serial)
fixed end. Let M2 be formed by atoms in M\A and these edges (constructed from different σ).
Note that, deleting O-atoms does not affect the correspondence between molecular and physical pictures
under topological reduction. However, this correspondence will break down after cutting. Therefore, we will
only talk in the molecule language after performing any cutting. For example, notions like p will only be
referring to particle lines as in Definition 3.1 (2), instead of physical particles.
In connection with the notion of cutting, we define the notion of ov-adjacency and a few more naturally
related notions, see Figure 14:

===== PAGE 69 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 69
Figure 13. Example of cutting (Definition 8.3). Left: simple case of cutting without O-
atoms. Right: cutting in the presence of O-atoms (showing only one maximal ov-segment
σ). Here green atoms belong to A and become M1 after cutting, while blue atoms belong
to M\A and become M2 after cutting. The n± and pj are as indicated.
Definition 8.4 (Ov- notions). Recall molecules M and ov-segments in Definition 3.1.
(1) Ov-adjacency. We define two atoms n1 and n2 to be ov-adjacent, if they are connected by an
ov-segment; if n1 is an ancestor (resp. descendant) of n2 along this ov-segment, we say it is an
ov-parent (resp. ov-child) of n2.
(2) Ov-connectedness. For a subset A⊆ M, we say it is ov-connected if for any two atoms n, n′∈ A
there exist atoms nj∈ M (0≤ j≤ q) such that ( n0, nq) = ( n, n′) and nj is ov-adjacent with nj+1.
The maximal ov-connected subsets of A are called ov-components of A.
(3) Ov-distance. Finally, define the ov-distance of two subsets B,B′⊆ M to be the minimal q such
that there exist atoms nj∈ A (0≤ j≤ q) such that ( n0, nq)∈ B×B′ and nj is ov-adjacent with
nj+1. Note that the ov-distance is 0 if B∩B′̸= ∅, and is ∞ if they are contained in different
connected components10 of M.
Figure 14. The ov- related notions in Definition 8.4. Here A is the set of colored atoms,
which has 3 ov-components Aj represented by 3 different colors (as opposed to 7 connected
components). The ov-distance between A1 and A2 is 2, while the ov-distance between A1
and A3 is∞.
Lemma 8.5. Regularity is preserved after deleting an O-atom or cutting a subset A as fixed or free.
10The ov-components of the whole molecule M are the same as the components. However the ov-components of subsets
A⊆ M might not be the same as its components.

===== PAGE 70 =====
70 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
Proof. Simply note that, the forbidden configuration, where a fixed end at an O-atom is serial with another
fixed end or a bond, cannot be constructed in the deleting or cutting process. This can be directly verified
for each step in Definitions 8.1 and 8.3. □
Remark 8.6. We make a few remarks concerning Definitions 8.3–8.4.
(1) The way to understand cutting is the following: suppose we cut A as free (and M\A as fixed) and
consider a maximal ov-segment σ as in Definition 8.3 (2), see Figure 13. Then, in M2 (which has
atom set M\A), every possible bond in σ is broken, and each atom in M2 has a new fixed end. We
may simply say that “the ov-segment σ is broken in M2”. On the other hand, in M1 (which has
atom set A), the ov-segments are preserved and the atoms in M1∩σ are still ov-adjacent along σ
(with the M2 atoms between them neglected) and they do not have any new fixed end.
(2) In particular, if we cut A as free, then this cutting generates (one or more) fixed end at every atom
n∈ M\A that is ov-adjacent to an atom in A, and not at any other atom. When n is C-atom, this
fixed end is bottom (resp. top) if n is ov-parent (resp. ov-child) of an atom in A (as for O-atoms, the
bottom/top distinction is less important, but it is important that M is regular, see Remark 8.7).
Remark 8.7. Recall Definition 8.2 or regular molecules. This regularity will be retained in all algorithms:
any simple pair formed by fixed end at O-atom and the serial free end is completely unrelated to the rest
of the molecule (and this O-atom gets detached from both its neighbors). Some simple consequences of this
will be used frequently below: (i) any O-atom of deg 2 must not be adjacent to any other atom, and cutting
it does not affect the rest of the molecule; (ii) for any O-atom n of deg 3, it can be connected to other
atoms along one ov-segment. All ov-parents and ov-children of n (if any) must belong to the same (maximal)
ov-segment and are ov-adjacent to each other.
Remark 8.8. Note that, deleting O-atoms (Definition 8.1) does not affect the correspondence between molec-
ular and physical pictures under topological reduction. However, after cutting, this correspondence will break
down, and we will only talk in the molecule language afterwards (for example notions like p will only be
referring to particle lines as in Definition 3.1 (2)), instead of physical particles.
We now discuss the operation of splitting into sub-cases. This is not an operation on the molecule M but
rather an operation on the domain of integration (or equivalently on the support of Q) in the integral (7.6).
In reality, it is used to classify the various degenerate and non-degenerate cases witch will require different
strategy in the algorithm (cf. Remark 9.5).
Definition 8.9. In splitting, we do not change M but split the support of Q in (7.6) as follows: decompose
1 into finitely many indicator functions 1 = P
j 1 Sj, and decompose Q =P
jQ1 Sj. Each of these terms
corresponds to a sub-case where we make extrarestrictions on the support ofQ provided by the conditions
definingSj. Examples of such defining conditions include |xe−xe′|≥ λ (or oppositely |xe−xe′|≤ λ),
|ve−ve′|≥ λ or|tn−tn′|≥ λ (or opposite) etc., where ( e,e′) are any two given edges, ( n, n′) are any two
given atoms, and λ is any given quantity, cf. the definition of good molecules in Definition 9.4.
Now we define the notion of operation sequences, which are sequences consisting of operations of deleting,
cutting and splitting:
Definition 8.10 (Operation sequence) . Given a molecule M and Q, define an operation sequence to
be a sequence of operations acting on M or Q, such that each one is either cutting a subset of M as in
Definition 8.3, or deleting an O-atom in M as in Definition 8.1, or splitting into finitely many sub-cases
(by decomposing 1 into indicator functions) as in Definition 8.9. We require that all the deletings must occur
before all the cuttings (splittings can occur anywhere in the sequence). If an operation sequence does not
contain splitting, then we call it a cutting sequence.
Finally, we define the elementary molecules, which are the result of the cutting sequences we will construct
in the algorithms below, and the main object of study in Section 9:

===== PAGE 71 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 71
Definition 8.11 (Elementary molecules). We define a molecule M to be elementary, if it satisfies one of the
properties, see Figure 15:
(1) {2} molecules: M contains one atom of deg 2, with both fixed ends being top (or both being
bottom) in the case of C-atom, and with the two fixed ends not serial in the case of O-atom.
(2) {3} molecules: M contains one atom of deg 3.
(3) {4} molecules: M contains one atom of deg 4.
(4) {33} molecules: M contains two atoms connected by a bond, which are both deg 3. We say M is
{33A} molecule if we can cut one atom as free such that the other atom becomes a {2} molecule
as defined above. Otherwise we say M is {33B} molecule.
(5) {44} molecules: M contains two atoms connected by a bond, which are both deg 4.
Figure 15. The elementary molecules as in Definition 8.11. Note the good/normal/bad
property of this molecule depends on the extra restrictions on the variables due to splitting,
see Definition 9.4; the edges ej and atoms nj are marked as will be used in the proof of
Propositions 9.1–9.3.
8.2. Operations on associated operators. In this subsection, we study the behavior of IM(Q) in (7.6)
under the cutting, deleting and splitting operations. Namely, we will prove IM(Q)≤I M′(Q) after deleting
(Propositions 8.13) andIM(Q) =IM1◦I M2(Q) after cutting (Proposition 8.14).
Recall the integral (7.6), where Q is a function of tM and zE. If we perform deleting or cutting to M
and turn it into M′ or M′ = M1⊔ M2, then we will get a new set of vector variables ze associated with the
new molecule (the time variables remain the same as atoms are not affected by cutting). In order to define
IM′(Q) andIM1◦I M2(Q) for the same Q, we need to match the vector variables for M′ with those for M.
More precisely, we need to define the following two mappings:
• A mapping from the set of bonds plus free ends of M to that of M′; this decides how we transform
the function Q in (7.6) between the integral variables of M and those of M′.
• A mapping from the set of new fixed ends of M2 to the set of new bonds plus free ends of M1; this
decides, while evaluatingIM2(Q), the value that zf is fixed to be, for each fixed end f of M2.
In Definition 8.12 below, we will construct these mappings under deleting and cutting operations, which
provides the correspondences between vector variables ze.
Definition 8.12 (Transformation of associated variables under cutting and deleting). Suppose M′ is formed
from M by deleting or cutting. Define the following mappings:

e27→e1. This means, we identify ze before cutting with ze1 after cutting, and also fix the fixed end
variableze2 to be ze1.
(3) The case of cutting: general . Let σ and related notions be as in Definition 8.3 (2), we define the

the same bottom edge e′∈ M1∩σ at p1 (it could be a free end or a bond). For example, in Figure

8.12. it then suffices to show that (8.3) equals (8.4) under the above identification and assignments.
No O-atom case. Assume M has no O-atom, then we are in the simpler scenario in Definition 8.3 (1).
In this case, each new free end e1∈ M1 is paired with a new fixed end e2∈ M2 and they come from breaking
a bond e∈ M; by Definition 8.12 (2) we see that the integration in (8.3) and (8.4) are the same. Moreover,
the ∆n and Q factors are also the same under the above identification and assignments, so trivially (8.3)
equals (8.4).
General case. In the general case with O-atoms, the identification and assignments of variables and
more complicated. We only need to consider a fixed maximal ov-segment σ (along with the relevant notions
pj) as in Definition 8.3 (2). Let the bonds in σ be ej, (1≤j≤q) from top to bottom, then in view of the
Dirac factors in (7.4), we know the integral (8.3) involves the Dirac factors
q−1Y

On the other hand, for each pi∈ A∩σ, consider the bond connecting pi to its child in σ, and let it be
eji. Then, by the identification and assignments as in Definition 8.12 (3), we know that the integral (8.4)
involves the Dirac factors
s−1Y

It is easy to see that (8.5)=(8.6), as both sides are exactly the Dirac mass supported on the linear subspace

δ(ze1−ze2)δ(ze2−ze3)δ(ze3−ze4)δ(ze4−ze5) =δ(ze1−ze4)δ(ze4−ze5)·δ(ze2−ze1)δ(ze3−ze1).
Finally, note that all the Dirac functions in (8.5) and (8.6) imply that all the variables zej are equal, so
by inserting them into the rest of the ∆n andQ factors in (8.3) and (8.4), we conclude that these remaining
factors are also the same for (8.3) and (8.4). This then shows that (8.3) equals (8.4), and completes the
proof of Proposition 8.14. □
Remark 8.15. In general, (8.2) holds with both sides being interpreted in the sense of distributions. However,
in this paper, we will only encounter the case where IM(Q) is a function whenever Q is, in which case (8.2)
holds classically. The condition that IM(Q) is a function is really a condition on the molecule M, which
restricts the number and places of its fixed ends, and will always be satisfies throughout the algorithm. That
being said, one can still interpret (8.2) in bigger generality in the sense of distributions as follows: If one
replaces all the delta functions appearing in the definition of IM by approximations of identity, then the
analog of formula (8.2) holds with no ambiguity. The same result then holds by taking the limits of those
approximations of identity (in the appropriate distributional sense).
Note that the operators IM1 andIM2 in Proposition 8.14 in general do not commute (unless M has no
fixed end). Therefore, it is important to order the resulting components after cutting, such that M1 which
is cut as free always occurs to the left of M2 which is cut as fixed (i.e. the operator IM2 is applied first,
and the corresponding variables are integrated first in the iterated integral). This ordering is defined in the
following definition.

===== PAGE 74 =====
74 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
Definition 8.16 (Ordering components after cutting) . Suppose a molecule M becomes a new molecule M′
after a sequence of cutting operations; we refer to the connected components of M′ as final components.
We define a total ordering≺cut between these final components inductively, as follows (see Figure 46 for an
example):
(1) In the first cutting operation, suppose M is cut into M1 (as free) and M2 (as fixed). Then for any
final componentX,Y of M′, we require thatX≺ cutY ifX⊆ M1 andY⊆ M2.
(2) For the final components that are contained in M1 (resp. in M2), order them inductively using≺cut
defined for the subsequent sequence of cutting operations applied to M1 (resp. to M2).
If M′ is the result of M after (deleting and) cutting, and M′ is the disjoint union of its final components,
i.e. M′ = M1⊔···⊔ Mk with M1≺cut···≺ cut Mk, then we define the associated integral operator by
IM′ =IM1◦I M2◦···◦I Mk. (8.7)
Remark 8.17. As explained above, if Mi≺cut Mj, then the operatorIMj is applied first, and the Mj variables
are integrated first. In many cases of the algorithm below, each time we will cut an elementary component
as free, and work on the remaining molecule (cf. Remark 10.7 below); in this case ≺cut is just the order of
being cut, i.e. Y≺ cutX if and only if Y is cut before X . This ordering will be useful in identifying a good
molecule after splitting, see Definition 9.4 (3a), (3e).
Suppose we have a cutting sequence, and one of the cuttings breaks a maximal ov-segment σ as in
Definition 8.3 and creates free and fixed ends. Then for any such fixed end e, there always exists a free end
or bond e′ such that ze =ze′, and we haveY≺ cutX whereX andY are the final components containing e
and e′ respectively. In fact, we can choose e′ to be the image of e under the mapping in Definition 8.12 (3),
thenY≺ cutX follows from Definition 8.16 (1). This will also be helpful in getting good molecules below.
By putting together Definition 8.9, Proposition 8.13 and Proposition 8.14, we arrive at the main conclusion
of this section, namely

9. Estimating the integral IM(QM)
In this section, we complete the proof of Proposition 6.2, pending the proof of the pure combinatorial
Proposition 9.7 (which is in Sections 10–13). Starting from Proposition 3.25, using Propositions 7.2, 7.5 and
8.18, we can reduce Proposition 6.2 to the estimate of quantities of form IM′(Q′), where M′ (and Q′) are
possible results of an operation sequence applied to M (and Q =QM), corresponding to possible sub-cases.
The operation sequence is constructed in Proposition 9.7 using the algorithms in Sections 10–13. In
Proposition 9.7 we can guarantee that:
• The number of sub-cases is acceptable (i.e. ≤C|M|·| logε|C∗ρ in Proposition 9.7);
• M′ is formed by elementary molecules (Definition 8.11);
• We have adequate control of these elementary molecules (and associated support information of Q′)
in terms of good and bad molecules (Definition 9.4 and (9.51)).
The goal of this section is to use the above information to prove an estimate for IM′(Q′), namely (9.53).
Note that thisIM′ is a composition ofIMj where Mj are components of M′ which are elementary molecules.
In Section 9.1 we will estimate eachIMj, and classify them as good, normal, and bad in Definition 9.4. Then
in Section 9.2, we put together these local estimates and apply Proposition 9.7, to prove (9.53) and thus
finish the proof of Proposition 6.2. These arguments rely on some technical estimates concerning volumes of
(ve) and cross-sections in (7.6), which we leave to Section 9.3.

Proposition 9.1 (2).
Finally, assume (i) is true but (ii) is not, by the above proof, we may assume that Q is supported in the
set where|t1−t2|≤ ευ. But since t1 and t2 must belong to disjoint time intervals in view of ℓ[n1]̸=ℓ[n2],
we conclude that t1 itself must belong to a fixed interval of length at most ευ. Then (9.25) follows from
exploiting the volume of support of t1. This completes the proof of (1).

Proof of (3). This is easy and follows from (1): simply note that (x1,v 1,x 7,v 7) belongs to a set of volume
ε2d(1−υ)| logε|C∗
with 0 < υ <1/(4d). Once ( x1,v 1,x 7,v 7) is fixed, we can perform the the integration in
the remaining variables just as in the{33A} molecule case in (1). This leads to the desired bound (9.25). □
Now we define the normal, good, and bad components, based on Propositions 9.1–9.3. The cutting
algorithm aims to produce good components while avoiding bad ones. These properties depend not only on
the molecule M, but also on the restriction on the support of Q, which comes from splitting (Definition 8.9).
Definition 9.4 (Normal, bad and good molecules) . Suppose M′ is formed from a full molecule M by
an operation sequence (Definition 8.10) and picking one sub-case at each splitting, and M′ contains only
elementary molecules. The initial link structure L between the bottom ends of M′ are inherited from that
of M (as the operations do not affect any bottom end). Note also that in the splitting steps, by selecting a
sub-case each time, we may have made certain extra restrictions to the support of Q in (7.6).
Based on all this information, we now define the notions of good, normal and bad molecules. Below, for
any one-atom molecule{n1}, we denote the 4 edges by e1,...,e 4, where e1 is serial with e3, and e2 is serial
withe4. For two-atom molecule{n1, n2}, we denote the 7 edges by e1,··· ,e 7 withe1 ande7 being the fixed
ends in the case of {33} molecules, see Figure 15. Recall also the constant υ := 3−d−1.
(1) The following molecules are normal:
(a) Any {2} molecule.
(b) Any {3} molecule, except those described in (3a) below.
(c) Any {33A} molecule, except those described in (3b) below.
(2) The following molecules are bad:
(a) Any {4} molecule.
(b) By convention, we also view any empty end as bad.
(3) The following molecules are good:
(a) Any {3} moleculeX ={n} with fixed end e1, where either the extra restriction includes one of
the following conditions:
|tn−tn′|≤ ευ or |vei−vej|≤ ευ or |xe−xe′|≤ ε1−υ or |xei−xej|≤ ε1−υ (9.46)
or (ei,ej)∈L , or (e,e′)∈L (i.e. the relevant pair of ends are initial linked as in Definition 3.1;
see also the 1 ε
L factor in Definition 7.3).
Here in (9.46) we assume that (i) n′ is an atom in a molecule Y withY≺ cutX (Definition
8.16), and e′ is a free end or bond at n′; (ii) e is a free end at n and ei̸=ej are two ends at n;
(iii) if n is O-atom, then e′ is not serial with e1, and ei not serial with ej. In this case, tn′ and
xe′ are fixed variables, so the extra restrictions (or initial link) makes X good.
(b) Any {33A} moleculeX ={n1, n2} with fixed ends ( e1,e 7), where either the extra restriction
includes one of the following conditions:
|xe1−xe7|≥ ε1−υ or |ve1−ve7|≥ ε1−υ or |tn1−tn2|≥ ευ, (9.47)
or ℓ[n1]̸=ℓ[n2]. In this case X is good as a {33A} molecule without strong degeneracy.
(c) Any {33B} moleculeX ={n1, n2} of two C-atoms with fixed ends ( e1,e 7), where the extra
restriction includes both of the following conditions:
|vei−vej|≥ ευ and |tn1−tn2|≥ ευ. (9.48)
Here (ei,ej) are two different ends at the same atom ( n1 or n2). In this case X is good as a
{33B} molecule without weak degeneracy.
(d) Any {44} moleculeX ={n1, n2}, where the extra restriction includes both of the following
conditions:
|xe1−xe7|≤ ε1−υ and |ve1−ve7|≤ ε1−υ. (9.49)

===== PAGE 84 =====
84 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
Here (e1,e 7) are two free ends at n1 and n2 respectively, such that M becomes a{33A} molecule
after turning these two free ends into fixed ends. In this case the strong extra restrictions (9.49)
makesX good.
(e) Any {4} moleculeX ={n}, where either the extra restriction includes one of the following
conditions:
|vei−vej|≤ ευ or |xe−xe′|≤ ε1−υ or |xei−xej|≤ ε1−υ, (9.50)
or (ei,ej)∈L , or (e,e′)∈L (i.e. the relevant pair of ends are initial linked as in Definition 3.1;
see also the 1 ε
L factor in Definition 7.3).
Here in (9.50) we assume that (i) n′ is an atom in a molecule Y withY≺ cutX (Definition
8.16), and e′ is a free end or bond at n′; (ii) e is a free end at n; (iii) (ei,ej) are two different
ends at n, and are non-serial when n is O-atom. In this case, xe′ is a fixed variable, so the extra
restrictions (or initial link) makes X good.
(f) Finally, by convention, we also view an empty end e as good, if ( e,e′)∈L for some e′, i.e.
e is involved in some initial link. In this case the initial link condition (see the 1 ε
L factor in
Definition 7.3) will ensure the gain and thus we view e as good.
Remark 9.5. We explain the classification of elementary molecules in Definition 9.4 as follows:
(1) Note that Definition 9.4 allows for some {4} molecules and empty ends to be both good and bad;
this will not cause any confusion, because in practice the property of good/non-good and bad/non-
bad are always exploited independently. The rule of thumb is that, each good molecule leads to a
powerευ gain in the estimate of the integralIM(Q) in (7.6), and each bad molecule leads to a power
ε−(d−1) loss in this integral (and each normal molecule leads to no gain or loss). These gain and loss
estimates easily follow from Definition 9.4 and Propositions 9.1–9.3.
(2) Note that the definition of good molecules depends on various extra restrictions imposed in Definition
9.4. In fact, these extra restrictions (corresponding to degeneracy and non-degeneracy conditions) are
what motivates the splitting steps in the operation sequence. Basically, we may split into degenerate
and non-degenerate sub-cases, and exploit different good molecules based on the extra restrictions in
each different sub-case. We may also design different cutting strategies based on different sub-cases,
which will be clear in the algorithm below (cf. the strongly and weakly degeneracies in Propositions
12.5 and 12.17, where we have a different cutting strategy from the main case).
Remark 9.6. Most of the elementary molecules that will occur in the algorithm (Sections 11–13) are{2},{3},
{4} and{33A} molecules. In fact, {33B} molecules will occur only in the MAINUD algorithm (Definition
13.12), and the{44} molecule will occur only in Choice 1 in the proof of Proposition 12.5. Our construction
will guarantee that the{33B} and{44} molecules are always good every time they occur.
9.2. Proof of Proposition 6.2. In this subsection we finish the proof of Proposition 6.2, assuming Propo-
sition 9.7 below, which states that there exists an operation sequence that satisfies the nice property (9.51).
This condition is natural in view of the gains and losses associated with good and bad molecules (see Remark
9.5; the choice of υ/2 instead of υ is due to the need to cover certain log losses).
The construction of the operation sequence in Proposition 9.7 will be left to Sections 10–13. The proof
in this subsection relies on Propositions 3.25, 7.2, 7.5, 8.18 and 9.1–9.3. We also rely on two estimates for
(ve) volume and cross sections in (7.6), namely Propositions 9.8 and 9.9. The proof of these estimates are
technical, and we leave it to Section 9.3.

Proof part 2: the forest case. Now consider the case when M has only one layer ℓ′, and is a forest.
By restricting to components, we may assume M is a tree (which need not be a binary tree). We shall divide
the atom set of M into finitely many binary trees and upside down binary trees (where the notions of parent
and child etc. are the opposite from binary trees), as follows, see Figure 17.
First choose any atom n∈ M, and let Tn be the set of descendants of n. This is a binary tree, because
each atom has at most one parent in Tn (as M has no cycle). Moreover, all bottom ends of Tn are also
bottom ends of M. Consider all the atoms nj̸∈Tn that are connected to Tn by a bond. Since M is a tree,
if we remove Tn from M (and turn any bond between nj and Tn to a free end at nj), then M becomes the
disjoint union of trees Mj, where nj∈ Mj for each j. Note also that each nj has only one bond connected
to Tn, which is turned to a bottom free end in Mj.
Now for each j, let T∗
nj be the set of ancestors of nj in Mj, which is now an upside down binary tree.
Clearly all top ends ofT∗
nj are also top ends of M, and all the discussions in Step 1 above also work for upside
down binary trees due to time reversal symmetry. Consider all the atoms njk∈ Mj\T∗
nj that are connected
to T∗
nj by a bond, then after removing T∗
nj from Mj, the Mj will become the disjoint union of trees Mjk
where njk∈ Mjk for each k. Also each njk has only one bond connected to T∗
nj, which is turned to a top
free end in Mjk. Therefore, we can choose Tnjk to be the set of descendants of njk in Mjk, which is a binary
tree, so that all bottom ends of Tnjk are also bottom ends of M, and so on.
Figure 17. The decomposition of a general tree M into binary trees and upside down
binary trees, as described in Part 2 of the proof of Proposition 9.8. Here n, nj, njk etc. and
Tn, T∗
nj, Tnjk etc., are defined as in Part 2. Different colors represent different binary trees
and upside down binary trees.
