10. Preparations for the algorithm
From this section until Section 13, we will prove Proposition 9.7, the main goal of the third part of the
paper. In this section, we make some preparations for the proof by restating the properties of the molecule
M in Proposition 9.7 (originally stated in Definition 3.20) in a more convenient way for the algorithm.
Start from the initial links L; we shall replace it by some new initial link set eL where each particle line
belongs to at most two new initial links in eL. This is because, see the proof of Proposition 12.17, for any
initial link, we will gain a power ofε from the particle line in it that is cut after the other; then the condition
for eL ensures that the number of gains from this is comparable with the number of initial links.
Proposition 10.1. Recall the setL of initial links (see Proposition 5.18), let the set of particle lines involved
inL be H0. Then we have

Define a graph L0 which is a subgraph of L, such that ( p1,p 2) ∈ L0 if and only if ( p1,p 2) ∈ Land
|xp1−xp2|≤ ε. Clearly we may restrict to a single component of the forest L (which is a tree), then in this

between any two adjacent particle lines in the above list, then it is easy to see that |xp1−xp2|≥ ε1−υ occurs
at most q times (when entering new components), which implies 1 ε+

Proof of (1)–(8). For (1): this is already proved in Part 1 of the proof of Proposition 7.2.
For (2): this follows from the correspondence between molecules and physical trajectories in Definition
3.8, and the fact that two (physical) particles in Rd cannot collide/overlap and immediately collide/overlap
again without other any other collision in between.
For (3): recall Definition 3.11. We know Mℓ′ can be constructed, starting from the C-molecule with only
the clusters, by adding the O-atoms corresponding to the overlaps joining different clusters, according to
cluster graph of Mℓ′. Since this cluster graph is a forest (Definition 3.20 (1c)), we know that the process of
adding O-atoms will not generate new cycles in Mℓ′, and will only insert O-atoms into edges of existing cycles
(turning these edges into ov-segments). Therefore the first statement in (3) is true. The second statement
follows from the first, as the O-atom must be an interior atom of an ov-segment connecting two C-atoms.
For (4): this is just Definition 3.20 (1a).
For (5): this follows directly from Definition 3.18.

To end this section, we record some intuitive facts about molecules, particle lines and cuttings, which will
be used frequently in the algorithm below.
Remark 10.5. Let A be a layer interval M[ℓ1:ℓ2] (Definition 10.3), or any union of connected components of
a layer interval, or the same for thin layers (defined in Definition 12.8 below). It is easy to see that: for any
atoms n, n′∈A that belong to the same particle line, and any atom m in the same particle line between n
and n′, we must have m∈A. This implies that (i) any particle line p can intersect at most one component
ofA, and (ii) if two particle lines p1,p2 are both connected to a particle line p viaA (Definition 3.19), then
p1 andp2 are also connected via A. This is used in the proof of Proposition 10.4 (9) above; see Figure 19.
Remark 10.6. The following fact will be convenient to note: suppose we cut A⊆ M as free, then this may
divide the remaining set M\A into connected components; however, any full component of M\A must also

===== PAGE 99 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 99
be a full component of M before cutting A as free (in other words, any newly created connected component
must have at least one fixed end). This is because, when any bond or ov-segment is broken in cutting A as
free, we get a fixed end at every atom in this ov-segment that belongs to M\A, see Remark 8.6.
Remark 10.7. In the algorithm below, we usually adopt the following convention: once we cut out any
elementary molecule S, or cut out any subset S (as free or as fixed) and subsequently cut it into elementary
molecules, we will ignore this S for the rest of the cutting sequence and consider the remaining molecule
M\S only. For example, when we say “keep cutting deg 2 atoms as free until there is no more deg 2 atom”,
we always mean there is no more deg 2 atom in the remaining molecule.
11. Main thread of the algorithm
We now start the proof of Proposition 9.7. Most of the proof below will focus on the case M∈F Λℓ; the
case M∈F err
Λℓ is left to the end of Section 13.5, and will only take two paragraphs. In this section, we first
discuss a simplified setting which contains most of the main ideas.
11.1. Toy models. To prove Proposition 9.7, we need to construct an operation sequence (with cutting,
deleting and splitting). This will be done using a number of cutting algorithms, which will be presented
in Sections 12–13.
The full cutting algorithms are quite complicated; however, there is a much simpler main thread, and
many of the complications actually come from the additional structures and conditions (“accessories”) that
have to be added to this main thread. In this section, we present the proof in a simplified scenario, which
basically isolates this main thread. The accessories needed in full generality will be discussed in Section 11.7.
Throughout this section, we will make the following two simplifications (and more later):
(1) M has no O-atoms, all {33} molecules are good, and H0 = ∅ (see Proposition 10.4 (6));
(2) Each layer Mℓ′ of M is a forest.
Here, regarding (1), recall that{33} molecules are good provided certain non-degeneracy conditions are met
(which is different for{33A} and{33B} molecules, see (9.47)–(9.48) in Definition 9.4). As such, simplification
(1) allows us to ignore any O-atoms, degeneracies and initial (time t = 0) cumulants, and (2) allows us to
ignore any cycles (or “recollisions”) happening within each layer.
Given a molecule M satisfying Proposition 10.4 and (1)–(2), the goal of this section is to construct a
cutting sequence that cuts M into elementary molecules, such that (recall υ = 3−d−1)
(υ/2)· #{33}−d· #{4}≥c·ρ, (11.1)
where #{33} is the number of {33} molecules etc., c is a positive constant and ρ is in Definition 7.1 (the
splitting and deletion of O-atoms is not needed in this simplified model; also note that there cannot be any
empty end if H0 = ∅, which easily follows from Proposition 10.4 (9)).
In Sections 11.2–11.5, we will consider a further simplified case, when M contains only two layers (for
treatment of multi-layer case, see Section 11.6). We make the following definition:
Definition 11.1. We say M is a 2-layer model, if it satisfies (1)–(2), and that
(3) M can be written as M = MU∪ MD, such that no atom in MD is parent of an atom in MU;
(4) Each of MU and MD is a tree, has no fixed end, and the number of bonds connecting MU and MD
is at least ρ.
Note that in practice, the ρ in (4) may be replaced by some different quantity, but they will always be
comparable, so this will not affect the proof.
In our proof, the 2-layer model in Definition 11.1 will be further divided into 3 cases, corresponding to
toy models I–III defined below. This classification is based on the following two considerations:
(1) Consider the set of atoms in MU that are adjacent to two atoms in MD by two bonds. Is this set
big or small relative to ρ?

===== PAGE 100 =====
100 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
Figure 20. Examples of toy models. Upper left: toy model I ( X = MU). Upper right:
toy model I plus (where # comp(X) = 2). Lower left: toy model II (where # comp(X) =|X|).
Lower right: toy model III (where # 2conn∼|X|/2). The blue bonds connect MU and MD.
The orange and pink atoms indicate those in X and 2-connections respectively. The pink
dots represent patterns that can be repeated.
(2) Consider all the atoms in MU that are adjacent to at least one atom in MD by at least one bond.
Are they all connected to each other, or are they separated by other atoms in MU?
The reason for considering these two questions will be clear later. For convenience, in this section we will
refer to the atoms in MU that are adjacent to two atoms in MD as 2-connections, and denote their number
by #2conn. We also define the set of atoms in MU that are adjacent to at least one atom in MD byX (note
that|X|≳ ρ), and define the number of connected components of X (with X viewed by itself, without
passing through atoms in MU\X) by #comp(X). We now define a 2-layer molecule to be:
• Toy model I : if #2conn = 0 and X = MU. This case involves the core arguments in our algorithm in
its simplest form, and will be proved in Section 11.2.
• Toy model I plus : if # 2conn = 0 and # comp(X)≪| X| (no 2-connections, X has few components).
This is an extension of the toy model I, and can be treated in similar manners, in Section 11.3. Here
we need to introduce the new idea of UP algorithm, which will play an important role in the later
proofs.
• Toy model II : if #2conn = 0 and #comp(X)≳|X| (no 2-connections, X has many components). This
requires a different argument, which involves making a clever choice in the UP algorithm introduced
above, see Section 11.4.
• Toy model III : if # 2conn≳|X| (many 2-connections). This relies on a simpler argument, which
again involves making suitable choices in the UP algorithm, see Section 11.5.

===== PAGE 101 =====

See Figure 20 for examples of toy models I–III (we distinguish toy models I and I plus only for convenience
of exposition). Also the case when 0 < #2conn≪| X|, which is missing in the toy models, can be treated
similarly to toy models I–II but with an extra step of removing 2-connections, see Section 11.7.4.
Once all the toy models (and hence the 2-layer model) have been addressed in Sections 11.2–11.5, we
can then treat the multi-layer case in Section 11.6. This relies on the process of layer selection, which
effectively reduces any multi-layerM to (basically) a 2-layer model, for which the proofs in Sections 11.2–11.5
apply.
Finally, in Section 11.7 we discuss the simplifications made in (1)–(4) above compared to the full generality
case, as well as the corresponding accessories (additions, extra conditions and proofs) that are needed to
treat the full generality setting.
Before proceeding, we make one remark about the presentation in this section and in Sections 12–13: in
this section, the cases are arranged for exposition purposes, for example we present the 2-layer model before
layer selection, and present toy models I and I plus before the other toy models, in order to showcase the
core ideas of our proof as early as possible. However, in Sections 12–13 we need to present the materials
in logical order, so we need to introduce layer selection (Section 12.4) before the toy models (Section 13),
and toy model III (Definition 13.1–Proposition 13.2) before toy model I plus (Definition 13.12–Proposition
13.13), etc.
To make the proof easier to read, each time we discuss any argument in Sections 12–13 that has a
counterpart in this section, we will explicitly point out which argument in this section does it correspond to.

11.2. Toy model I. In this subsection we consider the following (first) toy model:
Definition 11.2. We say M is a toy model I , if it is a 2-layer model as in Definition 11.1, and that each
atom in MU has exactly one bond connecting to an atom in MD (i.e. # 2conn = 0 and X = MU).
Definition 11.3. Let MD be part of the 2-layer model as in Definition 11.1 (or any resulting molecule after
an operation sequence). We define MD to be proper, if it does not contain deg 1 or 2 atoms, does not contain
two adjacent deg 3 atoms, and does not contain two deg 3 atoms which are both adjacent to the same deg
4 atom, see Figure 21.
Figure 21. Examples of{33} and{343} molecules (left), and proper molecules (right, see
Definition 11.3). Note these notions are applied to MD, in which the top/bottom distinction
is less important, hence we draw an equivalent simplified picture below, with no distinction
of top/bottom, and with the deg (i.e. degree) of each atom indicated. Similar simplified
illustrations will occur a few times below.
If M is a toy model I, we define the following algorithm applied to it:
Definition 11.4 (algorithm for toy model I) . Let M be a toy model I. We define the following cutting
sequence. For an example see Figure 22:
(1) If not all atom in MU have been cut, then choose a lowest atom n∈ MU that has not been cut (i.e.
either n has not child in MU or all its children in MU have been cut).
(2) If n is adjacent to an atom m∈ MD which has deg 3, then cut {n, m} as free and cut m as free from
{n, m} if n has deg 4; otherwise just cut n as free.
(3) If MD contains two adjacent deg 3 atoms (say r and r′), then cut{r, r′} as free; repeat until MD has
no adjacent deg 3 atoms.
(4) If MD contains two degree 3 atoms (say r and r′′) both adjacent to the same deg 4 atom (say r′),
then cut{r, r′, r′′} as free and cut r as free from{r, r′, r′′}; then go to (3)–(4) and repeat, until MD
becomes proper (Definition 11.3).
(5) Go to (1) and choose the next lowest atom n∈ MU that has not been cut, and so on.
(6) Suppose all atoms in MU have been cut. If MD is proper, then cut any deg 3 atom; if not, then go
to (3)–(4) and repeat to remove them, and next cut any deg 3 atom, and so on.
Remark 11.5. In Definition 11.4, we are essentially applying a “naive” algorithm in MU where we simply
cut a lowest atom each time, and a “greedy” algorithm in MD where we exploit a{33} molecule (or what we
call a{343} molecule12 where two deg 3 atoms are adjacent to the same deg 4 atom). As mentioned above,
this is the core idea of our proof, in its bare simplest form. It is a simple special case of the MAINUD
algorithm, which corresponds to the toy model I plus in Section 11.3.
Before stating the main result for the algorithm in Definition 11.2, we first prove a useful lemma that will
be applied frequently below, see Figure 23.
12The reason to include this {343} molecule is technical: removing only {33} cannot exclude all forbidden scenarios, i.e.
Lemma 11.8 would not be true if we did this.

Figure 22. Algorithm for toy model I (Definition 11.4), applied to the example in Figure
20. Each time we cut the atom(s) in the red box, creating fixed ends. The type of the
elementary molecules to be cut is listed at the upper right corner.
Lemma 11.6. Suppose M is a forest and we cut a connected subset S⊆ M as free (for example, when S
contains a single atom, or 2 atoms connected by a bond, or 3 atoms connected by 2 bonds, etc.). Then, for
each atom q∈ M\S that is connected to an atom in S by a (pre-cutting) bond e, the post-cutting molecule
MD\S has exactly one component Xq∋ q, which is in bijection with q (and q is the unique atom in Xq that
is adjacent to S pre-cutting, via the bond e). When viewed in Xq, the bond e is viewed as a free end at q
before cutting, and is turned into a fixed end after cutting.

===== PAGE 104 =====
104 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
Figure 23. An illustration of Lemma 11.6. Here S is a connected subset, and there are
bijections between q and Xq, where q∈ MD\S is connected to S by a bond e, and Xq is
the component containing q after cutting S as free. When viewed in Xq, the operation of
cuttingS as free creates exactly one fixed end in Xq. All these crucially depend on the fact
that MU and MD have no cycles.
Proof. This follows from the property of forests. In fact, any q1 and q2 (with corresponding bonds e1 ande2)
must belong to different components of MD after cutting S; if not, then (pre-cutting) they can be connected
by a path involving atoms in MD\S, but they can also be connected by a path with all intermediate atoms
being in S (using the bonds e1 and e2 and the connectivity of S by itself), which produces a cycle and
contradicts the forest assumption. Thus, for each q we get a unique post-cutting component Xq, such that e
is the only pre-cutting bond between Xq and S. Clearly, when viewed in Xq, the effect of cutting S as free
is equivalent to turning one and only one free end at q (the one corresponding to e) into a fixed end. □
Proposition 11.7. Let M be a toy model I, and we apply the algorithm defined in Definition 11.4. Then
in this process we only obtain elementary molecules, and we have
#{4} = 1, #{33}≥ (|MU|− 1)/5. (11.2)
Since|MU| =|X|≳ρ≫ 1, this clearly implies (11.1).
Proof. Recall Definition 11.4 (1)–(6). We divide the proof into 4 parts.
Proof part 1. We first prove that each atom in MU is contained in an elementary molecule (after
cutting). By the choices we make in (1) and (5) in Definition 11.4, we know that for each n∈ MU, at the
time it is cut, it must be a lowest atom that still remains in MU; thus no child of it still remains in MU,
so cutting n will not introduce any top fixed end in MU (and neither does cutting anything in MD). This
means that MU will not contain any top fixed end throughout the process. Since each n∈ MU is either cut
by itself (when we cut{n} as free in Definition 11.4 (2)) or belongs to a {33} molecule (when we cut{n, m}
as free in Definition 11.4 (2)), it is clear by definition of elementary molecules that n always belongs to an
elementary molecule.
Proof part 2. We next prove that each atom in MD is contained in an elementary molecule. Note that
MD initially is a tree, and will remain a forest in the whole cutting process (cutting an atom will not create
any cycle, but may cause a tree to become a disconnected forest). Now consider each step in Definition 11.4
(2) involving n∈ MU, at which time MD must be proper by the construction of our algorithm. Consider
the possible effect of this step on MD (we may assume that n is connected to one atom m∈ MD by a bond,
otherwise cutting n will have no effect on MD), see Figure 24:
(a) If we cut n as free (which means m has deg 4), then when viewed in MD, this step simply turns one
and only one free end at m∈ MD into a fixed end, turning m from deg 4 to deg 3.
(b) If we cut {n, m} as free (which means m has deg 3), then when viewed in MD, this step simply cuts
m, which has deg 3, as free from MD.
Here in (a), we note that m must have deg 4 before cutting n (otherwise m must have deg 3 because MD is
proper, in which case we will cut {n, m} by Definition 11.4 (2)); also when viewed in MD, the bond between
n and m is initially viewed as a free end at m, and is then turned into a fixed end after cutting n as free,

===== PAGE 105 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 105
turning m from deg 4 to deg 3. Likewise m must have deg 3 in (b), and when viewed in MD, cutting{n, m}
as free is equivalent to cutting{m} as free because n has no other bonds connecting to other atoms in MD.
Figure 24. Operations (a) and (b) defined in the proof of Proposition 11.7. In either case
an atom mj∈ MD is connected to an atom nj∈ MU by a blue bond. In case (a) m1 has
deg 4, and we cut {n1} as free, creating a fixed end at m1∈ MD and turning it into deg
3; in case (b) m2 has deg 3, and we cut {n2, m2} as free, which when viewed in MD just
corresponds to cutting{m2} as free, creating fixed ends at its adjacent atoms.
Now, regarding the effects described in (a)–(b), we have the following lemma:
Figure 25. The greedy algorithm in Definition 11.4. Starting from upper left graph (which
is proper), we apply operation (a), which creates a {343} molecule (in red box). We cut it,
which then creates a{33} molecule. We cut it, and the graph becomes proper again.
Lemma 11.8. Suppose MD is proper at some point of the algorithm, and becomes non-proper after either
(a) or (b). Then, MD will once more become proper after applying Definition 11.4 (3)–(4), via cuttings of
{33} and{343} molecules only (with each{343} molecule further cut into a {3} and a{33} molecule), and
without seeing any deg 2 atom , see Figure 25 for an illustration.
Proof. Note that MD is always a forest, so we can apply Lemma 11.6. Note that right before performing
(b) at a deg 3 atom m, any atom in MD adjacent to m must have deg 4 (because MD is proper). Then
by Lemma 11.6, MD will break into at most 2 components after performing (b), and the effect of (b) when
viewed in each component is equivalent to (a). Therefore, we only need to prove Lemma 11.8 for (a).
Assume now MD becomes non-proper, say a {33} molecule forms, after (a). Let this molecule be {r, r′},
where r′ has deg 3 before (a), while r has deg 4 before (a) and is turned into deg 3 after (a). Then, before
(a), any atom in MD adjacent to r or r′ must have deg 4 (see Figure 26) because otherwise we either have
two adjacent deg 3 atoms before (a), or have two deg 3 atoms adjacent to the same deg 4 atom before (a),

===== PAGE 106 =====
106 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
contradicting properness. Therefore, by Lemma 11.6, we see that cutting the {33} molecule breaks MD into
at most 4 components, and the effect of this cutting when viewed in each component is again equivalent
to (a). We then induct on the size of MD and apply induction hypothesis to each of these components, to
conclude the proof.
Finally, assume MD becomes non-proper, but with no{33} molecule forming, after (a). In this case there
must be a {343} molecule forming, say{r, r′, r′′}, where r′ and r′′ respectively have deg 4 and 3 before (a),
and r has deg 4 before (a) and is turned into deg 3 after (a). Then, before (a), any atom adjacent to r′ or
r′′ must have deg 4 (see Figure 26) for the same reason as above thanks to properness. Moreover, before
(a), any atom adjacent to r must also have deg 4, because otherwise we would get a {33} molecule after (a),
contradicting our assumption. Therefore, by Lemma 11.6, we see that cutting the{343} molecule breaks MD
into at most 6 components, and the effect of this cutting when viewed in each component is again equivalent
to (a). We then conclude the proof by the same induction as above. This proves Lemma 11.8. □
Figure 26. Proof of Lemma 11.8. After applying (a) to a proper MD, a deg 4 atom is
turned into deg 3, thus creating a {33} (top) or {343} molecule (bottom). Crucially, all
the atoms with “?” must be deg 4, because MD is proper before applying (a), and because
applying (a) does not create {33} molecule in the bottom case. Then, after cutting the
{33} or{343} molecule, when viewed in each resulting component (which contains one “?”
atom), we create only one fixed end and turn the “?” atom from deg 4 to deg 3, and so on.
Now, with Lemma 11.8, we can finish Part 2 of the proof, i.e. each atom in MD is contained in an
elementary molecule. In fact, those cut in Definition 11.4 (2) clearly belong to {33} molecule. Those cut in
Definition 11.4 (3)–(4) also belong to {33} or{343} (and subsequently{3} or{33}) molecules by Lemma
11.8. Finally, those cut in Definition 11.4 (6) either belong to {3} molecule, or are cut in the process of
applying Definition 11.4 (3)–(4) and thus belong to {3} or{33} molecules by Lemma 11.8.
Proof part 3. With Parts 1 and 2 of the proof, we now know that every resulting molecule is elementary;
it suffices to calculate #{4} and #{33}. We first show that #{4} = 1. Since the first atom cut in Definition
11.4 (2) must have deg 4, it suffices to show that # {4}≤ 1. In fact, for each n∈ MU, at the time n is cut,
it must be the lowest remaining atom in MU, which means any possible child of n in MU must have been
cut before, creating a fixed end at n. Therefore, if n has any child in MU at all before any cutting, then it
cannot have deg 4 when it is cut.
On the other hand, before any cutting, MU can have at most one atom without child in MU (see Figure
27); if not, say there exist two such atoms n and n′, then consider a path ( n = n0,··· , nj = n′) between n
and n′ within MU (since MU is a tree). Then n1 must be parent of n as n has no child in MU, and then n2
must be parent of n1 as n1 has a child n∈ MU and another child in MD by Definition 11.2, and so on. In the
end n′ = nj must be parent of nj−1∈ MU, which contradicts the assumption on n′. This proves #{4} = 1.
Proof part 4. Finally we show that #{33}≥ (|MU|− 1)/5. Consider the bahevior of the quantity
λ := #deg3− #comp,

where #deg3 and #comp represent the number of (remaining) deg 3 atoms and components inMD respectively,
so that initially λ =−1 and finally λ = 0. Note that when viewed from MD, each step in Definition 11.4
(1)–(6) is one of the followings:
• Operation (a) or (b) defined above (which occur in Definition 11.4 (2));
• Cutting{33} or{343} molecule as free (which occur in (3)–(4) and (6) in Definition 11.4);
• Cutting{3} molecule as free (which occur in Definition 11.4 (6)).
Define the number of these operations to be # (a), #(b) and #cut{33} etc., then we have
#{33} = #(b) + #cut{33} + #cut{343}. (11.3)
Moreover, using Lemma 11.6 in the proof of Lemma 11.8 (and the fact that MD is proper right before
each (a) or (b) or cutting of {3}), it is easy to see that
• Operation (a) increased λ by 1, and operation (b) does not change λ;
• Cutting{33} or{343} decreases λ by 1, and cutting{3} does not change λ.
Using the initial and final values of λ we deduce that
#(a)− (#cut{33} + #cut{343}) = 1. (11.4)
Finally, consider the bonds between MU and MD (there are|MU| of them). Each such bond is broken either
in Definition 11.4 (2) (which corresponds to exactly one operation (a) or (b)), or in cutting one of {33} or
{343} molecules; moreover each (a), each (b), each cutting of {33} and each cutting of {343} can break at
most 1, 2, 3 and 4 such bonds respectively. This implies that # (a) + 2#(b) + 3#cut{33} + 4#cut{343}≥| MU|.
Combining this with (11.3) and (11.4), we deduce that # {33}≥ (|MU|− 1)/5, as desired. □
11.3. Toy model I plus. In this subsection we extend the toy model I to allow more general connections
between MU and MD.
Definition 11.9. We say M is a toy model I plus , if it is a 2-layer model as in Definition 11.1, and each
atom n∈ MU has at most one bond connecting to an atom in MD (i.e. # 2conn = 0), and the number of
connected components of X satisfies
#comp(X)≪|X|, (11.5)
where X is defined at the beginning of this note. Here, if two atoms in X can only be connected via atoms
in MU\X, then they belong to different components of X.
Let us explain the intuition behind Definition 11.9, in particular the role of (11.5). In the case of toy
model I, we have X = MU which is connected, hence # comp(X) = 1. Moreover, as in Part 3 of the proof of
Proposition 11.7, we see that MU has at most one lowest atom (i.e. atom without child in MU; in fact it is

===== PAGE 108 =====
108 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
not hard to see that MU must have an “upside down binary tree” shape). This latter fact allows us to apply
a “naive” strategy in MU where we always cut the lowest remaining atom (and avoid any top fixed ends),
without producing too many {4} atoms.
In the current case, MU may have many lowest atoms, so we cannot always choose and cut a lowest
remaining atom. Instead, we improve the strategy by choosing a lowest remaining deg 3 atom n (here we do
not consider deg 2 atoms, as they will always be lowest in MU and always have to be cut at top priority).
Now cutting n may create top fixed ends, but only at descendants of n (which all have deg 4 when n is
chosen). As such, we need to subsequently cut all descendants of n after cutting n. This leads to the idea of
the UP algorithm, see Definition 11.10 below, which is another basic ingredient in our proof.
Note, however, that this UP algorithm cannot be simply combined with the “greedy” algorithm in MD
as in Definition 11.4, because n may have (deg 4) descendants in MU, and this naive attempt may break
the key monotonicity property (see Part 1 of the proof of Proposition 11.14) and lead to non-elementary
molecules. As such, we should only apply UP at places in MU which will not affect MD, i.e. in MU\X.
Within X we still apply the naive strategy, which may lead to {4} molecules due to X.
We can then see the role of (11.5) and the dichotomy concerning #comp(X); in fact, same as in Part 3 of
the proof of Proposition 11.7, each component ofX can have at most one lowest atom . In the naive strategy,
this leads to {4} molecules whose number is comparable to # comp(X). Under the assumption (11.5), this
is negeligible compared to the number of good {33} molecules, which will be comparable to |X| (i.e. the
number of bonds between MU and MD).
With the above intuition, we start by defining the UP algorithm in the toy model case. For the full
version in the real problem, see Definition 12.1.
Definition 11.10 (The UP algorithm, toy model case) . Let M be any molecule which does not have top
fixed end. Define the following cutting sequence. For an example see Figure 28:
Figure 28. The UP algorithm (Definition 11.10). As in the above figures, the red box
indicates the atom(s) to be cut next, the green box indicates the atom(s) inSn (see Definition
11.10 (2)), which will be cut after cutting n. Note also when n and its parent n+ (or child
n−) form a{33} molecule, we should cut them together; see Definition 11.10 (3).
(1) If M has a deg 2 atom, then cut it as free. Repeat until M does not have deg 2 atoms.
(2) Choose a lowest deg 3 atom n that has not bee cut (or a lowest atom if M has no deg 3 atoms). Let
Sn be the set of descendants of n. This n and Sn is fixed until all atoms in Sn have been cut.

===== PAGE 109 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 109
(3) Starting from n, each time choose a highest atom m inSn that has not been cut. If m has deg 3 and
has a parent m+ or child m− that also has deg 3, then cut {m, m±} as free; otherwise cut m as free.
Repeat until all atoms in Sn have been cut, then go to (1)–(2) and choose the next n, and so on.
Remark 11.11. The UP algorithm is a combination of a “global low-to-high” (where each time we select the
lowest remaining deg 3 atom in Definition 11.10 (2)) and “local high-to-low” procedure (where each time we
cut the highest remaining atom in Sn in Definition 11.10 (3)). This leads to a key monotonicity property,
see Proposition 11.12.
The step (3) in Definition 11.10 may seem complicated, we explain it as follows: let m be the highest
remaining atom inSn, the simple option is to just cut m as free. However, in the case described in Definition
11.10 (3), the m± will become deg 2 once m is cut as free; moreover m−∈ Sn will be a highest remaining
atom in Sn, and m+̸∈Sn will occur as a deg 2 atom in Definition 11.10 (1) once all atoms in Sn have been
cut. As such, this deg 2 atom m± have to be cut at top priority, so instead of cutting {m} we can combine
this with the cutting of m±, which also forms a good {33} molecule{m, m±}. This then leads to the step
(3) in Definition 11.10.
We next prove a key monotonicity property of the UP algorithm and a sufficient condition for it to
produce at least one {33} molecule (in fact{33A} molecule). See Proposition 12.2 for the full version.
Proposition 11.12. Let M be as in Definition 11.10 and connected (otherwise consider each component of
M). Then after applying algorithm UP to M (and same for DOWN):
(1) We have the following monotonicity property, which we refer to as (MONO-toy): at any time, any
atom in ( M\Sn)∪{ n} has no top fixed end , and any atom in Sn\{n} has no bottom fixed end . In
particular, applying UP only produces elementary molecules.
(2) We have #{4}≤ 1, and #{4} = 1 if and only if M is full.
In (3) below we assume M has no deg 2 atoms.
(3) If M contains a cycle or contains at least two deg 3 atoms, then # {33}≥ 1. If not (i.e. M is a tree
and contains at most one deg 3 atom), then all the elementary molecules are {3} molecules with at
most one exception of {4} molecule.
Proof. Proof of (1). We first prove (MONO-toy), which implies all components are elementary (since each
atom is either cut by itself or belongs to a {33} molecule, due to (1)–(3) in Definition 11.10). Initially there
is no Sn and M has no top fixed end by assumption, so it suffices to prove that (MONO-toy) is perserved.
First, any deg 2 atom n cut in Definition 11.10 (1) must have two bottom fixed ends by (MONO-toy), so it
has no child remaining when it is cut, thus cutting it will not create any top fixed end .
Now let n andSn be as in Definition 11.10 (2), and m be a highest remaining atom in Sn as in Definition
11.10 (3). When n is chosen in Definition 11.10 (2), all atoms in Sn\{n} must have deg 4 by construction,
and thus have no bottom fixed end. Consider the operation of cutting m as free in Definition 11.10 (3). We
then know that that this cutting does not create any bottom fixed end inSn\{n} (as m is highest), and does
not create any top fixed end in (M\Sn)∪{ n} (as any child of m must belong to Sn\{n}, see Figure 29). The
same holds for the operation of cutting {m, m±}, because this is equivalent to cutting {m} and then cutting
a deg 2 atom (either m+̸∈Sn with two bottom fixed ends or m−∈Sn\{n} with two top fixed ends). This
proves that (MONO-toy) is perserved, as desired.
Proof of (2). Note that after the first cutting, M will always contain an atom that is not deg 4 (cf.
Remark 10.6). By construction, this means that the n chosen in Definition 11.10 (2) will always be deg 3;
moreover, any m∈Sn\{n} that is cut in Definition 11.10 (3) also cannot be deg 4, because m has a parent
in Sn which must have been cut before m thanks to the highest assumption of m. Thus only the first atom
can produce a possible {4} molecule, so #{4}≤ 1; moreover #{4} = 1 if and only if the first atom cut has
deg 4, i.e. when M is full.
Proof of (3). Suppose M has no deg 2 atom, we first prove that either # {33} ≥ 1, or all resulting
molecules are{4} and{3} (with #{4}≤ 1). If not, then we must have a {2} molecule. Consider the first

===== PAGE 110 =====
110 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
Figure 29. Illustration of the (MONO-toy) property of the UP algorithm (Proposition
11.12). Left: when we choose n in Definition 11.10 (2), there is no top fixed end outside
Sn\{n} and no fixed end at all in Sn\{n} (i.e. all deg 4). Right: when we cut a highest
remaining atom m in Sn, we only get top fixed end in Sn and bottom fixed end outside Sn.
atom m that has deg 2 when we cut it, and consider the cutting operation that turns m into deg 2, which
must be cutting some deg 3 atom p as free (this p cannot have deg 4, because a degree 4 atom is cut only
when no degree 3 atoms are present, and in this case cutting a degree 4 atom cannot produce a degree 2
atom). This cutting creates a fixed end at m, so this p must be a parent or child of m; moreover this m and
this p must both be deg 3 at the time of cutting p, but this violates Definition 11.10 (3).
Finally we prove #{33} = 0 if and only if M is a tree and has at most one deg 3 atom. For this, consider
the increment of the quantity σ := #bo/fr− 3#atom, where # bo/fr is the number of bonds plus the number
of free ends (not counting fixed ends) and # atom is the number of remaining atoms. It is easy to see that
cutting each{2},{3},{33} and{4} molecule increases σ by 1, 0, 1 and−1 respectively. Since finally σ = 0,
we see that #{4}− (#{2} + #{33}) =σinit (initial value of σ).
Note that initially # bo/fr = # deg− #bond where # deg is the total degree of all atoms (not counting
fixed ends) and # bond is the number of bonds (because each bond contributes twice to the degrees), so
σinit = (|M|− #bond)−| M3| where|M3| is the number of (initially) deg 3 atoms in M. Moreover by
connectedness we have #bond≥| M|− 1 with equality if and only if M is a tree, and note also #{4}∈{ 0, 1}
(and #{4} = 1 if and only if|M3| = 0 by the same proof in Part 2), it is then easy to see that #{2}+#{33} = 0
if and only if M is a tree and |M3|≤ 1. This completes the proof. □
Now we can define the algorithm for toy model I plus. As described in the above heuristics, this features
a naive algorithm (always cutting the lowest) in X, an UP algorithm in MU\X, and a greedy algorithm
in MD. Up to some simplifications, this is just the MAINUD algorithm, which is the main algorithm in
Section 13, see Definition 13.12. Note also that the part of Definition 11.13 involving MD is exactly the same
as Definition 11.4; the only difference occurs in MU.
Definition 11.13 (Algorithm for toy model I plus) . Let M be a toy model I plus with X be defined in the
beginning of this note (this X is fixed throughout the algorithm). We define the following cutting sequence.
For an example see Figures 30–31:
(1) If MU has a deg 2 atom, then cut it as free. Repeat until MU does not have deg 2 atoms.
(2) Consider the set of all (remaining) atoms that either belong to X, or has deg 3; choose n to be a
lowest atom in this set (if this set is empty then choose n to be any lowest atom). Let Sn be the set
of descendants of n in MU, and fix n and Sn until the end of (6).
(3) Starting from n, each time choose a highest atom p in Sn that has not been cut. If p is adjacent to
an atom q∈ MD which has deg 3, then cut {p, q} as free and cut q as free from {p, q} if p has deg
4; otherwise just cut p as free.

Figure 30. Algorithm for toy model I plus (first half) in Definition 11.13, applied to the
example in Figure 20. In addition to Figure 22, we use green box to indicate atom(s) in Sn
(see Definition 11.13 (2)) which will be cut after cutting n.
(4) If MD becomes non-proper, then perform the same steps (3)–(4) in Definition 11.4 until it becomes
proper again. Then go to (3) and repeat, until all atoms in Sn have been cut.
(5) When all atoms in Sn have been cut, go to (1)–(2) and choose the next n, and so on.
(6) When all atoms in MU have been cut, perform the same step (6) in Definition 11.4 to finish off MD.

Proposition 11.14. Let M be a toy model I plus, and we apply the algorithm defined in Definition 11.13.
Then in this process we only obtain elementary molecules, and we have
#{4}≤ #comp(X), #{33}≥ (|X|− 1)/5− #comp(X). (11.6)
Since|X|≳ρ≫ 1 and using (11.5), this clearly implies (11.1).
Proof. We divide the proof into 4 parts just as Proposition 11.7.
Proof part 1. We show that each atom in MU is contained in an elementary molecule. As in Part 1
of the proof of Proposition 11.12, this follows from the same (MONO-toy) property but for MU only: any
atom in ( MU\Sn)∪{ n} has no top fixed end , and any atom in Sn\{n} has no bottom fixed end.
To prove the monotonicity property, we first note that, for any n chosen in Definition 11.13 (2) and at
the time it is chosen, each atom in Sn\{n} has deg 4 and thus has no bottom fixed end. Then consider the
process of cutting in Definition 11.13 (3) and below, see Figure 32. Following exactly Part 1 of the proof
of Proposition 11.12, we see that cutting any atom in MU in this process does not create any top fixed end
in (MU\Sn)∪{ n} or any bottom fixed end in Sn\{n}; moreover, for the cutting of any atom in MD in this
process (such as the one in Definition 11.13 (4)), it only creates bottom fixed ends in MU, and only at atoms
in (MU\Sn)∪{ n}. This is because Sn\{n} contains no atom in X by construction, and thus has no bond

===== PAGE 113 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 113
connecting to MD, hence no fixed end can be created at any atom in this set when cutting anything in MD.
This proves (MONO-toy) for MU.
Figure 32. Part 1 of the proof of Proposition 11.14: the preservation of the monotonicity
property (just as for UP) in MU. This is essentially the same as the right half of Figure 29,
except the green bonds connecting MD to atoms in MU (those atoms must be in X). By
our choice,Sn\{n} is disjoint withX, so none of these bonds involve any atom in Sn (once
n has been cut). Thus, if we do any cutting in MD (as in Definition 11.13 (4)), this only
creates bottom ends outside Sn\{n}, hence preserving the monotonicity property in MU.
Proof part 2. We show that each atom in MD is contained in an elementary molecule. Since the part
of Definition 11.13 involving MD is exactly the same as Definition 11.4, this part is also exactly the same as
Part 2 of the proof of Proposition 11.7.
Proof part 3. We show that #{4}≤ #comp(X). Same as Part 2 of the proof of Proposition 11.12, we see
that any p∈Sn\{n} in Definition 11.13 (3) cannot have deg 4 when it is cut. Therefore, the only possible
{4} atoms must come from those n chosen in Definition 11.13 (2). By our choice in Definition 11.13 (2),
such n must belong to X and must be lowest atoms in X, i.e. it belongs to X but does not have any child
in X before any cutting.
Now we claim that any two lowest atoms in X must belong to different components of X (see Figure 27).
If not, say n and n′ are connected by a path ( n = n0,··· , nj = n′) in X, then arguing as Part 3 of the proof
of Proposition 11.7 and noticing that each atom in X has a child in MD, we deduce that each ni must be
parent of ni−1 which then leads to contradiction with properties of nj = n′. We then conclude that the
number of lowest atoms in X does not exceed # comp(X), hence #{4}≤ #comp(X).
Proof part 4. Finally we show that #{33}≥ (|X|− 1)/5− #comp(X). Since the part of Definition 11.13
involving MD is exactly the same as Definition 11.4, this part is essentially the same as Part 4 of the proof
of Proposition 11.7. There is only one difference: in the current case, each step of cutting {p, q} as free in
Definition 11.13 (3) (which corresponds to the operation (b) in Part 4 of the proof of Proposition 11.7 when
viewed in MD) may produce two{3} molecules instead of one{33} molecule, but this only happens when 13
p has deg 4, and the number of such exceptions is bounded by # comp(X) following Part 3 above. Therefore,
instead of (11.3) we have
#{33}≥ #(b) + #cut{33} + #cut{343}− #comp(X), (11.7)
and the same proof then yields that, instead of # {33}≥ (|X|− 1)/5 as in (11.2), we now have # {33}≥
(|X|− 1)/5− #comp(X). This completes the proof. □
13This cannot happen for toy model I, because there p can have deg 4 only when it is the first atom cut, in which case q
cannot have deg 3.

11.4. Toy model II. In this subsection we introduce the toy model II, which is opposite to toy model I
plus via the dichotomy, where we assume # comp(X)≳|X| instead of # comp(X)≪|X| in Definition 11.9.
Definition 11.15. We say M is a toy model II, if it satisfies all the assumptions in Definition 11.9 for toy
model I plus with the same notations, except that (11.5) is replaced by |X|≥ 2 and
#comp(X)≳|X|. (11.8)
Let us explain the strategy for toy model II, which will be completely different from toy models I and I
plus. Here we will not gain from the greedy algorithm in MD; instead we first cut all of MD as free without
exploiting any gain, and then gain from MU under the assumption (11.8). Note that, after cutting MD as
free, there will not be any deg 2 atoms in MU, and the deg 3 atoms in MU are exactly those in X.
The intuition is that, due to the lower bound of the number of components if X given by (11.8), the deg
3 atoms in MU are now separated by deg 4 atoms . Essentially, we expect that for such molecules, we can
design a cutting algorithm with minimal loss caused {4} molecules, such that each connected component of
the set of deg 3 atoms provides a good{33} molecule. In fact, this can be achieved by a clever choice in the
UP algorithm.
We next introduce the main algorithm in this case (together with an auxiliary lemma needed in this
algorithm). Up to simplifications, this is just the 3COMPUP algorithm in Definition 13.6.
Lemma 11.16. Suppose M is a molecule that is a tree, contains only deg 3 and 4 atoms and has no top
fixed end. Let the set of deg 3 atoms in M be M3 with|M3|≥ 2. Define a set A⊆ M to be no-bottom if any
child of any atom in A must also be in A (see Figure 33). Then, there always exists a connected, no-bottom
subset A⊆ M with|A∩ M3| = 2.
Figure 33. An example of connected, no-bottom sets in Lemma 11.16; this one is a union
of two intersecting descendant sets Sm and Sn. In general each such set is a union of
descendant sets Sn with n forming a connected set; see the proof of Lemma 11.16.
Proof. For any n∈ M, let Sn be the set of descendants of n (including n itself), then it is always connected
and no-bottom. If there exists n such that|Sn∩ M3|≥ 2, then choose a lowest such n. Since n has no top
fixed end, we know that either (i) n has one child n1, or (ii) n has deg 4 and has two children n1, n2. In
either case, by assumption we have |Snj∩ M3|≤ 1 for each j, which then implies |Sn∩ M3|≤ 2 because
Sn = (∪jSnj)∪{ n}. This implies |Sn∩ M3| = 2, as desired.
Now assume|Sn∩ M3|≤ 1 for each n∈ M. If |Sn∩ M3| = 1, there is a unique p = p(n)∈Sn∩ M3; now
consider all atom pairs (n, n′) such that p(n) and p(n′) both exist andare different (which exist because|M3|≥
2), and one such pair (n, n′) that are connected by a shortest path in M. Let this path be ( n0, n,··· , nj = n′),
then for each 0 < i < jwe must have either Sni∩ M3 = ∅ or p(ni) = p(n) (otherwise the pair ( n, ni)
corresponds to a shorter path, contradiction). Now let A =∪j
i=0Sni, it is clear that A is connected, no-
bottom and A∩ M3 ={p(n), p(n′)}, as desired. □

===== PAGE 115 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 115
Definition 11.17 (The 3COMPUP algorithm, toy model case) . Let M be a molecule that is a tree,
contains only deg 3 and 4 atoms and has no top fixed end. Let the set of deg 3 atoms in M be M3 with
|M3|≥ 2. Define the following cutting sequence. For an example see Figure 34:
Figure 34. Algorithm for toy model II, applied to the example in Figure 20. Here we first
cut all of MD as free. Then, each time we choose a connected, no-bottom subset A that
contains exactly two deg 3 atoms, see Definition 11.17 ((1)). We then apply UP to cut all
atoms inA (cf. Figure 28 for the application of UP) and repeat. Note that each application
of UP guarantees at least one{33} molecule.
(1) Apply Lemma 11.16 to M and select a connected no-bottom subset A. This set is fixed until the end
of (5).
(2) Choose a lowest deg 3 atom n in A that has not been cut. Let Sn be the set of descendants of n.
This n and Sn are fixed until the end of (3).
(3) Starting from n, each time choose a highest atom m inSn that has not been cut. If m has deg 3 and
has a parent m+ or child m− that also has deg 3, then cut {m, m±} as free; otherwise cut m as free.
Repeat until all atoms in Sn have been cut.
(4) If M has deg 2 atom, then cut it; repeat until M has no deg 2 atoms. Then go to (2) and choose the
next n, and so on.

===== PAGE 116 =====
116 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
(5) When all atoms in A have been cut, we subsequently cut all deg 2 atoms as in (4), and then cut all
components of M with only one deg 3 atom using UP.
(6) Go to (1) and select the next A from one of the remaining components of M, and so on.
Remark 11.18. Let us explain the ideas in Lemma 11.16 and Definition 11.17. Note that steps (2)–(4) in
Definition 11.17 are exactly the same as UP but applied to a particular set A, so we can view 3COMPUP
as a clever way of carrying out UP. The role of the set A is explained as follows: first it is connected
(otherwise we can cut each of its components separately); it should also be no-bottom, because when cutting
any atom we should also cut its descendants to guarantee the monotonicity property. Finally we need it to
contain two deg 3 atoms so that this sequence of cutting will produce at least one {33} molecule, and we
need exactly two deg 3 atoms so that we do not waste too many components of the deg-3-atom set (which
are the key objects leading to the gain).
Proposition 11.19. Let M be a toy model II. We first cut MD as free and cut it into elementary molecules
by DOWN (i.e. the dual of UP with all directions reversed), then apply 3COMPUP (Definition 11.17)
to MU. Then all resulting components are elementary, and we have
#{4} = 1, #{33}≥ #comp(X)/10. (11.9)
Since|X|≳ρ≫ 1 and using (11.8), this clearly implies (11.1).
Proof. Note that steps (2)–(4) in Definition 11.17 are exactly the same as UP, so the proof that all molecules
are elementary (as well as # {4} = 1 which comes only from MD) follows in the same way as Proposition
11.12. We now focus on the proof that # {33}≥ #comp(X)/10.
For any molecule M, define # comp(M) to be the number of components of M, and # 3comp(M) to be the
number of components of the set of deg 3 atoms in M, and
λ(M) := 1
5#comp(M) + 1
10#3comp(M). (11.10)
In Definition 11.17 (applied to MU), the whole algorithm consists of finitely many loops where one loop is
formed by steps (1)–(5) in Definition 11.17. Consider each such loop which reduces M to M′, we shall prove
the following:
(♣) If M is a tree, has no top fixed end and contains only deg 3 and 4 atoms with at least two deg 3
atoms, then each component of M′ satisfies the same property. Moreover for this loop we have
#{33}≥λ(M)−λ(M′). (11.11)
Note that adding up (11.11) leads to the desired lower bound of #{33} (initially MU has no deg 2 atoms nor
top fixed end, and # 3comp(MU) = #comp(X)), thus it suffices to prove ( ♣) for a given loop. We divide the
proof into 4 parts.
Proof part 1. We prove that each component of M′ satisfies the desired properties. This is obvious, as
the lack of top fixed ends follows from the same proof as Proposition 11.12, and the lack of deg 2 atoms and
components with only one deg 3 atom follows from the cuttings done in Definition 11.17 (5).
Proof part 2. Now we need to prove (11.11). We start by making some preparations. Note that A is a
connected subset of M; by Lemma 11.6 in the proof of Lemma 11.8, each atom m∈ M\A that is connected
to an atom n = n(m) in A by a pre-loop bond e corresponds to a unique component Xm after cutting the
atoms in A as free, see Figure 35 (the loop will in fact cut as free more atoms). Note also that m must be a
parent of n due to the no-bottom property of A.
Let M3 be the set of deg 3 atoms in M (before executing the loop), and consider all components of M3
that intersect A or are connected to A by a bond. We know A has exactly two atoms in M3; apart from
the one or two components that contain these two atoms, each component under consideration must be a
subset of a unique Xm where m has deg 3 and n = n(m) has deg 4 (call it Ym). Let B be the union of these
components with A (Figure 35). We claim that after steps (1)–(4) in Definition 11.17 and cutting the deg 2
atoms in Definition 11.17 (5), exactly those atoms in B have been cut as free.

nothing else (as cutting B only turns deg 4 atoms into deg 3 but does not produce further
deg 2 atom).
In fact, the atoms A must have been cut by definition. For any atom in the M3 components forming B,
by definition it is connected to an atom in A by a chain of deg 3 atoms; once the atom in A has been cut, the
atoms on this chain will successively become deg 2, so they must have been cut (either in Definition 11.17
(4)–(5), or as part of {33} molecule in Definition 11.17 (3)). On the other hand, each atom in M\B has at
most one bond connecting to B (as B is connected and M is a tree), and must be deg 4 if it has one such
bond (otherwise it would belong to one of the M3 components forming B), so each atom in M\B will have
deg 3 or 4 after cutting B as free, thus it will not be cut in Definition 11.17 (1)–(4) or in cutting the deg 2
atoms in Definition 11.17 (5).

the quantity σ defined there but within A, we can show that, apart from at most two exceptions, each deg
4 atom in A will have deg 3 when it is cut.

by at most two m (hence at most two components), we conclude that

11.5. Toy model III. In this subsection we introduce the toy model III. Note that in both toy models I
plus and II, we are assuming the absence of 2-connections, i.e. # 2conn = 0. Now we define the toy models
III, which are (essentially) opposite to toy models I plus and II via the dichotomy, where we assume 14
#2conn≳|X| instead of # 2conn = 0.
14There is also the case 0 < #2conn≪| X|; in this case we will first cut all such 2-connections, which generates an error
which is eventually negligible but still requires some extra care; see Section 11.7.4 for more discussions.

===== PAGE 119 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 119
Figure 37. Part 4 of the proof of Proposition 11.19. Left: suppose we cut B and are left
with all the components as in Lemma 11.6. Within each such component, a fixed end is
created, turning a deg 4 atom into deg 3. Right: for each component, turning one deg 4
atom into deg 3 may decrease the number of components of the set of deg 3 atoms
by 2 (by joining 3 such components into 1 with the new deg 3 atom), but no more. Note
that the blue circles here are unrelated to the blue circles in Figures 35–36 and represent

Definition 11.20. We say M is a toy model III, if it is a simplified 2-layer model in Definition 11.1, and
#2conn≳|X|, (11.16)
where #2conn and X are defined at the beginning of this note.
The strategy for toy models III is simple: we work in MD as if MU were absent, and apply the DOWN
algorithm (the dual of UP) to it. Now consider any 2-connection n∈ MU, which will become deg 2 when
all atoms in MD have been cut. Assume n is adjacent to two atoms m1, m2∈ MD, and assume m1 is cut
before m2 in the DOWN algorithm applied to MD. Then, right before m2 is cut, this m2 will have deg 3
(say) by Proposition 11.12; also since m1 has been cut at this time, now n must also have deg 3, which leads
to a{33} molecule, so we simply cut it and enjoy the gain. This way we guarantee that each 2-connection
belongs to a {33} molecule, which is sufficient under the assumption (11.16). We shall refer to this as the
2CONNDN algorithm (and the dual version 2CONNUP), see Definition 13.1 for the full version (which
is more complicated due to the various necessary additions including the O-atoms).
Definition 11.21 (Algorithm for toy model III) . Let M be toy model III. We define the following cutting
sequence. For an example see Figure 38:
(1) If MD has a deg 2 atom, then cut it as free. Repeat until MD does not have deg 2 atoms.
(2) Choose a highest deg 3 atom n in MD that has not bee cut (or a highest atom if MD has no deg 3
atoms). Let Zn be the set of ancestors of n in MD. This n andZn is fixed until all atoms in Zn have
been cut.
(3) Starting from n, each time choose a lowest atom m in Zn that has not been cut. If m has deg 3 and
has a parent p∈ MU that also has deg 3, then cut {m, p} as free; otherwise cut m as free. Repeat
until all atoms in Zn have been cut, then go to (1)–(2) and choose the next n, and so on.
(4) When all atoms in MD have been cut, finish off MU by applying the UP algorithm.
Note that steps (1)–(3) in Definition 11.21 are essentially just DOWN (i.e. the dual of UP) applied to
MD, except we propritize the {33} molecules formed by an atom in MD with an atom in MU.
Proposition 11.22. Let M be toy model III. Then, after applying the cutting sequence defined in Definition
11.21, all molecules we get are elementary, and we have
#{4} = 1, #{33}≥ 1
2· #2conn. (11.17)

120 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
Figure 38. Algorithm for toy model III, applied to the example in Figure 20. Here we
perform DOWN which is the dual of UP in MD, so in Definition 11.10 (2) in UP algorithm
we choose the highest deg 3 atom instead of the lowest, and in in Definition 11.10 (3) in UP
algorithm we choose the lowest remaining atom instead of the highest, etc. Here green box
indicates atoms in Zn (the set of ancestors of n), and when an atom n∈ MD forms a{33}
molecule with an atom m∈ MU we always cut it. In the end we see that each 2-connection
(pink atom) belongs to one {33} molecule, as desired. As the gain is already enough, the
rest can be cut into elementary molecules using UP in MU and DOWN in MD.
Proof. Since steps (1)–(3) in Definition 11.21 are essentially the same as in DOWN, it is easy to prove all
molecules we get are elementary and #{4} = 1, in the same way as the proof of Proposition 11.12. Now we
only need to prove that #{33}≥ #2conn/2.
Let n∈ MU be any 2-connection, which is connected to two atoms m1, m2∈ MD by two bonds. Assume
m1 is cut before m2 in Definition 11.21 (they cannot be cut at the same time, as Definition 11.21 (3) does
not provide an option to cut a {33} molecule with both atoms in MD). Arguing in the same way as in Part
2 of the proof of Proposition 11.12, we know that m2 cannot have deg 4 when it is cut. On the other hand,
note that MD is a tree initially without deg 3 atoms; by arguing in the same way as in Part 4 of the proof
of Proposition 11.12 and calculating the quantity σ defined there but within MD, we conclude that m2 also
cannot have deg 2 when it is cut.
Therefore, m2 must have deg 3 when it is cut; at this time n must also have deg 3 because m1 has been cut,
m2 has not, and n also has two top edges (not fixed ends) that are not affected, see Figure 39. By Definition
11.21 (3) we conclude that m2 must belong to a {33} molecule, which means that n must be adjacent with
an MD atom in some{33} molecule. Since this is true for each 2-connection, and each {33} molecule may
correspond to at most two 2-connections in this way (i.e. given the {33} molecule there can be at most two
n that are adjacent with the MD atom in this{33} molecule), we conclude that #{33}≥ #2conn/2. □

11.6. Reduction to 2-layers: layer selection. In Sections 11.2–11.5 we have considered the 2-layer
models in Definition 11.1. In fact, this two-layer case already captures most of the essential difficulties in

constant) to the 2-layer case. This is refered to as layer selection, which we define as follows; for the precise
definition see Definition 12.12.
