MU are above that forming MD).
We next show that MU is a forest. In fact, each component of M(ℓD:ℓU) must intersect a particle line in
r(MℓU−1) (it must intersect a particle line in p(Mℓ′) for some ℓ′ < ℓU by Proposition 10.4 (5); by applying
Proposition 10.4 (7) we know that this component must also intersect a particle line in r(Mℓ′)⊆p(Mℓ′+1),
by repeating this and using Remark 10.5, we eventually get to a particle line in r(MℓU−1)). As there is no
exceptional component in Definition 11.23 (3), we know that each component of M(ℓD:ℓU) is a tree by itself,
and intersects a unique particle line in r(MℓU ). Since MℓU is a forest by itself, we see that MU = M(ℓD:ℓU] is
also a forest (namely, if we start with a forest, and attach a number of trees to it with each tree connected
to the original forest by a unique edge, then the result is still a forest).
We next show that the number of components of MD does not exceed sℓU , which is≪ρ′′ because
sℓU≤C−1·sℓU−1 =C−1·ρ′≤C−1· (C′)ℓ·ρ′′≪ρ′′

===== PAGE 123 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 123
by (11.20) andC≫ (C′)ℓ. In fact, by the same arguments for M(ℓD:ℓU) as above, we see that each component
of M(ℓD:ℓU] must intersect a particle line in r(MℓU ), so the number of these components is bounded by
|r(MℓU )| =sℓU by Remark 10.5.
Finally, consider each of the ρ′′ = v∗
ℓD particle lines that is connected to either a particle line in in
r(MℓU−1) or to a cycle within M[ℓD:ℓU), say p. By definition there exists a path going from an atom on
p to an atom on either a cycle or another particle line in r(MℓU−1). This path must enter MℓD, because
the above-stated property of p will not hold without layer ℓD; moreover it also must exist MℓD (including
exiting MD when getting onto another particle line in r(MℓU−1)), because MD by itself does not contain a
cycle. By considering the first entry and first exit at MD, we obtain two bonds between MU and MD that
are connected within MD; moreover the first entry bond must be connected to p via M(ℓD:ℓU), and is thus
in bijection with p. This proves the desired result, namely the existence of at least ρ′′ bonds between MU
and MD, each of which being connected to another by a path within MD. □
Remark 11.25. We make two remarks concerning Definition 11.23 and Proposition 11.24.
(1) In Definition 11.23, we focus on MUD = MU∪ MD = M[ℓD:ℓU] and ignore other layers. In practice,
we need to cut M[ℓD:ℓU] as free from M and cut the other layers into elementary components (first
M>ℓU using UP, then M<ℓD using DOWN). This may create some{4} molecules, but their number
is easily bounded using Proposition 11.12 and the upper bound on the number of components of
M>ℓU and M (see Definition 12.14 and Proposition 12.15).
(2) Compared to Definition 11.1, the MU and MD in Proposition 11.24 are forests instead of trees.
However, the number of components of MU is≪ ρ′′ by Proposition 11.24 and is thus negligible;
on the other hand, MD may have many components, but this is compensated by the fact that the
ρ′′ bonds between MU and MD in Proposition 11.24 are connected within MD. Indeed, in the case
when MD has many components, this (in fact a related property of MD, see Proposition 12.15 (3b))
allows us to first cut MU as free and gain from subsequently cutting MD, see Proposition 13.3.
Remark 11.26. In practice, there are two complications to layer selection: first, if some single layer Mℓ′
contains cycles (without simplification (2) in Section 11.1), then we need an extra layer refining process
(see Definition 12.12), which is discussed in Section 11.7.2. Second, if v∗
ℓD+1̸= 0 (i.e. there are exceptional
components in Definition 11.23 (3), though their number is still ≪ ρ′′ and negligible), we need to first cut
them as free before defining MU and MD (Definition 12.14). This will create some {4} molecules but their
number is bounded (see Propositions 12.13 and 12.15), and will also introduce fixed ends to MU and MD
which necessitates the pre-processing step, see discussions in Section 11.7.4.
11.7. Additions in the general case. In this subsection, we discuss the additional “accessories” needed
to address the general case, without the simplifications (1)–(2) in Section 11.1. These should be viewed
as branches that are attached to the main thread of proof, which are less essential but necessary. These
accessories are listed in Table 2, and will be discussed in Sections 11.7.1–11.7.4 below.
11.7.1. O-atoms and ov-segments. First, in the real problem, the molecule M may contain O-atoms, and the
cutting operation in this case is more complicated (see Definition 8.3 (2) compared to Definition 8.3 (1)).
As we already see in Definition 8.3, the notion of ov-segments is important in the analysis of O-atoms,
and should be viewed as a generalization of edges/bonds. In Definition 8.4, we have introduced the notions
of being ov-adjacent, ov-parent and ov-child, and ov-components etc., which are direct generalizations of
adjacent, parent and child, and connected components. Fortunately, in almost all cases, this generalization
does not cause any essential change to the proof; we simply translate every sentence in the proofs in Sections
11.2–11.5 into the language of ov-segments, with “adjacent” replaced by “ov-adjacent” and “components”
replaced by “ov-components”, etc. As an example, by replacing “connected” with “ov-connected” and
“bond” with “ov-segment” in Lemma 11.6, we obtain the following statement, which is just Lemma 13.4:

===== PAGE 124 =====
124 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
• Suppose we cut S⊆ M as free, where S is an ov-connected subset of M and M is a tree. Then, for
each atom q∈ M\S that is connected to an atom in S by a (pre-cutting) ov-segment e, the post-
cutting molecule MD has exactly one component Xq corresponding to q. Moreover, when viewed in
Xq, the operation of cutting S turns exactly one free end at q into a fixed end.
11.7.2. Reducing each layer to a tree: layer refining. In both the toy models in Sections 11.2–11.5 and layer
selection in Section 11.6, it is crucial that each layer Mℓ′ of M is a forest. In reality, to deal with the case
where some Mℓ′ contains cycles, we need to define the layer refining process as follows.
Recall (Proposition 10.4 (3)–(4)) that each Mℓ′ is formed by joining finitely many C-molecules by O-
atoms, such that each C-molecule has circuit rank ρ(W )≤ Γ, and joining these C-molecules by O-atoms
does not add any cycle. We start with the following simple intuitive lemma:
• For each molecule M with at most Γ independent cycles, we can always divide it into at most Γ + 1
subsets Mj (1≤ j≤ Γ + 1), such that each subset is a tree, and no atom in Mi can be parent of
atom in Mj for i<j .
For the proof of the above lemma, see Lemma 12.7. Now, to complete the layer refining process for each
single layer Mℓ′, we need to delete some O-atoms to ensure each component of Mℓ′ has ρ value at most Γ,
and then apply this lemma to divide it into at most Γ + 1 thin layers, such that each thin layer is now a
forest. For details see Definition 12.8 and Proposition 12.9. This then allows us to repeat the layer selection
process in Section 11.6; the use of thin layers instead of layers leads to one extra step in layer selection, but
this is easily adjusted, see Definition 12.12.
We remark that the above layer refining process does require the total number of independent cycles in
these single-layer C-molecules to be negligible (compared to ρ). When this total number of cycles is large,
we can perform an alternative cutting sequence that exploits these cycles, see Propositions 12.10 and 12.11.
This requires layer refining of a subset of layers and also leads to a minor auxiliary notion of primitivity, see
Definition 12.4 and Lemma 12.6.
11.7.3. Strong and weak degeneracies. In Definition 11.1 we have assumed that all{33} molecules are good.
In reality there may be exceptions to this when the (xe,ve) for fixed ends e at this{33} molecule takes some
very specific values, as stated in the definition of good molecules (Definition 9.4). For example, a {33A}
molecule will not be good, if
|xe1−xe7|≤ ε1−υ and |ve1−ve7|≤ ε1−υ, (11.21)
where e1 and e7 are the two fixed ends of the {33} molecule.
As such, we define a strong degeneracy to be an instance where (11.21) happens at two edges that would
become the two fixed ends if we cut the two atoms as a {33A} molecule; see Definition 12.4. Similarly we
can define the notion of weak degeneracy associated with the {33B} molecules, which involves one or two
atoms and some conditions opposite to the ones stated in Definition 9.4; see Definition 12.16. Note that
these definitions only involve certain edges and atoms in M, and is not a priori associated with any concrete
cutting sequence. This provides the flexibility, so that we can classify the degeneracies and non-degeneracies
before performing any cutting, and perform different cutting operations according to different cases, which
allows us to minimize the need of backtracking.
Our strategy is that, when there are many degeneracies, we shall perform an alternative cutting sequence,
where we do not gain from {33} molecules but instead gain from the extra degeneracy assumptions such
as (11.21). Compared to the sophisticated algorithms exploiting {33} molecules, this alternative cutting
sequence essentially follows the simple procedure where we cut every atom by itself, so that most of them are
cut as{3} molecules. Usually integrating a {3} molecule does not gain any power, but if this {3} molecule
contains an edge or atom that is involved in some degeneracy condition like (11.21), then it is clear that this
{3} molecule integration should gain some power, which makes it a good molecule, see Definition 9.4.
In practice, there will be two places in the overall algorithm where we need to consider degeneracies:
the first time for strong degeneracies, see Proposition 12.5 and the second time for weak degeneracies, see

===== PAGE 125 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 125
Proposition 12.17. If there are many (strong or weak) degeneracies, then we can apply the alternative cutting
sequence to conclude; if there are few (strong and weak) degeneracies, then their effect will be negligible and
we can get rid of them and proceed with the main thread of our algorithm.
11.7.4. Additional ingredients and pre-processing. Finally, we discuss a few minor ingredients that are needed
in the full case, that are not discussed in Sections 11.7.1–11.7.3 above.
(a) Initial cumulants. In this section we have assumedr(M0) =H0 = ∅ in Proposition 10.4; in reality this
H0 may exist, which corresponds to the initial (time t = 0) cumulants. This may affect the layer selection
process, as it is now possible in Proposition 10.4 (9) that most particle lines in r(Mℓ′) form initial links
instead of being connected to other particle lines or cycles. In this case we simply exploit the initial link
condition to obtain good {3} molecules (see Definition 9.4); the will be quite easy, see Proposition 12.17.
(b) Cutting exceptional components. Recall the notion of exceptional components in Definition 11.23 (3);
in Section 11.6 we have assumed there is no such exceptional components, soMU = MℓU∪M(ℓD:ℓU) is a forest
(Proposition 11.24). In general, when such exceptional components exist (but with a negligible number),
we need to first cut them as free, before defining MU and MD which can be forests. This cutting process
is described in Definition 12.14; it may create some {4} molecules, but their number can be bounded in
Propositions 12.13 and 12.15. It may also introduce fixed ends to MD, which then requires the pre-processing
step to treat, see (d) below.
(c) Cutting 2-connections. Recall that in the 2-layer models, there is a case when the number of 2-
connections satisfies 0 < #2conn≪| X| (see Section 11.5), which is not covered in any toy model. In this
case we first cut all these 2-connections as free, so no more 2-connections remain in the resulting molecule;
this corresponds to Stage 1 in the proof of Proposition 12.18 in Section 13.5. After this, there will be fixed
ends in MD similar to (b) above, so we should apply the extra pre-processing step (see (d) below).
(d) Pre-processing. In Definition 11.1, we have assumed that the 2-layer molecule M has no fixed ends;
however, in reality, such fixed ends may exist as shown in (b)–(c) above, which also leads to fixed ends and
deg 3 atoms after we subsequently cut all deg 2 atoms. In fact those fixed ends in MU will not matter as
there will not be any top fixed ends; however the fixed ends in MD may affect the properness of MD (which
is needed in the MAINUD algorithm) and needs to be addressed.
In this case, depending on the number of components of deg 3 atoms in MD (similar to the dichotomy in
Sections 11.3–11.4), we either apply 3COMPDN (i.e. the dual of 3COMPUP) in MD similar to Section
11.4 with enough gain (this corresponds to Stage 2 in Section 13.5, or apply an extra pre-processing step to
turn MD into a proper molecule (this corresponds to Stage 3) . The pre-processing depends on a specific
process SELECT, see Definition 13.10 and Proposition 13.11.
After pre-processing, we are then ready to apply the dichotomy in Sections 11.3–11.4, to reduce to either
toy model II (corresponding to Stage 5), or toy model I plus (corresponding to Stage 6). Finally, note that
the pre-processing step may also cause some complications, as the set X involved in the dichotomy needs to
be replaced by some natural modification X1, which will be explained in details in the MAINUD, see the
definition of X0 and X1 in Definition 13.12, and Stage 4.
11.7.5. Strategy flowchart. We present a flowchart summarizing the proof of Proposition 9.7, see Figure 41.
12. Cutting algorithm I: reduction to UD molecules
In this section and Section 13 we will construct the cutting algorithm in Proposition 9.7. As already
mentioned in the beginning of Section 11, these algorithms and arguments will be presented in a different
order from Section 11, to be consistent with the logical flow of the proof. The goal of this section is to prove
several cases of Proposition 9.7, namely Propositions 12.3, 12.5, 12.11 and 12.17, and reduce Proposition
9.7 to Proposition 12.18, by getting rid of degeneracies (Propositions 12.5 and 12.17), introducing layer
refinement (Section 12.3) and layer selection (Section 12.4).

===== PAGE 126 =====
126 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
Figure 41. Yes/No flow chart for the proof of Proposition 9.7. Here each green box in-
dicates end of a proof path; each “Do” describes the algorithm, process or steps we take,
and “Define” indicates the new notions defined. The notions of strongly degenerate, prim-
itive and weakly degenerate (abbreviated str. deg., prm. and wea. deg.) pairs are defined
in Definitions 12.4 and 12.16; the other notions are defined at the place indicated. The
“many/large/small” etc. are all relative to ρ; for the precise threshold of these statements,
see the corresponding propositions.
12.1. The UP algorithm and the large|H| case. In this subsection, we prove the first case of Proposition
9.7, namely Proposition 12.3, where we assume |H| is large. We start by introducing the full version
of algorithm UP, which is a direct extension of its toy model version in Definition 11.10, by replacing
parent/child with ov-parent/ov-child (cf. Section 11.7.1). This is a general purpose or “default” algorithm
that minimizes the number of {4} molecules, which also guarantees sufficiently many good molecules only
in some cases. However, this algorithm alone is enough to treat the simple scenarios studied in this section.
Moreover, even in later cases where other algorithms are needed to achieve gain in a key subset of the

===== PAGE 127 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 127
molecule, it is usually helpful to use UP (or its dual DOWN) to finish the decomposition of the remaining
parts of the molecule without loss.
Definition 12.1 (The algorithm UP). Define the following cutting algorithm UP. It takes as input any
(regular) molecule M, which has no top fixed end at any C-atom (we henceforth abbreviate it as C-top
fixed ends; same for C-bottom fixed ends ). For any such M, we define the cutting sequence as follows:
(1) If M contains any deg 2 atom n, then cut it as free, and repeat until there is no deg 2 atom left.
(2) Consider the set of all remaining deg 3 atoms in M (i.e. those deg 3 that have not been cut), and
choose a lowest atom n in this set. If M has no deg 3 atoms, then choose a lowest atom n in M. Let
Sn be the set of descendants of n (including n); this n and Sn will be fixed until the end of (3).
(3) Starting from n, each time choose a highest remaining atom m in Sn. If m has deg 3, and either an
ov-parent m+ or an ov-child m− of m also has deg 3, then cut{m, m+} or{m, m−} as free; otherwise
cut m as free. Repeat until all atoms in Sn have been cut. Then go to (1).
We also define the dual algorithm DOWN, by reversing the notions of parent/child, lowest/highest,
top/bottom etc. It applies to any molecule that has no C-bottom fixed end.
The next proposition is the full version of Proposition 11.12.
Proposition 12.2. Let M be any molecule as in Definition 12.1. We may assume M is connected (otherwise
consider each component of M). Then after applying algorithm UP to M (and same for DOWN):
(1) We have the following monotonicity property (MONO): throughout the process of UP, the set
Sn\{n} will not have any C-bottom fixed end, and ( M\Sn)∪{ n} will not have any C-top fixed end
(in particular, when all atoms in Sn have been cut and before the next n is chosen in Definition 12.1
(2), there is no C-top fixed end in whole M). In particular, the molecule M is cut into elementary
molecules, and #{33B} = #{44} = 0.
(2) We have #{4}≤ 1, and #{4} = 1 if and only if M is full.
In (3)–(4) below we assume M has no deg 2 atoms.
(3) If M contains a cycle or contains at least two deg 3 atoms, then # {33A}≥ 1. If not (i.e. M is a tree
and contains at most one deg 3 atom), then all the elementary molecules are {3} molecules with at
most one exception which might be a {4} molecule.
(4) If M contains at most one deg 3 atom and contains a cycle, then we can split into 2 sub-cases such
that for each sub-case we have # good≥ 1.
Proof. We first make some comments on the result and the proof. Here, compared to (MONO-toy) in
Proposition 11.12, the (MONO) property changes the absence of fixed ends to the absence of C-fixed ends,
because of the presence of O-atoms (we do not need to worry about fixed ends at O-atoms because they
cannot affect any other atom; see Remark 8.7).
The main new ingredient compared to Proposition 11.12 is (4), which guarantees a good molecule instead
of just a{33A} molecule, and is thus useful in strongly degenerate cases (Definition 12.4, Proposition 12.5)
where{33A} molecules might not be good. The proof of (4) also provides an example how the splitting
into sub-cases in Proposition 9.7 works, by decomposing 1 into suitable indicator functions with prescribed
supports. Apart from these, the proof is essentially the same as Proposition 11.12.
Proof of (1). We first prove (MONO). Note that this statement is true initially, as M has no C-top
fixed end. Moreover at each time of choosing n in Definition 12.1 (2), there is also no C-bottom fixed end
in Sn\{n}, because all atoms in Sn\{n} must have deg 4 (since n is the lowest deg 3 atom). Therefore, it
suffices to show that (MONO) is preserved. For this, first note that if n has deg 2 as in Definition 12.1 (1),
and has no C-top fixed end, then cutting n as free will only generate bottom fixed ends at its ov-parents (cf.
Remark 8.7), so the absence of C-top fixed end in the whole molecule is preserved.
Next, suppose n is chosen as in Definition 12.1 (2), andm is a highest remaining atom inSn as in Definition
12.1 (3). If we cut m as free, this does not create any C-bottom fixed end in Sn\{n} (because m is a highest

===== PAGE 128 =====
128 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
remaining atom, so any ov-parent of m inSn\{n} has already been cut), and does not create any C-top fixed
end in ( M\Sn)∪{ n} (because any ov-child atom of m must again be in Sn\{n}), so (MONO) is preserved.
The same can be proved for the operation of cutting {m, m+} or{m, m−}; let us consider{m, m+} as the
other case is similar. Here, cutting {m, m+} may create bottom fixed ends at ov-parents of m and m+, but
none of them belong to Sn\{n} (so (MONO) is not affected), because m is a highest remaining atom (so no
ov-parent of m still remains in Sn, in particular m+̸∈Sn and no ov-parent of m+ can be in Sn). Moreover,
cutting{m, m+} may create top fixed ends at ov-children of m and m+, but they all have to be in Sn\{n}
(so again (MONO) is not affected). This last statement is obvious for m as m∈Sn. As for m+, since it has
deg 3, has no C-top fixed end, and has one ov-child being m, we know it must have a bottom fixed end (or is
O-atom, cf. Remark 8.7). Since m is ov-child of m+, we know that any other ov-child of m+ must belong to
the maximal ov-segment containing m+ and m. Using the structure of ov-segments, we know that any other
ov-child of m+ that is C-atom must occur at the bottom of this ov-segment, thus it must be an ov-child of
m and belong to Sn\{n}. This shows (MONO) is preserved in the whole process, thus it is true for all time.
Now, note that by construction, any atom in M is either cut by itself, or belongs to a {33} molecule.
By (MONO), all the single-atom molecules we cut must have deg at least 2, and those of deg 2 must have
two fixed ends either both being top or both being bottom (or non-serial for O-atoms, due to regularity).
Similarly, all the{33} molecules{m, m+} and{m, m−} cut in Definition 12.1 (3) must be {33A}, because at
the time it is cut, (MONO) implies that the m+̸∈Sn must be an ov-parent of m with no C-top fixed end,
and m−∈Sn\{n} must be an ov-child of m with no C-bottom fixed end (in particular, if we cut m as free
from{m, m±}, then{m±} will become an elementary {2} molecule). This proves that all molecules we cut
are elementary, and #{33B} = #{44} = 0.
Proof of (2). If M has only deg 4 atoms, then the first cutting in M must create a {4} molecule; at
any time thereafter, M will contain at least one non-deg-4 atom (cf. Remark 10.6), and by Definition 12.1
(2)–(3), any subsequent cutting will not create any{4} molecule (the n chosen in Definition 12.1 (2) must
have deg 3; also any m∈ Sn\{n} will have deg at most 3 when it is cut in Definition 12.1 (3), because m
has an ov-parent in Sn that has already been cut before). The same holds true for any M that contains
non-deg-4 atoms initially, in which case we do not get any {4} molecule. This proves (2).
Proof of (3). Here and in the proof of (4) below we assume M has no deg 2 atoms. We claim that if
#{33A} = 0, then necessarily also #{2} = 0. Assume the contrary, consider the first atom m that has deg 2
when it is cut, and consider the cutting operation that turns m into deg 2, which must be cutting a single
atom p (otherwise p would belong to a {33A} molecule). Note that m must have deg 3 at the time p is cut
(because cutting p only generates one fixed end at m, see Proposition 10.4 (2)), and p also has deg 3 or 4
when it is cut (it cannot have deg 2 because m is the first). If p has deg 4 when it is cut (so {p} is cut as
a{4} molecule), then by the proof in (2) we know that all atoms must have deg 4 initially, and p must the
first atom cut; but that would imply that m has deg 4 at the time p is cut, contradiction.
We now know that{p} is cut as a {3} molecule, and both p and m have deg 3 at the time p is cut. As p
must be an ov-parent or ov-child of m at this time (because cutting p creates a fixed end at m), by Definition
12.1 (3), we know that we should have cut p together with another atom instead of cutting it alone as a {3}
molecule, contradiction.
Next, define σ := #bo/fr− 3|M| and consider its increment under cutting operations, where # bo/fr is the
number of bonds plus the number of free ends (not counting fixed ends), and|M| is the number of (remaining)
atoms in M. It is straightforward to check that cutting a{2},{33},{3} and{4} molecule increases the value
of σ by 1, 1, 0 and −1. For example, cutting a {4} molecule turns 4 bonds/free ends into fixed ends when
there is no O-atom (the case of O-atoms is similar and the result is the same) which decreases # bo/fr by 4,
and also decreases|M| by 1, leading to ∆σ =−1. In view of the initial and final values of σ, we then get
#{4}− (#{2} + #{33A}) =σinit =|M|−| M3|− #bond, (12.1)

===== PAGE 129 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 129
where σinit is the initial value of σ (with final value σfin = 0), #bond is the number of bonds (initially) in M
and|M3| is the number of deg 3 atoms; here the last equality is because each atom has deg 3 or 4, and each
bond is exactly counted twice, so # bo/fr = (total deg of all atoms)− #bond = 4|M|−| M3|− #bond.
Since M is connected, we know |M|− #bond ≤ 1, with equality if and only if M is a tree. Now, if
#{33A} = 0 (thus #{2} = 0), then we have #{4} +|M3| =|M|− #bond by (12.1), which is only possible when
|M|− #bond = 1 (so M is a tree) and |M3|≤ 1; conversely, if M is a tree and has at most one deg 3 atom,
then|M|− #bond = 1 and|M3| + #{4} = 1 by (2), so we get #{2} = #{33A} = 0. This proves (3).
Proof of (4). Finally we prove (4). Following UP, we first cut n∈ M as free, which is either the only
deg 3 atom, or some deg 4 atom in M; note that this does not create any deg 2 atom (because it creates
at most one fixed end at any atom, see Proposition 10.4 (2)). In the subsequent cuttings, consider the first
{33A} molecule (which exists due to (2)) and the two fixed ends (e1,e 2) at it, which are created when cutting
previous atoms p1 and p2 as free, and neither p1 nor p2 has deg 2 when it is cut, by the same proof in (3).
If p1̸= p2, say p1 is cut before p2, then there exist two free ends e′

is good due to Definition 9.4. This completes the proof. □
Now, with the UP algorithm, we can already prove the first simple case of Proposition 9.7, namely when
|H|≥ (C∗

Proposition 12.3. Proposition 9.7 is true in the case when |H|≥ (C∗
12)−1·ρ.
Proof. Under the assumption|H|≥ (C∗
12)−1ρ, we simply cut the whole molecule M using UP. By Definition
9.4 we know that # bad = #{4} + #ee, where #ee is the number of empty ends. Recall (9.51) in Proposition

9.7. We shall prove that ν :=|H|− #{4}− #ee≥ 0, and that (note that d− 1≥ 1 and C∗

(υ/2)· #good +ν≥ (υ/8)· (C∗

In the proof below, in Part 1 we will show ν≥ 0 by using Proposition 12.2 and bounding the number of
connected components of M by|H| (the empty ends will be trivial), which is done by repeatedly iterating
Proposition 10.4 (5)–(7) and showing that every component of M must intersect a particle line in H, a
process we call the (PL) argument below. In Parts 2–3, by using Proposition 10.4 (9), we will show that
either ν≳|H| (i.e. the number of components of M is strictly less than |H|), or a constant percentage of
these components contain cycles or initial links. In the former case we already gain enough powers, while in
the latter case we can obtain sufficiently many good molecules either by Proposition 12.2 (4) or by exploiting
the initial links.
Proof part 1. We first proveν≥ 0. By Proposition 12.2 (2) we have #{4}≤ #comp, where #comp is the
number of components of M, so it suffices to show # comp + #ee≤|H|. Here we present an argument which
will be used many times later in different variants, which we refer to as the (PL) argument below (see
Figure 43). Start from any component W of M; by Proposition 10.4 (5), this W must intersect a particle line
p∈p(Mℓ′) for some ℓ′. By Proposition 10.4 (7), this p is equal to or connected to some p′∈r(Mℓ′+1) via
Mℓ′+1; in either case p′ also intersects W (note that each particle line can intersect at most one component
of M, see Remark 10.5). We then replace p byp′ and repeat the same argument to get p′′∈r(Mℓ′+2) and
so on, in the end we see that W must intersect a particle line q∈ r(Mℓ) = H (in particular this q∈ H is
not an empty end). Since each q intersects at most one component of M, this shows that # comp is bounded
by the number of particle lines in H that are not empty ends.
Figure 43. The (PL) argument in the proof of Proposition 12.3: start from the blue particle

is connected to the red particle line p′ via Mℓ′+1, leading to a larger component of M[ℓ′:ℓ′+1]

another particle linep′′, and so on. This argument will be used in a few variants below (e.g.
Proposition 12.13 relying on Proposition 12.9).
On the other hand, if p is any particle line which is an empty end, then p∈ p(Mℓ′) for some ℓ′, and
contains no atom; by repeating Proposition 10.4 (7) we know that p must also belong to H. This means
that #ee is equal to the number of particle lines in H that are empty ends, so putting together we obtain
that #comp + #ee≤|H|, as desired.
Proof part 2. Now we have ν≥|H|− #comp− #ee≥ 0, it suffices to prove (12.2). Define
• X to be the number of particle lines in H that are connected to another particle line in H via M,
• Y to be the number of components of M that contains a cycle,
• andZ to be the number of initial links involving two non-empty-end particle lines, such that at least
one of them belongs to a component that does not contain a cycle.
We claim that
X +Y + 2Z≥|H|− 4· #ee. (12.3)
In fact, by Proposition 10.4 (9), each particle line p in H either (i) belongs to the X particle lines listed
above, or (ii) is not connected to other particle lines in H but is connected to a cycle, or (iii) does not belong

to (i) or (ii) but forms an initial link. Here in (ii) the p must intersect one of the Y components with cycle,
and each component is counted at most once (if two particle lines correspond to the same component then
they would be connected and thus would be in (i)), leading to at most Y particle lines. In (iii), suppose p
forms an initial link that involvesp′ as in Definition 3.19, then each p′ corresponds to a unique p (otherwise

4· #ee choices ofp′ because each empty end belongs to at most two initial links). This proves (12.3).

Proof part 3. Next we prove that
#good≥Y +Z/2 + #ee. (12.5)
In fact, each empty end must belong to a link by Proposition 10.4 (9), so it must be good. In the process of
UP we may choose to first cut the components with cycles; by Proposition 12.2 (4), each of these components
contributes at least one good molecule under UP (possibly after splitting into 2 sub-cases, but the total
number of sub-cases caused by this is bounded by 2 |M| and is acceptable in Proposition 9.7), so we get at
least Y good molecules.
After this, we are left with a forest without fixed end, so each of the subsequent elementary molecules we
cut will be {3} or{4} molecules by Proposition 12.2 (3). Moreover, for each of the Z initial links, at least
one of the involved particle lines (i.e. the one belonging to a no-cyclic component) has still not been cut; we
call the corresponding bottom free end e1 and call the one initial linked to it e2, note that e2 may or may
not have been cut.
In the subsequent cuttings of the remaining forest, let σj be the maximal ov-segment containing e1, and
assume σ2 is broken (i.e. has any atom on it cut) before σ1. Let p be the first atom in σ1 that is cut, then
p must be cut as a {3} or{4} molecule and has no fixed end along σ1 (as it is the first atom cut in σ1),
so the initial link condition guarantees that p is a good ({3} or{4}) molecule by Definition 9.4, see Figure
44. Similarly, if σ1 and σ2 are broken at the same time, say when cutting an atom p, then the initial link
condition again guarantees that{p} is a good {3} or{4} molecule.
Figure 44. Part 3 of the proof of Proposition 12.3. Here σ2 is broken before σ1, and p is

at p), so{p} is a good{3} molecule, thanks to the initial link condition and Definition 9.4.
In summary, each of the Z initial links provides one good molecule in the process of cutting forests,
leading to Z/2 good molecules (each good molecule is counted at most twice as each particle line is involved
in at most 2 initial links). This produces Z/2 extra good molecules and thus proves (12.5). Finally, putting
together (12.3)–(12.5), we get

12.2. Strong degeneracies. From now on we only need to consider the case|H|< (C∗
12)−1·ρ in Proposition
9.7 (we will make this assumption until the end of Section 13), and the goal is to prove (9.51). Note also
that #bad = #{4} + #ee and|H|≥ #ee by the same proof as in Proposition 12.3, so it suffices to prove
(υ/2)· #good−d· #{4}≥ (C∗
13)−1·ρ. (12.7)
In this subsection we treat the case of sufficiently many strong degeneracies (Definition 12.4 below), see
Proposition 12.5 (also cf. Section 11.7.3). For technical reasons (explained in the proof of Proposition 12.5
below) we also need the extra notion of primitivity in Definition 12.4 below.
Definition 12.4. Let M be a molecule as in Proposition 10.4. Define a pair of atoms {n, n′} in M to be
strongly degenerate if
(i) They are ov-adjacent and in the same layer ( ℓ[n] =ℓ[n′]).
(ii) There exist edges ( e,e′) at n and n′ respectively, such that cutting{n, n′} as free in M and turning
(e,e′) into fixed ends results in a {33A} molecule, as in Definition 8.11;
(iii) We have the restriction that |xe−xe′|≤ ε1−υ and|ve−ve′|≤ ε1−υ.
We also define an atom pair {n, n′} in M to be primitive, if they are in the same layer ( ℓ[n] = ℓ[n′]), n
is ov-parent of n′, and there do not exist C-atoms qj (1≤j≤r) with r≥ 1, such that qj+1 is ov-child of qj
for each j with q0 = n and qr+1 = n′.
Proposition 12.5. Recall Definition 12.4. Suppose we make the further restriction that M contains at
least (C∗
12)−1ρ disjoint strongly degenerate primitive pairs (by inserting the corresponding indicator function
1 str.deg.prm), then Proposition 9.7 is true.
Proof. Recall|H| < (C∗
12)−1·ρ and the goal is to prove (12.7). The idea is to restrict sufficiently many
strong degeneracies to a single layer Mℓ′ (by definition, the two atoms in any strongly degenerate pair must
be in the same layer), and cut most of the atoms involved in these degeneracies as {3} molecules which will
be good due to the extra restrictions given by strong degeneracy, see Choice 3 below. If this is not possible,
then there must exist sufficiently many cycles in Mℓ′ (i.e. Y ≳ ρ in our notions below), so we can try to
gain from the cyclic clusters using Proposition 12.2 (4), see Choice 2. If this is also not possible, then Mℓ′
must have many components (i.e. X≳ρ in our notions below); in this case we can cut each pair of strongly
degenerate atoms as a good {44} molecule, see Choice 1 . However, we need to make sure that cutting
this{44} molecule does not violate the (MONO) property needed to execute the UP algorithm, which then
naturally leads to the notion of primitive atom pairs in Definition 12.4.
First assume there exists at least A := (C∗
12)−1ρ disjoint strongly degenerate primitive pairs. By splitting
into at most | logε|C∗A sub-cases (i.e. decomposing 1 into the corresponding indicator functions), we may
fix one choice of these pairs. By using pigeonhole principle and replacing A with L−1· A, we may restrict all
these atom pairs{nj, n′
j} to a single layer, say Mℓ′. Define Q to be the union of all connected omponents of
Mℓ′ that contain at least one strongly degenerate primitive pair {nj, n′
j}. We then
(i) Cut Q as free, then cut M>ℓ′ as free from M\Q and cut it into elementary molecules using UP;
(ii) Then cut M<ℓ′∪ (Mℓ′\Q) into elementary molecules using DOWN.
It is easy to see that this can always be done, and the contribution of these steps (i.e. coming from atoms
outside Q) to #{4} is at most 2|H|. This because the number of components of M>ℓ′ is≤|H| by repeating the
(PL) argument in the proof of Proposition 12.3 (i.e. starting with a particle line intersecting one component
and iteratively applying Proposition 10.4 (7)), and moreover each full component of M<ℓ′∪ (Mℓ′\Q) after
the rest has been cut must also be a full component in the original molecule M (see Remark 10.6) and there
are at most|H| of them, so the upper bound on # {4} follows from Proposition 12.2 (2).
Now we only need to cut Q into elementary molecules. For each connected component Q′ of Q, consider
the clusters in Q′ as defined in Proposition 10.4 (4) (i.e. ov-components of the set of C-atoms in Q′); denote
these clusters by Qj, then Q′ is formed by joining these Qj with O-atoms. Denote by X the number of
components of Q, and by Y the total number of Qj that are cyclic (i.e. ρ(Qj)> 0 or Qj contains a cycle).

Note that Proposition 12.5 deals with the case where there exist many strongly degenerate primitive pairs.
As for the non-primitive pairs, it turns out that they always lead to good molecules if they are cut as {33A},
as shown in the following lemma.
Lemma 12.6. For each{33A} molecule{n, n′} obtained in any cutting sequence after any deletion of O-
atoms, if the atom pair{n, n′} is not primitive in M before any operation, then by splitting into 2 sub-cases,
we can either make this {33A} molecule good, or cut it into a good {3} and a normal{2} molecule.
Proof. Consider any{33A} molecule{n, n′} that is not primitive before any operation, and let qj be as in
Definition 12.4. Note that n and n′ must belong to the same (original) layer Mℓ′ (otherwise{n, n′} is good
by Definition 9.4), and so does every qj. Consider the cycle formed by the ov-segments from qj to qj+1 (with
n = q0 and n′ = qr+1) and from n′ to n, which is contained in the single layer Mℓ′. By Proposition 10.4
(3), we see that both n and n′ must be C-atom. Moreover the C-atoms qj, and the ov-adjacency relations
between them, are not affected by deletion of O-atoms. Now, consider the C-atom q1 which is ov-child of n,
and qr which is ov-parent of n′ (they could be the same atom).
Consider any cutting sequence. If q1, or any (O-) atom r in the ov-segment between q1 and n is cut
before{n, n′} (i.e. belongs to a molecule after cutting that is ≺cut{n, n′}), then we can make the splitting
depending on whether (i) |tn−tn′|≥ ευ or (ii) |tn−tn′|≤ ευ. In case (i) the {n, n′} is already good by
Definition 9.4; in case (ii), due to the ov-child relations we have
tn≥tr≥tq1≥···≥ tqr+1 =tn′,
in particular|tn−tn′|≤ ευ implies that
|tn−tr|≤ ευ, |tn′−tr|≤ ευ.
Now we may cut either n or n′ from{n, n′} as a{3} molecule such that the remaining atom becomes a {2}
molecule (one of the two choices must be possible due to the definition of {33A} molecules), then this {3}
molecule will be good by Definition 9.4, so the lemma is proved.
In the same way, if qr, or any (O-) atom r in the ov-segment between qr and n′ is cut before{n, n′}, then
the lemma is also proved by the same arguments as above.
Finally, if none of q1, qr and none of the above-mentioned O-atoms is cut before {n, n′}, then at the time
{n, n′} is cut, the atom n cannot have any bottom fixed end (because it is ov-parent of n′, and the ov-segment
between n and q1 does not lead to any fixed end at n as the other atoms on this ov-segment are all cut after
n); in the same way n′ has no top fixed end at the time {n, n′} is cut. However {n, n′} is a{33} molecule,
which means that n must have a top fixed end and n′ has a bottom fixed end, and thus {n, n′} will be a
{33B} molecule instead of{33A}, contradiction. This completes the proof. □
Reduction 1. With Propositions 12.3 and 12.5 and Lemma 12.6 proved, from now on, we may always
assume in Proposition 9.7 that |H|≤ (C∗
12)−1ρ and the number of disjoint strongly degenerate primitive
pairs is at most (C∗
12)−1ρ.
Using also Lemma 12.6, we see that each{33A} molecule must be good with at most (C∗
12)−1ρ exceptions
(these exceptions are when the corresponding atom pair is both strongly degenerate and primitive; if it is
not strongly degenerate then we get a good molecule by Definition 9.4, if it is not primitive we get a good
molecule by Lemma 12.6).
As such, we see that Proposition 9.7 will follow if we can prove that
(υ/2)· (#good)−d· (#{4})≥ (C∗
11)−1·ρ (12.11)
under the assumption that all {33A} molecules are good , which we will assume throughout the rest of
the proof.

12.3. Layer refinement and the large R case. The goal of this subsection is to introduce the layer
refining process (discussed in Section 11.7.2) and prove the next case of Proposition 9.7 when R is large,
namely Proposition 12.11. The following lemma is already stated in Section 11.7.2:
Lemma 12.7. Let M be a connected molecule with ρ(M)≤ Γ, see Definition 3.13. Then we can divide M
into at most Γ + 1 disjoint subsets M(k) for 1≤ k≤ γ≤ Γ + 1, such that (i) each M(k) is a tree, and (ii)
if k < k′ then no atom in M(k) can be parent of any atom in M(k′). See Figure 47. Note that, unlike the
original layer defined by time intervals, there is no time inequality between atoms in different thin layers
within the same original layer, other than those coming from parent-child relations.
Figure 47. Left: example of layer refining (Lemma 12.7, each thin layer is a forest). Right:
the proof of Lemma 12.7. Note that n1 and n2 are connected by a path above n (forming a
cycle); also Mhigh consists of those atoms that can be connected to n1 without entering Sn.
Proof. We induct on ρ(M). If ρ(M) = 0 then M is a tree and there is nothing to prove. If ρ(M)> 0, then
M must contain a cycle of atoms. Let a lowest atom in this cycle be n, which is adjacent to two atoms n1
and n2 in this cycle, then both nj must be parents of n. Let Sn be the set of descendants of n (including n
itself), we then define
Mhigh :=

m∈ M : m is connected to n1 by a path not containing atoms in Sn
	
, (12.12)
and Mlow := M\Mhigh, see Figure 47. Note in particular Sn⊆ Mlow and n1, n2∈ Mhigh (n2∈ Mhigh because
any atom in the cycle other than n cannot belong to Sn). Clearly Mhigh is connected, because for any
m∈ Mhigh and any m′ on the path connecting m to n1 defined by (12.12), we must also have m′∈ n1 by
definition, so each atom in Mhigh is connected to n1∈ Mhigh by a path completely within Mhigh.
To prove Mlow is connected, choose any atom m∈ Mlow\Sn. Since M is connected, there is a path in M
connecting m to n. Let the first atom in this path (starting from m) that belongs to Sn be p, and consider
any atom q̸= p between m and p on this path, then m is connected to q by a path not containing atoms
in Sn, which means q∈ Mlow (as q∈ Mhigh would imply m∈ Mhigh). Thus we know each m∈ Mlow\Sn is
connected to an atom p∈ Sn by a path completely within Mlow, but Sn is connected (every atom in Sn is
connected to n by a path completely within Sn), so Mlow is also connected.
Moreover, if an atom m1∈ Mhigh is connected to an atom m2∈ Mlow by a bond, then we must have
m2∈ Sn (otherwise m1∈ Mhigh would imply m2∈ Mhigh). This means m1 must be a parent of m2, since
otherwise m1 is a child of m2, so m2∈Sn implies m1∈Sn⊆ Mlow, which is impossible. Finally, since there
are at least two bonds between Mhigh and Mlow (those connecting n to n1 and n2), we see that
|M| =|Mhigh| +|Mlow|, |B|≥|B high| +|Blow| + 2 ⇒ ρ(Mhigh) +ρ(Mlow)≤ρ(M)− 1≤ Γ− 1,
whereB (resp. Bhigh andBlow) is the set of bonds in M (resp. Mhigh and Mlow). We now apply induction
hypothesis to Mhigh and Mlow, and note that no atom in Mlow is parent of an atom in Mhigh, to divide M
into at most (ρ(Mhigh) + 1) + (ρ(Mlow) + 1)≤ Γ + 1 subsets that satisfy requirements (i) and (ii), so the
proof is complete. □

===== PAGE 137 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 137
With Lemma 12.7 we can then define the full layer refining process. The idea is to delete those O-atoms
joining two cyclic clusters (or cyclic components), so that each component contains at most one cyclic cluster
after the deletions; in particular each component will have ρ value at most Γ, so we can apply Lemma 12.7
to each component.
Definition 12.8 (Layer refining). Let M be a molecule formed by layers Mℓ′, as in Proposition 10.4. We
define the following operation of layer refining to turn Mℓ′ into a new layered molecule eMℓ′. Here the
atoms and edges of eMℓ′ are the same as Mℓ′ except some O-atoms being deleted as in Definition 8.1 (for
convenience, we will not distinguish between Mℓ′ and eMℓ′ below), but the layering of the atoms are different:
the atoms in eMℓ′ are divided into at most Γ + 2 disjoint subsets, each of them indicated by a refined layer
number. We will call these thin layers. The layer refining process is defined as follows:
(1) Recall all the clusters (i.e. C-molecules) in Mℓ′. We repeatedly delete O-atoms in Mℓ′, as long as each
deletion disconnects two cyclic clusters Qj (i.e. these two clusters belong to different components
after deletion), until no more O-atoms can be deleted. For each connected component of Mℓ′ that
does not contain a cyclic cluster, we assign thin layer ( ℓ′, 0) to all its atoms; note that with this
definition, the thin layer (ℓ′, 0) plays a special role different from those thin layers ( ℓ′,k ) for k≥ 1.
(2) Apply Lemma 12.7 to each component Q of Mℓ′ that contains a cyclic cluster (we are guaranteed
to have ρ(Q)≤ Γ, which will be proved in Proposition 12.9 below), to divide it into Qk, where
1≤k≤ Γ + 1. Then, for each atom in Mℓ′, assign it thin layer ( ℓ′,k ) if it belongs to Qk for some
component Q.
The thin layers defined above are linearly ordered by lexicographic ordering (where we first compare ℓ′
and then compare k); if layer ℓ′ is not refined, we identify it with ( ℓ′, 0). Note that the original layer ℓ′
precisely contains all the thin layers (ℓ′,k ). For any ζ and ξ, we define MT
ζ to be the set of atoms with thin
layerζ; the associated notions MT
(ζ:ξ), MT
[ζ:η] etc. are defined similarly.
In Proposition 12.9 below we state and prove some properties of the molecule M after layer refining. The
important points are that (a) each thin layer after refinement is a forest, see Proposition 12.9 (3)–(4); (b)
the number of O-atoms deleted is bounded by Rℓ′ :=ρ(Mℓ′) for the refined layers Mℓ′, see Proposition 12.9
(1); and (c) the original properties of M (notably Proposition 10.4 (5)–(7) and (9)) still hold after layer
refining, with a number of exceptions that is bounded by the number of deleted O-atoms, see Proposition
12.9 (5)–(8).
The proof of (a)–(b) above relies on the construction in Definition 12.8, in particular the fact that we delete
all O-atoms joining two cyclic clusters, so each component contains at most one cyclic cluster after layer
refining; see Parts 1–2 in the proof of Proposition 12.9 below. The proof of (c) is straightforward, basically
because deleting each O-atom creates at most two exceptions to the relevant properties in Proposition 12.9
(5)–(8), see Part 3 in the proof of Proposition 12.9 below.
Proposition 12.9. If we perform the layer refining for ℓ′ as in Definition 12.8, then
(1) The number of O-atoms deleted in the process is at most Rℓ′ :=ρ(Mℓ′).
(2) After deleting the O-atoms, each component Q of Mℓ′ that contains a cyclic cluster satisfiesρ(Q)≤ Γ,
in particular Lemma 12.7 is applicable in Definition 12.8.
(3) Each MT
(ℓ′,k) (where 1≤k≤ Γ + 1) is a forest, and has most Rℓ′ connected components.
(4) MT
(ℓ′,0) is a forest, and the number of components of MT
(ℓ′,0) is not more than the number of compo-
nents of Mℓ′ before refining.
Now consider the result of refining any set of layers in M, which we still denote by M for convenience.
Then we have the followings
(5) For ζ <ξ, no atom in MT
ζ can be parent of any atom in MT
ξ .
(6) Each connected component of MT
(ℓ′,0) (where ℓ′≤ℓ) must intersect some particle line p∈r(Mℓ′).
(7) With at most 2 Rℓ′ exceptions, for each particle line p∈p(Mℓ′), either p intersects a component of
MT
(ℓ′,0), or it intersects a component of MT
(ℓ′,k) for some 1≤k≤ Γ + 1, orp∈r(Mℓ′).

138 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
(8) Recall R =P
ℓ′ Rℓ′. For eachℓ′ <ℓ , with at most 2R exceptions, each particle linep∈r(Mℓ′) either
forms an initial link within MT
<(ℓ′+1,0), or is connected to a cycle or another particle line p′∈r(Mℓ′)
via the subset MT
<(ℓ′+1,0).
Proof. Proof part 1. First note the following facts about the refinement in Definition 12.8:
(i) Deleting any O-atom o in Mℓ′ always breaks a component of Mℓ′ into two;
(ii) Each component of Mℓ′ that does not contain a cyclic cluster remain the same after refining. Each
of the other components after refining contains a unique cyclic cluster.
In fact, (i) is true because if n1 and n2 are the two atoms adjacent to o along two ov-segments in two
different particle lines, then they cannot be connected after deleting o, otherwise we get a cycle (before
deleting o) in view of the two bonds from o to nj (j∈{ 1, 2}), such that the two bonds at o are not serial
in the cycle, contradicting Proposition 10.4 (3). Also (ii) is obvious by our construction (each component
not containing cyclic clusters is not affected, and each component after refining cannot contain two cyclic
clusters, otherwise we would be able to delete one more O-atom).
Proof part 2. Next we prove (1)–(4). Note first that the number of cyclic clusters in Mℓ′ does not
exceed Rℓ′ (because each cyclic cluster contributes one the to the value of ρ(Mℓ′)). Now (1) is easy because
each deletion increases the number of cyclic components by 1 (due to (i) above), and this number cannot
exceed Rℓ′ which is the total number of cyclic clusters (imagine we delete all O-atoms, then the number of
cyclic components would be equal to Rℓ′), so the number of O-atoms deleted cannot exceed Rℓ′. Moreover,
(2) follows from the fact that each component contains only one cyclic cluster (the other clusters are trees
and do not add to the ρ value, and joining the clusters by O-atoms also does not increase the ρ value).
Also (4) is obvious because MT
(ℓ′,0) has not been affected by the deletion of O-atoms, and is a forest because
it does not contain any cyclic cluster (cf. Proposition 10.4 (3)). As for (3), the number of components of
MT
(ℓ′,k) (k≥ 1) is at most Rℓ′ because this is an upper bound for the number of components of Mℓ′ containing
one cyclic cluster; also each MT
(ℓ′,k) (k≥ 1) is a forest which directly follows from Lemma 12.7.
Proof part 3. Now we prove (5)–(8), where we perform layer refining to any set of layers ℓ′, and denote
the result still by M. To prove (5), due to the lexicographic ordering of thin layers, we only need to prove
it for a single layer ℓ′, and we only need to consider those components containing one cyclic cluster Qj
(otherwise all thin layers are (ℓ′, 0)). But by definition, this follows from Lemma 12.7 (for each component
of Mℓ′) and the fact that no bond exists between different components of Mℓ′.
Next we prove (6)–(7). Note that particle lines can be defined in the same way before and after the
refinement, since deleting an O-atom corresponds to removing one atom from a particle line p and merging
two consecutive edges. From Proposition 10.4 (5)–(7) we already know that before layer refining , any
connected component in Mℓ′ must intersect some particle line p with p∈ r(Mℓ′). This then proves (6)
because MT
(ℓ′,0) is not affected by layer refining: there is no bond connecting it to any MT
(ℓ′,k) fork> 0, and
it also does not contain any of the O-atoms deleted in the refinement process. Similarly (7) follows from
Proposition 10.4 (5)–(7): any particle line p∈p(Mℓ′) must either belong to r(Mℓ′), or intersects Mℓ′ (which
is then divided into MT
(ℓ′,k) for 0≤k≤ Γ + 1). The only exceptions are those containing one of the deleted
O-atoms, but the number of these particle lines is at most 2 Rℓ′ as each O-atom belongs to at most two
particle lines.
Finally we prove (8). Note that before layer refining, this just corresponds to Proposition 10.4 (9); however,
after the layer refinement, we have deleted at mostR O-atoms in M[1:ℓ′] (note that by our conventions, M[1:ℓ′]
is just MT
<(ℓ′+1,0) after the refinement). If we add back these O-atoms one by one, then each time we create
at most one cycle in one component, or merge at most two components of M[1:ℓ′], and does not affect any
initial link. Define a particle line p to be single if it does not satisfy the requirement of (8), then adding back
one O-atom at most reduces the number of single particle lines by 2 (for example, if adding back an O-atom
merges two components, then it may turn two single particle lines into non-single as they are now connected
to each other; the other particle lines are unaffected, as they cannot intersect the relevant components by

===== PAGE 139 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 139
the single assumption). By Proposition 10.4 (9), it then follows that the number of single particle lines is at
most 2R, as desired. □
Before stating and proving Proposition 12.11 below (i.e. the case R≳ ρ), we need one more auxiliary
result concerning a special case of UP. The intuition will be explained in the proof of Proposition 12.11.
Proposition 12.10. Suppose M is a full molecule divided into two subsets MU and MD, such that (i) MU is
a forest, and (ii) no atom in MD is parent of atom in MU. Define # comp(M) to be the number of components
of M, and define # cyc.comp(MD) to be the number of components of MD that contains a cycle within this
component. Then, see Figure 48, after applying the algorithm UP, we have that #{33B} = #{44} = 0, and
#{4}≤ #comp(M), and # {33A}≥ #cyc.comp(MD)/4. (12.13)
Figure 48. Proposition 12.10. Here the cyclic clusters in MD are shown, with nj being
the first atom in each component that has deg 2 when cut, and pj∈ MU is its ov-parent;
see the proof of Proposition 12.10 below.
Proof. The statements about #{33B}, #{44} and #{4} already follow from Proposition 12.2. Now we prove
the statement about #{33A}. First we prove the following claim: if A is a component of MD that contains
a cycle, and no atom in A belongs to a {33A} molecule, then at least one atom in A must be deg 2 when
it is cut. To prove this claim, choose a cycle θ within A, we may think of θ as formed by ov-segments, and
consider the endpoints of these ov-segments. Let n be the one among these endpoints that is cut after all
the other endpoints, then n is ov-adjacent to other endpoints which are cut before n, which creates two fixed
ends at n, so n must be deg 2 when it is cut.
Now, ifA is as above, and suppose n∈A is the first atom in A that has deg 2 when it is cut, and consider
the cutting operation that turns n into deg 2. Then this cutting operation must involve an atom p that is
ov-adjacent to n; note that this p belongs to either A or MU. If p∈A, then p cannot be deg 2 when it is cut
(as n is the first); this p also cannot be deg 4 when it is cut (because we cut deg 4 only when every atom
has deg 4, and cutting a deg 4 atom p cannot turn the deg 4 atom n into deg 2, cf. Proposition 10.4 (2)), so
it must be deg 3 when it is cut, but n also must has deg 3 when p is cut, which violates Definition 12.1 (3).
Therefore we must have p∈ MU. Since p is cut before n, it cannot be cut in Definition 12.1 (1) (as cutting
any deg 2 C-atom in Definition 12.1 (1) requires that this atom has two bottom fixed ends, see also Remark
8.7), nor can it have deg 4 when it is cut (for the same reason as in the last paragraph). Suppose p belongs
to Sq for some q as in Definition 12.1 (3), since MU is a forest, we know that p has at most one parent in
Sq (otherwise we have two different paths going from p to q that form a cycle in MU), so it must have deg
3 when it is cut (the top edge at p corresponding to the non Sq parent, as well as the two bottom edges at
p, are not fixed when p is cut; see Figure 49), while at the same time n also has deg 3. By Definition 12.1
(3), we then know that p belongs to a{33A} molecule.
As a conclusion, we know that each component A of MD that contains a cycle, must either contain one
atom in a {33A} molecule, or intersect a particle line of some atom in some {33A} molecule. Clearly each
{33A} molecule can be so obtained by at most 4 components (as each particle line can intersect at most one
component of MD, see Remark 10.5), so we get #{33A}≥ #cyc.comp(MD)/4. □

12.4. Layer selection and weak degeneracies. From now on we will focus on proving (12.11) under
the assumptions in Reduction 1. The goal of this subsection is to reduce this result to Proposition 12.18
(and prove Proposition 12.17 which contains the simple cases of initial cumulant and weak degeneracies), by
applying the procedure of layer selection (discussed in Section 11.6).
The full layer selection process, defined in Definition 12.12 below, is essentially the same as the toy version
in Definition 11.23 in Section 11. The only difference is that we need to perform layer refining for layers
Mℓ′ (ℓ′≤ℓ1), which leads to an extra step of picking a thin layer ζU within layer Mℓ1, see Definition 12.12
(2). The rest is the same as in Definition 11.23, except that v∗
ζ in Definition 12.12 (2) is defined for thin
layers instead of layers, and the ζD we pick is also a thin layer (following the same rule as in Definition
11.23).

them must first enter MT
ζD and then exit MT
ζD, which leads to two UD connections e ande′
that are connected via MD (so by definition both of them will be good). If e is not broken
in any subsequent cutting, it is easy to see that e will remain good (either with connection
to e′, or with a deg 3 atom).
Now we consider the behavior of ( e,e′) under the cutting operations in Option 1. Clearly each cutting
in Option 1 (ii) cannot break the maximal ov-segment containing e (i.e. cannot cut any atom in this
ov-segment as in Definition 8.3), because we only cut components not contained in Y , and any deg 2 atoms
that are subsequently cut in Option 1 (ii) must belong to MT
ζD (resp. MT
ζU ) and must be O-atom or have
two top (resp. bottom) fixed ends. Since the component X has at least one bond connecting to MT
ζU∪Y
and is thus not cut as free in Option 1 (iii), we know that e remains a UD connection after Option 1 .
Moreover, if the component X is not affected by the cuttings in Option 1 (i.e. no atom in X is cut, and
no fixed end is created at atoms in X), then we must have Z1⊆ Y so e′ is also a UD connection that is
still connected to e via MD even after Option 1; if X is affected, then the (possibly new) component of
X containing n will contain at least one deg 3 atom after Option 1 (cf. Remark 10.6). In either case the
desired property for e follows.
Finally, if the above p′ does not exist, then p must form a cycle. In this case, using this cycle within
MT
[ζD:ζ0] and note that MT
ζD itself is a forest, we can still find thee ande′ satisfying (a)–(c) above withe̸=e′,
except that it is now possible to have Z1 =Z; but this does not affect the proof, and the same arguments
as above then proves (3c). □
Now we can prove the next case of Proposition 9.7, namely Proposition 12.17 below, which contains the
initial link case (when ζD is absent) and the weak degenerate case (where there are many weak degeneracies
in MD as defined in Definition 12.16) below. In the weakly degenerate case we choose Option 2 in Definition
12.14, which allows to gain from the weak degeneracy conditions at the {3} and{4} molecules in MD.

13. Cutting algorithm II: analyzing UD molecules
The goal of this section is to prove Proposition 12.18 for the two-layer molecule MUD , by introducing the
various algorithms corresponding to the toy models in Section 11. To simplify notation in this section, if a
molecule MUD = MU∪ MD satisfies that (a) each of MU and MD is a forest and has no deg 2 atoms, and
(b) no atom in MD is parent of any atom in MU, then we say it is a UD molecule. If in addition MU has
no C-top fixed end and MD has no C-bottom fixed end, then we say MUD is canonical.
Reduction 2. Our proof below relies on the properties (3a)–(3c) in Proposition 12.15. For convenience we
record them here:
(i) Each of MU and MD is a forest and has no deg 2 atoms. No atom in MD is a parent of any atom in
MU, and MU (resp. MD) has no C-top (resp. C-bottom) fixed end.
(ii) The number of full components of MU is at most 10(C∗
5)−1·ρ′′′. Each component in MD either has
at least two bonds connecting to MU, or has one bond connecting to MU and one deg 3 atom.
(iii) There exist at least (ρ′′′/4) many UD connections, such that each UD connection is either connected
to another UD connection via MD, or has its MD endpoint belonging to component of MD that
contains a deg 3 atom.
13.1. The 2CONNUP algorithm and toy model III. We start by introducing the full 2CONNUP
algorithm corresponding to toy model III. Compared to the toy version (Definition 11.21), the main extra
ingredient is Definition 13.1 (1); see the proof of Proposition 13.2 for more explanations.
Definition 13.1 (The algorithm 2CONNUP). Define the following cutting algorithm 2CONNUP. It
takes as input any canonical UD molecule MUD , such that MD has no full component that has no bond
connecting to MU. Define M1
2conn to be the set of atoms n∈ MD that have deg 3 and are ov-adjacent to one
atom m∈ MU, such that no other MD atom on the ov-segment between n and m has deg 3. Define M2
2conn
to be the set of atoms n∈ MD that are ov-adjacent to two atoms in MU along two different particle lines,
and M2conn = M1
2conn∪ M2
2conn. For any such M = MUD , we define the cutting sequence as follows:
(1) If any (maximal) ov-segment contains at least A := 32υ−2 atoms in M2
2conn, then we choose n0 to
be any one of these atoms, and cut it as free. Repeat until no such ov-segment exists.
(2) If M contains any deg 2 atom n, then cut it as free, and repeat until there is no deg 2 atom left.
(3) Choose a lowest deg 3 atom n∈ MU among all remaining deg 3 atoms in MU (or a lowest atom in
MU if MU has no deg 3 atom). Let Sn be the set of descendants of n in MU; this n and Sn will be
fixed until the end of (4).
(4) Now, starting from n, each time choose a highest atom m inSn. If m has deg 3, and m is ov-adjacent
to an atom p∈ M2conn that also has deg 3, then cut {m, p} as free. Otherwise just cut m as free.
Repeat until all atoms in Sn has been cut. Then go to (2).

===== PAGE 152 =====
152 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
(5) Repeat (2)–(4) until all atoms in MU has been cut. Then apply algorithm DOWN to cut the rest
of MD into elementary molecules.
We also define the dual algorithm 2CONNDN, by reversing the roles of MU and MD and the notions of
parent/child etc., just as how we define DOWN from UP in Definition 12.1.
The key estimate for the algorithm 2CONNUP is stated in the following proposition, which is the full
version of Proposition 11.22 in Section 11.
Proposition 13.2. For the algorithm 2CONNUP, we have #{33B} = #{44} = 0, and
(υ/2)· #{33A}−d· #{4}≥C−1(|M1
2conn| +|M2
2conn|)−C· #fucp(MU), (13.1)
where #fucp(MU) is the number of full components of MU (note the difference with #comp(MU), i.e. the number
of all components ). Recall also the notion of constant C in Definition 1.6 (2). Similar results hold for the
dual algorithm 2CONNDN, if we reverse the roles of MU and MD.
Proof. We first explain the relevance of Definition 13.1 (1), see Figure 56. Note that, in Definition 11.21
where there is no O-atom, each atom in M2

containing two atoms in M2
2conn; however, now an atom in M2

so there might be other atoms in M2

The point is that (i) the number of such operations is negligible, (ii) each operation breaks
two ov-segments, so the total number of ov-segments broken is also negligible, and (iii) after
Definition 13.1 (1), each ov-segment contains at most O(1) atoms in M2
2conn, so the same

If we still argue naively as in Definition 11.21, it might happen that we break an ov-segment containing

thus #{33B} = #{44} = 0. Now we only need to prove (13.1).

13.2. The 3COMPUP algorithm. The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Proposition 12.18

define the full 3COMPUP algorithm (see Definition 13.6) corresponding to toy model II ((see Stage 5 in
Section 13.5), which is a direct extension of the toy version in Definition 11.17 by including O-atoms and
translating into the ov-segment language (cf. Section 11.7.1). Apart from this translation, Definition 13.6
and Proposition 13.7 are exactly the same as Definition 11.17 and Proposition 11.19 in Section 11. Before
proceeding, we state and prove the full version of Lemma 11.6 in Section 11 in the presence of O-atoms (by
translating adjacency to ov-adjacency etc.).

===== PAGE 156 =====
156 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
Lemma 13.4. Let M be a molecule that is a tree, and S⊆ M is an ov-connected set. Suppose we cut S as
free from M. Then, for each atom q∈ M\S that is ov-adjacent to an atom in S before cutting, there is a
unique componentXq⊆ M\S after cutting that contains q. This Xq is in bijection with q, and when viewed
in Xq, the operation of cutting S creates only one fixed end in Xq at q, see Figure 58.
Figure 58. Illustration of Lemma 13.4. The main differences with Lemma 11.6 are that (i)
nowS is allowed to be ov-connected instead of connected (with possible O-atoms in between
which also belong to different components after cutting S as free), and (ii) each ov-adjacent
atom q (not necessarily adjacent) now leads to its own component. For example, the q and
q′ will belong to different components after cutting S as free, but again each component has
exactly one new fixed end.
Proof. Consider all the ov-segments connecting an atom in S to another atom (in S or not in S), and all
atoms (including the intermediate O-atoms) on these ov-segments. Let the set of all such ov-segments (and
the bonds contained in these ov-segments) be Λ, and the set of all these atoms be Z⊇ S, then the atoms
in Z\S are precisely all the atoms q as stated. Note that all the bonds in Λ with an endpoint in M\S are
broken after cutting S as free; we claim that at this time, any two different atoms q1, q2∈Z\S must be in
two different components of M\S. In fact, if not, then they are connected in M\S by a path of bonds not
containing any bond in Λ. However, q1, q2∈Z are also connected by another path containing only bonds in
Λ, using that S is ov-connected. This then produces a cycle in M, contradiction.
Now we have proved that q7→Xq is a bijection, and for each Xq, the only atom in it that is ov-adjacent
to an atom in S is q. As such, cutting S as free only creates fixed end at q and not at any other atom in Xq.
If we get more than one fixed end at q, then q must be ov-adjacent to two atoms in n1, n2∈ S along two
different ov-segments (note: breaking two bonds at q that belong to the same ov-segment creates only one
fixed end at q, see Definition 8.3). Note that n1 is connected to n2 by a path of bonds in Λ; since q̸∈S, we
know that q either does not occur in this path, or occurs in this path but as one of the intermediate O-atoms
(so the two bonds at q in this path must be serial ). However, the two ov-segments connecting q to nj also
form a path between n1 and n2, in which the two bonds at q are not serial . Thus these two paths cannot
coincide, which again produces a cycle in M. This contradiction then proves that cutting S as free produces
only one fixed end at q (and in Xq), as desired. □
Lemma 13.5. Suppose M is a molecule that is a tree, contains only deg 3 and 4 atoms and has no C-top
fixed end. Let the set of deg 3 atoms in M be M(3) and assume|M(3)|≥ 2. Define a set A⊆ M to be no-
bottom if any child of any atom in A must also be in A. Then, there always exists a connected, no-bottom
subset A⊆ M with|A∩ M(3)| = 2.
Proof. For any n∈ M, let Sn be the set of descendants of n, then it is always connected and no-bottom. If
there exists n such that|Sn∩ M(3)|≥ 2, then choose a lowest such n. Since n is either O-atom or has no
top fixed end, we know that either (i) n has one child n1, or (ii) n has deg 4 and has two children n1, n2. In
either case, by assumption we have |Snj∩ M(3)|≤ 1 for each j, which then implies |Sn∩ M(3)|≤ 2 because
Sn = (∪jSnj)∪{ n}. This implies |Sn∩ M(3)| = 2, as desired.
Now assume |Sn∩ M(3)|≤ 1 for each n∈ M. If |Sn∩ M(3)| = 1, there is a unique p = p(n)∈ Sn∩
M(3); now consider all atom pairs ( n, n′) such that p(n) and p(n′) both exist and are different (which exist

===== PAGE 157 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 157
because|M(3)|≥ 2), and one such pair ( n, n′) that are connected by a shortest path in M. Let this path be
(n0, n,··· , nj = n′), then for each 0 <i<j we must have either Sni∩ M(3) = ∅ or p(ni) = p(n) (otherwise
the pair ( n, ni) corresponds to a shorter path, contradiction). Now let A =∪j
i=0Sni, it is clear that A is
connected, no-bottom and A∩ M(3) ={p(n), p(n′)}, as desired. □
Definition 13.6 (The 3COMPUP algorithm). Let M be a molecule which is a forest, contains only deg
3 and 4 atoms and has no C-top fixed end, and assume that each component of M contains at least two deg
3 atoms. Let the set of deg 3 atoms in M be M(3). Define the following cutting sequence:
(1) Apply Lemma 13.5 to one component of M and select a connected no-bottom subset A. This set is
fixed until the end of (5).
(2) Choose a lowest deg 3 atom n in A that has not been cut. Let Sn be the set of descendants of n
(note that Sn⊆A). This n and Sn are fixed until the end of (3).
(3) Starting from n, each time choose a highest atom m inSn that has not been cut. If m has deg 3 and
has an ov-parent m+ or ov-child m− that also has deg 3, then cut {m, m±} as free; otherwise cut m
as free. Repeat until all atoms in Sn have been cut.
(4) If M has deg 2 atom, then cut it; repeat until M has no deg 2 atoms. Then go to (2).
(5) When all atoms in A have been cut, we subsequently cut all remaining deg 2 atoms in M as in (4),
and then cut all components of M with only one deg 3 atom using UP.
(6) Go to (1) and select the next A from one of the remaining components of M, and so on.
Proposition 13.7. Let M be as in Definition 13.6. Define # 3ovcp(M) to be the number of ov-components of
M(3) (the det of deg 3 atoms in M), and # comp(M) to be the number of components of M. Then M is cut
into elementary molecules after 3COMPUP (Definition 13.6), and we have
#{33B} = #{44} = #{4} = 0; # {33A}≥ 1
10· #3ovcp(M) + 1
5· #comp(M). (13.7)
Proof. Note that steps (2)–(3) in Definition 13.6 are exactly the same as those UP (see Definition 12.1
(2)–(3), note also the step of cutting deg 2 atoms is done in Definition 13.6 (5)), but applied to A only.
Since A is no-bottom which means Sn⊆ A whenever n∈ A, it is easy to see (following the same proof as
Proposition 12.2) that the (MONO) property is preserved, and thus M is cut into elementary molecules only,
and #{33B} = #{44} = #{4} = 0. We now focus on the proof of (13.7).
Note that the algorithm 3COMPUP consists of finitely many loops where one loop is formed by steps
(1)–(5) in Definition 13.6, and (say) turns M into M′. Define λ(M) to be the right hand side of the last
inequality in (13.7), then we only need to consider each single loop (1)–(5) in Definition 13.6, and prove that:
(♣) If M is a forest, has no top fixed end and contains only deg 3 and 4 atoms with each component
containing at least two deg 3 atoms, then the result M′ after executing the loop (1)–(5) in Definition
13.6 satisfies the same property. Moreover for this loop we have
#{33}≥λ(M)−λ(M′). (13.8)
Note that adding up (13.8) leads to the desired lower bound of #{33}, thus it suffices to prove (♣) for a given
loop. We divide the proof into 4 parts, which exactly correspond to the 4 parts of the proof in toy model II
(Proposition 11.19).
Proof part 1. We prove that each component of M′ satisfies the desired properties. This is obvious, as
the lack of top fixed ends follows from the same proof as Proposition 12.2, and the lack of deg 2 atoms and
components with only one deg 3 atom follows from the cuttings done in Definition 13.6 (5).
Proof part 2. Now we need to prove (13.8). We start by making some preparations. Note that A is an
ov-connected subset of M; by Lemma 13.4, each atom m∈ M\A that is ov-adjacent to an atom n∈A by a
pre-loop ov-segment corresponds to a unique component Xm after cutting the atoms in A as free (the loop
will in fact cut as free more atoms). Note also that m must be an ov-parent of n as A has no bottom.
Let M(3) be the set of deg 3 atoms in M (before executing the loop), and consider all ov-components
of M(3) that intersect A or are ov-adjacent to A by an ov-segment (we call them good). We know A has

cutting B as free, thus it will not be cut in steps (1)–(4) in Definition 13.6 or in cutting the deg 2 atoms in
Definition 13.6 (5).
