13.3. Pre-processing. In this subsection we introduce thepre-processing step, discussed in Section 11.7.4
(d). We start with the notion of ov-proper, which is the extension of proper in Definition 11.3.
Definition 13.8. Let M be a molecule, we say it is ov-proper, if it is a forest, contains only deg 3 and 4
atoms, and the ov-distance (Definition 8.4) between any two deg 3 atoms is at least 3.
Note that MD may have fixed ends as the result of the cuttings in Definition 12.14 (and further cuttings
of 2-connections, see Stage 1 in Section 13.5), and might not be ov-proper. The idea of pre-processing is
to turn MD into ov-proper again by cutting as free a suitable set S. Namely, given a subset of atoms in
Z∪Y ⊆ MD (which in practice will include the deg 3 atoms and weakly degenerate atoms (Definition 12.16)),

===== PAGE 160 =====
160 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
we define a function SELECT (Definition 13.10) which outputs a set S⊇ Z∪Y , such that cutting S as
free turns MD into ov-proper, see Proposition 13.11 (1). Moreover, since the UD connections are important
in the algorithm MAINUD below, we also prove a result on how cutting S as free may affect the UD
connections, see Proposition 13.11 (2).
The idea of the SELECT function is a simple greedy algorithm. Start with the set Z∪Y and its ov-
components. If cutting Z∪Y as free would create (deg 2 atom or) two deg 3 atoms with ov-distance ≤ 2,
then there must exist two ov-components A,B ⊆ Z∪Y with ov-distance≤ 4. We then add to Z∪Y the
intermediate atoms in the shortest path of ov-segments betweenA andB to bring them together, and repeat
this process until it is no longer possible. In the end we get S = SELECT(MD,Z,Y ) such that cutting S
as free makes MD ov-proper; see Definition 13.10 and Proposition 13.11 (1). As for Proposition 13.11 (2),
the proof relies on Lemma 13.9 below, which is in the same spirit as the proof of uniqueness of lowest atom
in Part 3 of the proof of Proposition 11.7 in Section 11.
Lemma 13.9. Let MUD be a UD molecule such that MD = ∅, and MU does not have C-top fixed end.
Suppose A⊆ MU is ov-connected within MU (cf. Definition 8.4) such that each atom in A has deg 3. Then
A contains a unique lowest atom n, such that (i) each atom m∈A is obtained from n by iteratively taking
ov-parents in A, and (ii) each ov-child of m∈ A is either one of the terms preceding m in the ov-parent
sequence defined in (i), or an O-atom between two consecutive terms preceding m, or an ov-child of n.
Similarly, if we assume MD̸= ∅, A is ov-connected within MU, each atom of A is C-atom, and each deg
4 atom of A has at most one ov-child in A, then conclusion (i) is still true.
Proof. Choose a lowest atom n∈A. We will prove (i), which also establishes the uniqueness of n, and prove
(ii) in the process. For any m∈ A, since A is ov-connected, there exists a sequence pj ∈ A (0≤ j≤ r)
such that ( p0, pr) = ( n, m) and pj+1 is ov-adjacent to pj; we may assume this sequence is shortest. Since
n is lowest, p1 must be ov-parent of p0 = n; since p1 has deg 3 (thus it is O-atom or has one bottom fixed
end, cf. Remark 8.7, we know that p2 must be ov-parent of p1 (otherwise p2 is ov-child of p1 along the same
maximal ov-segment containing p1 and p0, which means p2 is ov-adjacent to p0 and we can skip p1 to get a
shorter sequence), and for the same reason p3 must be ov-parent of p2, and so on. This proves (i). Then (ii)
follows from the same proof (any ov-child of pj either belongs to the ov-segment between pj and pj−1, or is
an ov-child of pj−1 so we can keep iterating). The proof of (i) in the case MD̸= ∅ is similar, where we note
that pj has at most one ov-child in A in the case when pj has deg 4, so pj+1 still cannot be ov-child of pj if
pj+1∈A and is C-atom. □
Definition 13.10 (The function SELECT). Let M be any UD molecule, such that MD has no deg 2 atoms,
and has no C-bottom fixed end. Let Z be the set of deg 3 atoms in MD, and Y be a subset of the set of deg
4 atoms in MD. Given ( MD,Z,Y ), we define a set S := SELECT(MD,Z,Y ) as follows, see Figure 61:
(1) Consider all the ov-components of Z∪Y in MD (this means we only consider the O-atoms in MD
when defining ov-connectedness in Definition 8.4), which is a finite collection of disjoint subsets of
MD.
(2) If any two of these subsets, say A and B, have the shortest ov-distance (within MD) which is at
most 4, then we choose the shortest path between an atom in A and an atom in B, which can
be divided into at most 4 ov-segments. Write this path as ( n0,··· , nq), where q≤ 4 and n0∈ A,
nq∈ B, and each ( nj, nj+1) is an ov-segment. Then replace the two sets A and B by a single set
A∪B∪{ n1,··· , nq−1}.
(3) Repeat (2) until this can no longer be done (i.e. when only one set is left, or any two sets have of
distance at least 5). Then output S := SELECT(MD,Z,Y ) as the union of all the current sets.
Proposition 13.11. Let the function SELECT be as in Definition 13.10. Then we have the followings:
(1) After cutting S := SELECT(MD,Z,Y ) from M as free, then the set MD\S in the resulting molecule
M\S is ov-proper (Definition 13.8). Note that MD\S may have both C-top and C-bottom fixed ends,
however this is compensated by the good property of being ov-proper.

===== PAGE 161 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 161
Figure 61. Top: the process of constructing the SELECT function in Definition 13.10
(for two ov-components A and B with ov-distance ≤ 4, add the intermediate atoms in
the ov-segments connecting them, until this cannot be done anymore). Bottom: proof of
Proposition 13.11 (1); if m and m′ belong to the same component A, then we get a cycle
from m→ n→···→ n′→ m′→···→ m (the path from m′ to m belongs to A).
(2) Consider all the maximal ov-segments in M that contains at least one atom in S and one atom in
MU. Then the number of these maximal ov-segments does not exceed 10(# ovcp(Z) +|Y|), where
#ovcp(Z) is the number of ov-components of Z (i.e. the set of all deg 3 atoms in MD), where the
ov-components are determined by considering MD only.
Proof. Proof of (1). At the end of the construction in Definition 13.10 (3), we get a number of disjoint
subsets, such that each one is ov-connected and the ov-distance between each two of them is at least 5, and
S is the union of all these subsets. Note also that the sub-molecule MD\S of the molecule formed by cutting
S as free from M, is equal to the molecule formed by cutting S as free from MD.
Now suppose after cutting S as free, there exist two deg 3 atoms n, n′∈ MD\S of ov-distance at most 2;
the case of a deg 2 atom in MD\S can be treated similarly. Since n, n′̸∈S, they must have deg 4 initially in
MD. As they become deg 3 after cutting S as free, we know that for each of n and n′, it must be ov-adjacent
to an atom in S. Assume n is ov-adjacent to m∈ S, and n′ is ov-adjacent to m′∈ S. If m and m′ belong
to two different subsets A and B defined above, then the ov-distance between m and m′ (and hence the
ov-distance between A and B) is at most 1 + 2 + 1 = 4, contradicting the assumption about these subsets.
Therefore, we know that m and m′ belong to the same set A defined above. But then there exists a
path µ1 : m→ n→ n′→ m′ (composed of ov-segments), and another path µ2 between m and m′, which
is composed of ov-segments connecting atoms in S (because m and m′ belong to the same ov-component
A of S). The path µ1 cannot be expressed in this way (because the atoms n, n′ separating the different
ov-segments, as well as the possible atom ov-adjacent to both n and n′, all belong to M\S), unless this whole
path belongs to a single ov-segment connecting m and m′ (meaning the atoms n, n′ etc., all belong to this
single ov-segment connecting m and m′), but in this latter case the path n→ n′ would no longer exist after
cuttingS as free (see Definition 8.3). Consequently, we know that the paths µ1 andµ2 cannot coincide, but
then they will form a cycle, which contradicts the fact that MD is a forest.
Proof of (2). In each step in Definition 13.10 (2) we add at most 3 atoms to the union of the current
subsets. Moreover, the number of such steps that can be carried out cannot exceed the initial number of
subsets (as each step reduces the number of subsets by at least 1), which is the number of ov-components of
Z∪Y , and is at most #ovcp(Z) +|Y|. This means that the number of newly added atoms in the construction
of S (starting from Z∪Y ) is at most 3(# ovcp(Z) +|Y|).
Therefore, we only need to consider the required maximal ov-segments that contains an atom in Z. In
each such maximal ov-segment, we have an atom m belonging to an ov-component W of Z, which has an
ov-parent in MU. By (the dual version of) Lemma 13.9 (ii), this ov-parent of m, which does not belong
to MD, must also be an ov-parent of the unique highest atom n of W ; therefore this maximal ov-segment

===== PAGE 162 =====
162 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
must contain n. As each ov-component W contains a unique highest atom n, and each atom belongs to at
most two maximal ov-segments, we conclude that the number of such maximal ov-segments does not exceed
2· #ovcp(Z) (as #ovcp(Z) is the number of ov-components of Z in MD), as desired. □
13.4. The MAINUD algorithm. In this section we introduce the last ingredient, which is also the central
algorithm in our proof, namely the MAINUD algorithm corresponding to toy model I plus (see Stage 6 in
Section 13.5). In Definition 13.12 below, we present the full version of this algorithm. Compared to the toy
version (Definition 11.13), the main difference (apart from standard translations into ov-segment language)
is that, the set X in Definition 11.13 is replaced by the set X1 (see Definition 13.12 below). The reason has
to do with the main dichotomy between toy models I plus and II in Section 11 (basically X1 is the set of
deg 3 atoms after cutting MD as free, which plays the same role as the X in Section 11); see the discussion
before Stages 1–6 in Section 13.5. Apart from this, Definition 13.12, Proposition 13.13 and its proof are
essentially the same as their toy versions (Definition 11.13, Proposition 11.14) in Section 11.

the set of descendants of n in MU, and fix n and Sn until the end of (6).
(3) Starting from n, choose a highest atom p inSn; if p is ov-adjacent to a deg 3 atom q∈ MD, then cut
{p, q} as free, and cut q as free from{p, q} is p has deg 4. Otherwise cut p as free.

===== PAGE 163 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 163
(4) If any two deg 3 atoms ( r, r′) in MD becomes ov-adjacent, then choose {r, r′} such that this ov-
segment contains the fewest bond, and cut {r, r′} as free. Repeat until no such instances exist.
(5) If the ov-distance of any two deg 3 atoms ( r, r′) in MD becomes 2 (within MD), say r and r′ are both
ov-adjacent to some r′′∈ MD, then cut {r, r′, r′′} as free and then cut r as free from {r, r′, r′′}. Go
to (4). Repeat until MD becomes ov-proper.
(6) When MD becomes proper, go to (3). Repeat until all atoms in Sn have been cut.
(7) When all atoms in Sn have been cut, go to (1). Repeat until all atoms in MU have been cut.
(8) When all atoms in MU have been cut, choose any deg 3 atom in MD and cut it as free. If the
ov-distance between any two deg 3 atoms becomes at most 2, go to (4) and repeat (4)–(5) until MD
becomes proper again. Then choose and cut the next deg 3 atom in MD, and so on.
Proposition 13.13. Let M be any UD molecule that satisfies (i)–(ii) in Definition 13.12. Consider the
number of UD connections e that are either (i) connected to some other UD connection via MD, or (ii)
connected to a component of MD that contains deg 3 atoms. We call such UD connections good and let
the number of them be # UD.
Let #fucp(MU) be the number of full components of MU, and let #ovcp(X1) be the number of ov-components
of the set X1 defined in Definition 13.12. Then, for the algorithm MAINUD, we have #{44} = 0, and each
{33B} molecule must have both its atoms in MD, and that
#{33A} + #{33B}≥ 1
5· (#UD− #fucp(MU)− #ovcp(X1)), and # {4}≤ #fucp(MU) + #ovcp(X1). (13.13)
Proof. We divide the proof into 4 parts, which exactly correspond to the 4 parts of the proof in toy model I
plus (Proposition 11.14); the presentation of Part 4 is slightly different, but essentially it is the same counting
argument (involving the same quantity # deg3− #comp).
Proof part 1. We prove the (MONO) property as in Proposition 12.2: at any time in the cutting
sequence, there is no C-top fixed end in ( MU\Sn)∪{ n} and no C-bottom fixed end in Sn\{n}. In fact, when
we choose n as in Definition 13.12 (2), every atom p∈Sn\{n} must have deg 4. In the process of Definition
13.12 (3)–(6), if we only consider the cutting within MU, then the desired property follows from the same
proof as in Proposition 12.2. Now suppose an arbitrary set of atoms in MD are also cut in the process, then
this will only create C-bottom fixed ends, and only at C-atoms t that are ov-adjacent to some atom in MD.
By definition we must have t∈X0∪X1, and thus t̸∈Sn\{n} by our choice, so it does not affect the desired
(MONO) property. Similarly, for any{33} molecule{p, q} cut in (3), the p must either be O-atom or p = n,
so this{33} molecule must be{33A} (as p has not C-top fixed end).
Proof part 2. We prove that each atom in MD belongs to a{3} or{33} molecule (which can be either
{33A} or{33B}). In fact, note first that initially MD is ov-proper (thus has no deg 2 atom), and does not
have full components not connected to MU by a bond (thus will not have any full component after cutting
MU as free), so the cuttings in Definition 13.12 (8) will not create any {4} molecule.
Next, note that MD initially is a forest, and will remain a forest in the whole cutting process (cutting an
atom will not create any cycle, but may cause a tree to become a disconnected forest). Now consider each
cutting step in Definition 13.12 (3) involving p∈ MU, at which time MD must be proper by the construction
in Definition 13.12 (4)–(6). This p has at most one ov-segment connecting to MD, because M
2
2conn = ∅ by
Definition 13.12 (i). If no such ov-segment exists, then cutting p does not affect MD. If one such ov-segment
exists, then the effect of the cutting step in Definition 13.12 (3) on MD, has the following two possibilities:
(a) If we cut p as free (which means all atoms in MD ov-adjacent to p have deg 4), then this step breaks
MD into connected components Xq which are in bijection with atoms q∈ MD ov-adjacent to p, and
this cutting creates only one fixed end at q when viewed within Xq, turning it from deg 4 to deg 3.
(b) If we cut {p, q} as free (which means q∈ MD is ov-adjacent to p and has deg 3), then when viewed
in MD, this step equivalent to cutting q (which has deg 3) as free in MD.
Here (a) follows from the same proof in Lemma 13.4 (i.e. q in bijection with Xq, in which only one fixed end
is created at q) as MD is a forest. In (b), the effect of cutting {p, q} as free to MD is equivalent to cutting

===== PAGE 164 =====
164 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
{q} as free, because p has no other ov-segment connecting to other atoms in MD (apart from those on the
ov-segment containing q).
Now, regarding the effects described in (a)–(b), we have the following lemma, which is extension of Lemma
11.8 in the proof of Proposition 11.7:
Lemma 13.14. Suppose MD is proper at some point of the algorithm, and becomes non-proper after either
(a) or (b). Then, MD will once more become proper after applying Definition 13.12 (4)–(5), via cuttings of
{3} and{33} molecules only, and without seeing any deg 2 atom .
Proof. Note that MD is always a forest, and our proof will crucially rely on Lemma 13.4. Consider first (a):
when viewed within each resulting component Xq, its effect is simply switching one free end at a deg 4 atom
into a fixed end, turning it into deg 3 . We call the latter step ( ∗). As for (b), right before performing it at
a deg 3 atom q, any atom in MD that is ov-adjacent to q must have deg 4 (because MD is ov-proper). By
Lemma 13.4, MD will break into finitely many components after performing (b), and the effect of (b) when
viewed within each component is again step (∗). Therefore we only need to prove Lemma 13.14 for step ( ∗).
Assume now MD becomes non-proper, with two ov-adjacent deg 3 atoms (say r and r′), after step (∗).
We may assume r′ has deg 3 before (∗), while r has deg 4 before (∗) and is turned into deg 3 after (∗). Then,
before (∗), any atom in MD ov-adjacent to r or r′ must have deg 4 , because otherwise we either have two
ov-adjacent deg 3 atoms before (∗), or have two deg 3 atoms ov-adjacent to the same deg 4 atom before (a),
contradicting properness. Therefore, by Lemma 13.4, we see that cutting the {33} molecule breaks MD into
finitely many components, and the effect of this cutting when viewed in each component is again equivalent
to step (∗). We then induct on the size of MD and apply induction hypothesis to each of these components,
to conclude the proof of Lemma 13.14.
Now suppose MD becomes non-proper, without two ov-adjacent deg 3 atoms, after (∗). In this case there
must be two deg 3 atoms, say r and r′′, that are both ov-adjacent to a deg 4 atom r′. We may assume r′ and
r′′ respectively have deg 4 and 3 before ( ∗), and r has deg 4 before (∗) and is turned into deg 3 after ( ∗).
Then, before (∗), any atom ov-adjacent to r′ or r′′ must have deg 4 for the same reason as above thanks to
properness. Moreover, before (∗), any atom adjacent to r must also have deg 4, because otherwise we would
get two ov-adjacent deg 3 atoms after (∗), contradicting our assumption. Therefore, by Lemma 13.4, we see
that cutting{r, r′, r′′} breaks MD into finitely many components, and the effect of this cutting when viewed
in each component is again equivalent to (∗). We then conclude the proof by the same induction as above.
This completes the proof of Lemma 13.14. □
Now, with Lemma 13.14, we can finish Part 2 of the proof, i.e. each atom in MD is contained in a {3}
or{33} molecule. In fact, those cut in Definition 13.12 (3) clearly belong to {33} molecule. Those cut
in Definition 13.12 (4)–(5) also belong to {3} or{33} molecules by Lemma 13.14. Finally, those cut in
Definition 13.12 (8) either belong to {3} molecule, or are cut in the process of applying Definition 13.12
(4)–(5) and thus belong to {3} or{33} molecules by Lemma 13.14.
Proof part 3. We prove that the number of deg 4 atoms n∈ MU cut in Definition 13.12 (3) is at most
#fucp(MU) + #ovcp(X1). In fact, such n must be the atom chosen in Definition 13.12 (2) (the p∈Sn\{n} in
(3) will not be cut as deg 4 because it has an ov-parent in Sn which must be cut before p). Moreover, there
are two possibilities when this n can has deg 4: either the component of MU containing n contains only deg
4 atoms, or n is the lowest remaining atom in X0∪X1 and has deg 4. The former case leads to at most
#fucp(MU) choices for n.
In the latter case, n must have deg 4 and must not have any ov-child in X0∪X1 (otherwise this ov-child
must have been cut before n as n is the lowest remaining atom inX0∪X1, so n would not have deg 4), which
means that n̸∈X0 by definition of X0 (and that M
2
2conn = ∅). Therefore n∈X1, and it must belong to one
of the initial ov-components of X1, say A. Since n has no ov-child in A, by Lemma 13.9 (i), it must be the
unique lowest atom in A (here A satisfies the assumption of Lemma 13.9, because each deg 4 atom in X1
must have an ov-child in X0∪ MD, thus has at most one ov-child in X1), which leads to at most # ovcp(X1)
choices for n due to the same number of choices of A. Putting together, this finishes Part 3 of the proof.

Proof of Proposition 12.18. Before proceeding with Stages 1–6 below, we first briefly summarize our strat-
egy, how the different toy models (I plus and II) in Section 11 occur in the proof, and how the different
algorithms (3COMPUP and MAINUD) are used. First note that, from the above assumptions, each of

MU and MD is essentially a tree (with negligible full components), with negligible number of 2-connections
in MU, and sufficiently many good UD connections (all relative to ρ′′′; same below).
In Stage 1 , we first cut as free all 2-connections (i.e. atoms in M
2
2conn) in MU and subsequently cut
any deg 2 atoms. This allows to completely get rid of 2-connections in MU at a loss of negligible {4}
molecules. The quantity # UD (i.e. the number of good UD connections) may decrease, but the decrease is
again negligible, so we still have # UD≳ρ′′′ after this stage.
Now, letZ be the set of deg 3 atoms in MD (in reality this needs slight modification to avoid components
with only one deg 3 atom, which we omit here). Consider # ovcp(Z) which is the number of ov-components of
Z; if #ovcp(Z)≳ρ′′′, then in Stage 2, we can apply 3COMPDN (the dual of 3COMPUP) to MD, which
is already sufficient by Proposition 13.7.
Therefore, we may assume # ovcp(Z)≪ ρ′′′. Now in Stage 3, we perform pre-processing to get rid of Z
as well as the set Y of weakly degenerate atoms in MD, by cutting as free the set S = SELECT(MD,Z,Y )
defined in Definition 13.10. By assumption we have # ovcp(Z)≪ρ′′′ and|Y|≪ ρ′′′, so by Proposition 13.11,
MD becomes ov-proper after cutting S as free, and the decrease of # UD is still negligible, so we still have
#UD≳ρ′′′ after this stage.
At this point we can see the dichotomy that distinguishes the cases of toy model I plus (where we apply
MAINUD) and toy model II (where we apply 3COMPUP). Let X0 and X1 be defined as in Definition
13.12; by definition, it is easy to see that if we cut MD as free, then the C-atoms in X0 are subsequently cut
as deg 2, and the deg 3 C-atoms in MU are exactly those in X1 (the O-atoms in MU will not matter in this
discussion). As such, this X1 plays the role of the set X in toy model II (see the discussion below Definition
11.15), so our dichotomy will be based on the value of #ovcp(X1), the number of ov-components ofX1 (which
is the analog of the number of components of X in Section 11).
Before proceeding, however, we need one more step: in order to apply 3COMPUP, we need that each
component of MU after cutting MD as free (and subsequently cutting deg 2 atoms) to contain at least two
deg 3 atoms. If a component A violates this, it is easy to see that before cutting MD, the set A has only one
bond connecting to M\A. This leads to Stage 4, where we get rid of these sets A by cutting them as fixed,
which allows us to apply 3COMPUP when needed. It is easy to see that we still have # UD≳ρ′′′.
Now we can consider the dichotomy discussed above: if # ovcp(X1)≳ρ′′′, then in Stage 5 we cut MD as
free and apply 3COMPUP to MU (as in toy model II), which is sufficient by Proposition 13.7. Finally, if
#ovcp(X1)≪ ρ′′′, then in Stage 6 we apply MAINUD to MUD (as in toy model I plus). Note that MD
is ov-proper and has no weak degeneracy atom thanks to Stage 3, so any{33} molecule will be good. We
then apply Proposition 13.13, and use that # UD≳ρ′′′ and #ovcp(X1)≪ρ′′′, to conclude the proof.

bond and one deg 3 atom) will lead to two fixed ends in A (or a fixed end at an O-atom), hence the result.

absence of C-top fixed ends in MU); denote the union of these components by eMU. In MUD before cutting,
the number of ov-components of X1 isµ := #ovcp(X1)≥ 10−6υ2·ρ′′′. After the cutting operations, the set of

62). Now note that, the number of ov-components of X1∪XO is no less than the number of ov-components
of X1 (which is obviously no less than µ, because these cuttings can only break ov-segments but not create
them). This is because for each deg 3 O-atom, it must have two edges (free ends or bonds) that are serial,

===== PAGE 169 =====

and one free end that is serial with one fixed end; in other words one cannot change to a different ov-segment
by going through this O-atom. Therefore, if two atoms in X1 are ov-connected within X1∪XO, they must
also be ov-connected within X1.

14.1. The leading terms. Start with the term fA,led in (14.4). Here M is a tree as ρ(M) = 0 and M is
connected (by Definition 3.20 (1b), note M has only one root particle line); moreover, it is straightforward
to verify that M is a tree and |M|p < Λℓ actually implies M∈T Λℓ. Therefore, the summation in fA,led in
(14.4) is taken with only the assumptions that M is a tree and |M|p < Λℓ. Note also that |M|p =|M| + 1
(i.e. number of collisions equals number of particles minus 1, in the absence of recollisions).
Now we introduce the following definition.
Definition 14.1. Let M be a molecule with single layer ℓ and one root particle line, which we denote by
prt. Define the highest atom in prt to be nrt, and the top end in prt to be ert, which is the unique fixed end
of M.

a bottom free end at ntr, draw a simple pair (Definition 8.2) at each intermediate O-atom in σ, and draw a
top fixed end at the lowest atom of σ (ntr is the highest atom), see Figure 64.
This operation is akin to the cutting operation in Definition 8.3, and it is easy to prove (similar to
Proposition 8.14) that we can match the edges before and after the operation (similar to Definition 8.12),
such that the value of IM|QM| is unchanged after the operation. For convenience, we still call the molecule
M after the operation, which is now regular.

of this{33A} molecule cannot both belong to the ov-segment containing etr (this follows from Proposition

one good molecule, by repeating the proof of Proposition 12.2 (4).
Now let us assume M∈T Λℓ. Let Mj be the clusters of M, with M1 containing the unique fixed end etr
(before the operation in Point 4). Then, after the operation in Point 4, there is only one deg 3 atom in
the component containing M1, and any other component of M also contains exactly one deg 3 atom oj on
an ov-segment within some Mj. For convenience we call such Mj lovely.
We then follow the strategy in Choice 2 in the proof of Proposition 12.5 (and formally allow clusters
to be empty ends as in that proof). Namely, we cut M1 as free and cut it into elementary components
using DOWN; for any lovely Mj, we also cut Mj∪{ oj} as free and cut it into elementary components
using DOWN. This then creates more lovely Mj and then we repeat this process until all atoms have been
cut. Here each Mj (or Mj∪{ oj}) contains exactly one deg 3 atom when it is cut, which is proved using
Proposition 10.4 (3), as in Choice 2 in the proof of Proposition 12.5.
As such, by Proposition 12.2 (3), we get a good component for each Mj with ρ(Mj) > 0 (i.e. with a
cycle). Since ρ =P

ρ(Mj)> 0, and hence the number of good components, is ≥ρ/Γ. This proves (14.30), which then completes
the proof of Proposition 6.1.

Proposition 7.2, the number of choices for layers Mℓ′ (ℓ′≤ℓ− 1), initial links and merging top and bottom
ends, is bounded by C|M|| logε|C∗ρ.
As for Mℓ′, by Definition 3.20 (4), we know it equals s−κ empty ends (with fixed labels) plus a graph,

of choices of Mℓ is bounded by C|M|·| logε|C∗(1+κ) (the factor| logε|C∗κ comes from identifying the κ root
particles in the graph).

15.1. The proof of Proposition 15.1. Now we prove Proposition 15.1. First we recall the theorem of
Burago-Ferleger-Kononenko [15], which establishes an upper bound for the number of collisions with fixed
number of particles in Rd; we restate it below in a more convenient form.
Proposition 15.2. Suppose M is a full molecule of C-atoms and A⊆ M is an atom set, such that for any
particle line p and any atoms n1≺ n2≺ n3 on the particle line p (in the ordering in Definition 3.5) with
n1, n3∈ A, we must have n2∈ A. Let q be the total number of bottom edges at atoms of A that are not
bonds between two atoms of A (i.e. these edges can be bottom ends of M or bonds connecting an atom in
A to an atom not in A), then we must have|A|≤ G(q), where G(q) := (2q + 1)·

Proof. We may identify particles with the corresponding particle lines, see Table 3. Let B be the set of
particle lines p that contain at least one atom (collision) in A. Each such particle line must contain a
bottom edge at an atom of A that is not a bond between two atoms of A, which implies that|B|≤ q.
For each particlep∈B, consider the collision n−

We also shrink I a little bit to eI such that I\eI contains no collision (other than the ones at the endpoint
of I, which do not belong to A). It is then easy to see that (i) any collision in A that happens within time
interval eI must be between two particles in B′, and (ii) any collision that happens within time interval eI
and involves an atom in B′ must belong to A. In particular, within this time interval eI, no particles in B′
collides with any other particle, so the restriction of the full dynamics to these particles is equal to the partial
duynamics with only atoms in B′. Let λI be the number of collisions in A that happen within time interval
eI, then the collision dynamics for the |B′| particles in B′ within interval eI produces at least λI collisions.

Before getting to the proof of Proposition 15.1, we first briefly explain the strategy.
Cutting deg 3 and deg 2 atoms. A naive idea to exploit the recollisions is to keep cutting deg 3 atoms
(the first being deg 4), until hitting a recollision and getting a {33} molecule. The main difficulty here, also
the main difficulty in general, is that we may be forced to cut long sequences of deg 2 atoms without any
gain. Let the set of deg 3 atoms being cut be A, then the set of subsequent deg 2 atoms being cut is obtained
by starting from A and iteratively taking atoms with 2 bonds with previous atoms, which is just X(A) in
the notion of Definition 15.3.
We note that each atom in A is cut as deg 3, so it has only one bond connecting to previously cut atoms;
in particular keep adding atoms in A will not create any new cycle, and we can think of these sets A as close
to being trees (i.e. with ρ(A) controlled). On the other hand, adding each atom in X(A) will create one
new cycle and increase the ρ value by 1. Finally, in choosing the set A we can optimize this process by each
time cutting the highest (or lowest) deg 3 atom as in UP, which will ensure this set A is transversal18 as in
Definition 15.3.
17The extra factor
 q
2

is due to some extra considerations needed for extended dynamics; see Appendix A.
18The intuition is as follows. Suppose we apply UP until getting the first deg 2 atom (or {33} molecule), and let the set
of deg 3 atoms cut be A. Then A basically consists of the union of sets of Sn for some n (which has no bottom in the sense of
Lemma 11.16), and a subset of the next Sn′ which will have no top (as we keep cutting the highest atom in Sn′). Note that

The sets Aj and reduction to toy models. Now, suppose we keep cutting sequences of deg 3 and deg
2 atoms as above, then we get sets Aj such thatAj+1⊇Aj∪X(Aj). Note that forst each sequence of deg 3
and the next sequence of deg 2 atoms, we get a {33} molecule at the transition of the two sequences; thus,

by applying the BFK theorem , we know that the number of bonds between X+(A) and A must also be
sufficiently larger than ρ(A) (Proposition 15.2).
Effectively, we can now think A as almost a tree, compare it to MD in the 2-layer model, and compare
X(A) (or more precisely X+(A) where we only take parents in the construction of X(A)) to the MU in the
2-layer model (this set is not clse to a tree, but this will not be used in the algorithms below). The role
of UD connections is now played by the bonds between X+(A) and A. We then repeat the same proof for
(part of) Proposition 12.18: if there are many 2-connections (corresponding to a subset X+
0 (A)⊆X+(A))
then apply 2CONNDN as in toy model III; if not, then we cut these 2-connections as in Stage 1, then
perform pre-processing as in Stege 3, then get rid of weak degeneracies and finally apply MAINUD as in
toy model I plus.
We now define the transversal subsets A of M, and prove some properties.
Definition 15.3. Let M be a full molecule of C-atoms, and A⊆ M is an atom set. We say A is transversal,
if we can decompose M\A into two disjoint subsets A+ and A−, such that no atom in A− is parent of any
atom in A∪A+, and no atom in A is parent of any atom in A+.
For any transversal set A, define the set X0(A) such that, an atom n∈ X0(A) if and only if n̸∈ A and
n has two bonds connected to two atoms in A. Inductively define Xq(A) such that, n∈Xq(A) if and only
if n̸∈ A, and either n∈ Xq−1(A), or n has two bonds connected to two atoms in Xq−1(A)∪A. Define
X(A) =∪q≥0Xq(A), see Figure 65.
Figure 65. Example of sets X(A) and X0(A) for a specific connected transversal set A.
Note that if we cutA as free, then those atoms inX0(A) (blue) will immediately become deg
2; if we keep cutting deg 2 atoms, then those atoms in X(A) (red) will eventually become
deg 2 and be cut.
Proposition 15.4. Let A be a connected transversal subset and M is a connected molecule.
(1) We can always choose (A+,A−) in Definition 15.3 such that each component of A+ has at least one
bond connected to A.
(2) Define the sets X±
q (A) in the same way asXq(A), but with the sentence “n has two bonds connected
to two atoms in Xq−1(A)∪A” replaced by “ n has two children (or two parents) in X±
q−1(A)∪A”.
Then we have X±
q (A) =Xq(A)∩A±. Define X±(A) =∪q≥0X±
q (A), then X±(A) =X(A)∩A±.
no-bottom (resp. no-top) subsets are transversal sets where A− = ∅ (resp. A+ = ∅), so the general notion of transversality
then arises naturally as combination of both.

(3) The set X(A)∪A is also connected and transversal. Moreover any atom in M\(X(A)∪A) has at
most one bond with atoms in X(A)∪A, so X0(X(A)∪A) = ∅.
(4) Recall ρ(A) defined in Definition 3.13. Then there exists a connected transversal set B⊇ A, such
that ρ(B) =ρ(A), and either B = M or X0(B)̸= ∅.
Proof. Proof of (1). SupposeA+ has a componentU that is not connected toA by a bond, then we replace
A+ byA+\U and A− byA−∪U, which still satisfies the requirements for ( A+,A−) in Definition 15.3, but
the number of components of A+ decreases by 1. Repeat until each component of A+ is connected to A by
a bond (or A+ becomes empty).
Proof of (2). First X±
q (A)⊆Xq(A)∩A±, which easily follows from definition and induction. To prove
the opposite direction, first if an atom n∈ X0(A) belongs to A±, then n must have two children (or two
parents) in A so n∈ X±
0 (A). We then proceed by induction: suppose X±
q−1(A) = Xq−1(A)∩A±, now
consider n∈ (Xq(A)∩A+)\Xq−1(A) which has two adjacent atoms n1 and n2 inXq−1(A)∪A. If n1 belongs
to A−, then n must be child of n1 (it is easy to see that both parents of n1∈A− must be in A∪A−) and
thus n∈A− which is impossible. This means n1∈A+∪A and same for n2. Since n1,2∈Xq−1(A)∪A, we
know by induction hypothesis that n1,2∈X+
q−1(A)∪A, thus n∈Xq(A) by definition. Same is true for A−.
Proof of (3). Connectedness is obvious by definition. To prove transversality, we simply decompose
M\(A∪X(A)) =B+∪B−, where B =A+\X+(A) and B− =A−\X−(A). This then satisfies the require-
ments (using the fact that any child (or parent) of any atom n∈X±(A) must belong to X±(A)∪A). The
second statement follows from the definition of X(A) in Definition 15.3.
Proof of (4). SupposeA is connected and transversal; we may assumeA̸= M andX0(A) = ∅ (otherwise
choose B = A). Since M is connected, there must exist an atom n∈ M\A that is either a parent or child
of an atom in A. We may assume it is a parent, and then choose a lowest atom n∈ M\A among these
parents. Since X0(A) = ∅, we know that n has only one bond with atoms in A; let A1 =A∪{ n}, then A1
is connected and ρ(A1) =ρ(A), see Figure 66.
Figure 66. Proof of Proposition 15.4 (4): n is a lowest parent of atom in A, which is
natural in terms of the UP or DOWN algorithm; C is the set of descendants of n in A+.

Now we know that A1 is also connected transversal; by replacing A by A1 and repeating the above
discussion, we eventually will reach some B such that either B = M or X0(B)̸= ∅, as desired. □
Now we define the algorithm TRANSDN, which is analogous to the 2CONNDN algorithm and is
applicable in the case when |X+
0 (A)| (corresponding to the 2-connections in toy model III) is large.

===== PAGE 182 =====

Definition 15.5 (Algorithm TRANSDN). Suppose M is connected full molecule and A is a connected
transversal subset. We may choose (A+,A−) as in Proposition 15.4 (1). Define the following algorithm:
(1) If A contains any deg 2 atom n, then cut it as free, and repeat until there is no deg 2 atom left.
(2) Choose a highest atom n in the set of all deg 3 atoms inA (or a highest atom inA, ifA only contains
deg 4 atoms). Let Zn be the set of ancestors of n in A.
(3) Starting from n, choose a lowest atom m inZn that has not been cut. If m has deg 3, and is adjacent
to an atom p∈X+
0 (A) that also has deg 3, then cut {m, p} as free; otherwise cut m as free. Repeat
until all atoms in Zn have been cut. Then go to (1).
(4) Repeat (1)–(3) until all atoms in A have been cut. Then cut the remaining part ofA+ as free and cut
it into elementary components using UP, then cut A− into elementary components using DOWN.
We also define the dual algorithm TRANSUP by reversing the notions of parent/child etc. Note that we
replace A+ and X+
0 (A) by A− and X−
0 (A), and also replace A+ byA− in Proposition 15.4 (1).
Proposition 15.6. For the algorithmTRANSDN (and same forTRANSUP), we have #{33B} = #{44} =
0 and #{4} = 1, and #{33A}≥|X+
0 (A)|/2−ρ(A)− 1.
Proof. It is clear, in the same way as in the proof of Proposition 12.2, that during the process, there is
no bottom fixed end in ( A\Zn)∪{ n} or in A−, and there is no top fixed end in Zn\{n} or in A+, so
#{33B} = #{44} = 0. Moreover #{4} = 1 because no component of the remaining part of A+ after Definition
15.5 (3) can be full when it is first cut in Definition 15.5 (4) (thanks to Proposition 15.4 (1)), and no
component of A− can be full when it is first cut in Definition 15.5 (4) (thanks to M being connected).
To prove the lower bound for # {33A}, note that only one atom in A belongs to a {4} molecule (as A
is connected). Let the number of atoms in A that belongs to a {2} molecule be q, then by considering
the increment of the same quantity σ := # bo/fr− 3|M| and applying the same arguments in the proof of
Proposition 12.2 (but restrected to A), and noticing that initially all atoms in A have deg 4, we deduce that
#{2} + #{33}− #{4} =−σinit = #bond−|A| =ρ(A)− 1,
and #{4} = 1, hence q≤ ρ(A). Then, for each n∈ X+
0 (A), which is adjacent to two atoms n1, n2∈ A by
two bonds, assume say n2 is cut after n1. The total number of such n2 is at least|X+
0 (A)|/2 as each n2 can
be obtained from at most two n. For each such n2, the corresponding n must have deg 3 when it is cut, so
by Definition 15.5 (3), it must belong to either {4}-, or{2}-, or{33A} molecule. Using the upper bound for
the number of{4} and{2} molecules, this completes the proof. □
Next we define the SELECT2 function, which is an extension of the SELECT function in the case when
A may contain cycles (but with ρ(A) bounded). The proof follows similar ideas, except for the extra step
(3), which is needed because of the possible cycle in A.
Definition 15.7 (The function SELECT2). LetA be a connected molecule with only C-atoms, no bottom
fixed end, and no deg 2 atoms. Let Z be the set of deg 3 atoms in A, and let Y be a subset of atoms
in A such that A becomes a forest after removing the atoms in Y . We define the function SELECT2 =
SELECT2(A,Z,Y ) as follows, see Figure 67.
(1) Consider all the components of Z∪Y in A, which is a finite collection of disjoint subsets of A.
(2) Since A only has C-atoms, the shortest ov-distance between sets (Definition 8.4) coincides with the
standard definition of distance on a graph. If any two of the subsets in (1), say U and V , have the
shortest distance (within A) which is at most 4, then we choose one shortest path between an atom
in U and an atom in V , and let the atoms on this path be nj. Then replace the two sets U and V
by a single set which is U∪V∪{ nj}.
(3) Repeat (2) until this can no longer be done. Next, if a single subset U contains two atoms (which
may be the same) that are connected by a path of length at most 4 with none of the intermediate
atoms belonging to U or any other subset, then add the atoms on this path to U. If this causes the
shortest distance between two subsets to be ≤ 4, then proceed to (2) and repeat it as above.

===== PAGE 183 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 183
(4) When no scenario in (2) or (3) occurs, we output S := SELECT2(A,Z,Y ) as the union of all the
current sets.
Figure 67. The SELECT2 function, which is basically the same as theSELECT function
(Definition 13.10, Figure 61). The left arrow: Definition 15.7 (2 (same as Definition 13.10),
where we add new atoms to connect two components. The right arrow: Definition 15.7 (3),
where we add the new atoms that form cycles.
Proposition 15.8. The molecule A becomes a proper forest after cutting S as free, and we also have
|S|≤ 10(|Y| +|Z| +ρ(A)).
Proof. The proof is essentially the same as Proposition 13.11. First A becomes a forest after cutting S as
free, because Y ⊆S. Also A becomes proper after cutting S as free, due to the same reason in the proof of
Proposition 13.11; in the language of that proof, the absence of the scenario in Definition 15.7 (2) prevents
the case when m and m′ belong to two different components U and V , and the absence of the scenario in
Definition 15.7 (3) prevents the case when m and m′ belong to the same components U.
As for the upper bound for |S|, simply note that each single step in Definition 15.7 (2)–(3) adds at most
3 atoms to S. Moreover each single step in Definition 15.7 (2) decreases the number of subsets by 1 (as
it connectes U and V together) and does not decrease ρ(S) (as ρ(S) is the number of independent cycles,
so adding more atoms cannot decrease ρ(S)). Each single step in Definition 15.7 (3) does not change the
number of subsets, but adds one more cycle to S; in particular adding this cycle increases the value of ρ(S)
by at least 1. Putting together, we know that the value of
γ := (number of sets in S)−ρ(S)
decreases by at least 1 in each step Definition 15.7 (2)–(3). Since we always haveρ(S)≤ρ(A) becauseS⊆A
(again it is easy to see that adding one atom or arbitrarily many atoms does not decrease the ρ value), we
know that the initial value of γ is≤| Y| +|Z| and the final value of γ is≥− ρ(A), which means that the
total number of steps in Definition 15.7 (3) is bounded by |Y| +|Z| +ρ(A), hence the result. □
Now we can define the algorithm MAINTRUP, which is the main algorithm in this section. It has two
options, Option 1, which is applied only in the (simple) weakly degenerate case, and essentially trivially
cuts most atoms in A as{3} molecules, so one can gain from extra restrictions from weak degeneracies. In
Option 2 , which is the main part, we basically combine Stage 1 (cutting 2-connections in X+
0 (A)) and
Stage 3 (pre-processing) in the proof of Proposition 12.18 at the end of Section 13, and the MAINUD
algorithm in toy model I plus, but wih the roles of MU and MD played byX+(A) and X respectively.
Definition 15.9 (Algorithm MAINTRUP). Suppose M is a connected full molecule and A is a connected
transversal subset. We may choose (A+,A−) as in Proposition 15.4 (1). Let the number of bonds connecting
an atom in X+(A) to an atom in A be #+
conn. It is easy to prove that there exists a set Y0⊆A of at most
ρ(A) atoms, such that A becomes a forest after cutting atoms in Y0 as free. Moreover, by splitting into at
mostC|A|+ρ(A) sub-cases, we can identify a set of weakly degenerate atoms and atom pairs in A in the sense
of Proposition 12.17; let Y1⊆A be the atoms involved in these weak degeneracies. We define the following
algorithm, which contains two Options that we can choose at the beginning.
In Option 1 we do the followings:

===== PAGE 184 =====
184 YU DENG, ZAHER HANI, AND XIAO MA
(1) Cut A as free, then cut it into elementary components using UP.
(2) Then cut A+ as free and cut it into elementary components using UP, then cut A− into elementary
components using DOWN.
In Option 2 we do the followings:
(1) Cut all atoms in X+
0 (A) as free. If any atom in A becomes deg 2, also cut it as free until A has no
deg 2 atom.
(2) If A remains connected after the above step, let Z be the set of deg 3 atoms in A, and Y be those
atoms inY0∪Y1 that have not been cut. Define S = SELECT2(A,Z,Y ) as in Definition 15.7, then
cut S as free and cut it into elementary components using DOWN. If A is not connected, apply
this step to each connected component of A.
(3) If not all atoms in X+(A) have been cut, then choose a lowest atom n in X+(A) that has not been
cut. If n is adjacent to an atom p∈A that has deg 3, then cut{n, p} as free; otherwise cut n as free.
If A becomes non-proper (Definition 11.3) then repeat the steps in Definition 13.12 (4)–(5) until A
becomes proper again.
(4) Repeat (3) until all atoms in X+(A) have been cut. Then repeat the step in Definition 13.12 (8)
until all atoms in A have been cut.
(5) Finally, cut (the remaining parts of) A+ as free and cut it into elementary components using UP,
and then cut A− into elementary components using DOWN.
We define the dual algorithm MAINTRDN in the same way (so # +
conn is replaced by #−
conn etc.).

Now, we can finally prove Proposition 15.1.
Proof of Proposition 15.1. First assume M contains at least one strongly degenerate primitive pair ( m, n),
then we can cut{m, n} as free and cut the rest of M into elementary components using either UP or DOWN
(cf. proof of Proposition 12.5, Choice 1). This creates one good {44} molecule and no{4} molecule (as M
is connected), so # good≥ 1 and # bad = 0, which implies (15.1).
Now assume M contains no strongly degenerate primitive pair, then we can show (by splitting into two
sub-cases) that each{33A} molecule is either good or can be cut into one {2} and one good{3}-atom. The
proof is similar to Lemma 12.6; suppose this {33A} molecule is{n, n′} where n is parent of n′, then we may
assume this is strongly degenerate and |tn−tn′|≤ ευ otherwise this is already good by Definition 9.4. Now
our assumption implies that{n, n′} must be non-primitive; in the absence of C-atoms, this implies that there
exists another chain of atoms going from n to n′ by iteratively taking children. If any atom q in this chain is
cut before{n, n′}, then the inequality tn≥tq≥t′
n allows us to argue as in the proof of Lemma 12.6 to get a
good molecule; if not, this means that at the time {n, n′} is cut, n still has two children (i.e. n′ and a child
on the chain), and n′ still has two parents (i.e. n and a parent on the chain), which means that n must have
a top fixed end and n′ must have a bottom fixed end, but this contradicts that {n, n′} is{33A} molecule.
Therefore, from now on, we will treat {33A} molecules as good. We start by choosing a connected
transversal subsetA1 of M which is a tree (i.e. ρ(A1) = 0) and either A1 = M orX0(A1)̸= ∅; the existence
of such A1 follows in the same way as in Proposition 15.4 (4) starting from a single atom with no children.
If A1̸= M, let X1 =X(A1), then A1∪X1 is connected transversal by Proposition 15.4 (3), so we can find
A2⊇A1∪X1 starting from A1∪X1, using Proposition 15.4 (4). If A2̸= M, then let X2 =X(A2) and find
A3 by Proposition 15.4 (4), then define X3 =X(A3) and so on, see Figure 68.

In this way, it is clear that each mj must belong to a{33} molecule, which also has to be{33A} molecule
because mj will have two top (or two bottom) free ends when it is cut, assuming mj∈ X+
0 (Aj) (or mj∈
X−
0 (Aj)). This produces at least D many{33A} molecules (which are treated as good), while # {4} = 1,
which implies (15.1).
(2) Suppose Aj = M for some j <D, then ρ(Aj)≥ Γ for this j. Therefore we may choose the smallest j
such that ρ(Aj+1)≥Kj+1. Let A =Aj, then we have
ρ(A)<K j, K j+1≤ρ(Aj+1) =ρ(A∪X(A))≤ρ(A) + 10|X(A)|,
so|X(A)|≥ (20)−1·Kj+1. We may assume|X+(A)|≥ (40)−1·Kj+1; consider the setX+(A), which obviously
satisfies the assumptions in Proposition 15.2, so by Proposition 15.2 we conclude that # +
conn > (60d)60d·Kj
in Definition 15.9. We then
• Run algorithm TRANSUP and apply Proposition 15.6 if |X+
0 (A)|≥ (30d)30d·Kj;
• Run algorithm MAINTRUP, Option 1 and apply Proposition 15.10 if |Y1|≥ (30d)30d·Kj;
• Run algorithm MAINTRUP, Option 2 and apply Proposition 15.10, if |X+
0 (A)|≤ (30d)30d·Kj
and|Y1|≤ (30d)30d·Kj.
In any case it is easy to verify that (15.1) is true, so the proof is complete. □
Appendix A. Some auxiliary results
In this appendix, we first prove some basic properties of the extended (E-) and truncated (T- or (Λ , Γ)-)
dynamics, in Proposition 4.5.
Proof of Proposition 4.5. We need to prove the following statements (1)–(5). Note that the flow property
forSE

===== PAGE 187 =====
LONG TIME DERIVATION OF THE BOLTZMANN EQUATION FROM HARD SPHERE DYNAMICS 187
(1) For E- and T-dynamics, the initial configurations in which two collisions happen at the same time,
or an infinite number of collisions happen in a finite time interval, or some collision happens at a
fixed time, form a Lebesgue zero set.
(2) Apart from Lebesgue zero sets, the mapping HE
N(t) defined by the E-dynamics (Definition 4.1,
Proposition 4.5 (2)) satisfies the flow property in (4.3).
(3) If the initial configuration z0∈D N, then the E-dynamics and the O-dynamics coincide.
(4) In the (Λ, Γ)-dynamics, the size and recollision number of any cluster Mj of the topological reduction
M always satisfy that|Mj|≤ Λ and ρ(Mj)≤ Γ.
(5) The equality (4.4) holds for both E- and T-dynamics.
(6) For O-, E- and T-dynamics, the total number of collisions has an absolute upper bound that depends
only on d and N.
Proof of (1). This follows from the same proof as in [1].
Proof of (2)–(3). Notice that by Definition 4.1, the motion of the particles starting from any time t
depends only on the configuration of the system at time t but not on the collision history before time t. The
flow property in (2) then follows. Moreover, by Definition 4.1, we know that if the distance between any two
particles i and j ever becomes≥ε along the E-dynamics, then their distance will remain ≥ε for the rest of
time; therefore, if the distance between any two particles is >ε initially, then all these distances will remain
≥ε forever, so the E-dynamics coincide with the O-dynamics, which proves (3).
Proof of (4). This follows from the definition of (Λ , Γ)-truncated dynamics (Definition 4.3): it is easy
to see that any collision that is allowed cannot cause |Mj| > Λ or ρ(Mj) > Γ, due to the definition of the
condition Pro in (4.2).
Proof of (5). We prove it forSE
N, and the SΛ,Γ

ordering them, we can divide R2dN into a countable union of disjoint open sets ( Oα)α∈N, where on each Oα

mappingHE
